# ADVERTISEMENT. <note>original: "ADVERTISEMENT." In the 18th century, this term was commonly used for a preface, introduction, or a formal notice to the reader.</note>

THE prevailing custom of entertaining the public with pieces of wit and humor *under the form of History, Lives, and Adventures, first moved the author to review the fables of antiquity: for he sees no reason why they should be neglected; since, by our methods of education, they are in a manner the fountain of science <note>original: "Science." Here, the word refers to the broader definition of "knowledge" or "learning" rather than just the physical sciences.</note>, the source of the first knowledge that we absorb. This indeed may be the chief cause of their being neglected: as they were the entertainment of our childhood, we think it right to throw them aside, like other toys, when we have grown to maturity. And yet it must be admitted that without a thorough knowledge and remembrance of them, we can hardly understand—at least not read with pleasure—those useful and invaluable productions of the ancients. The legendary <note>original: "fabulous." In this context, it means "of or belonging to fables or myths."</note> heroes of antiquity afford us as much instruction as modern ones; and*

<summary>In this preface, the author argues that classical mythology should not be discarded as childish, as it forms the foundation of education and is essential for understanding the great works of ancient literature.</summary>
<keywords>mythology, classical education, fables, antiquity, ancients, instruction</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Advertisement" or preface from the previous page, following the catchword "have."</meta>

[ ii ]

have this further advantage, that after so many ages they have not yet grown old or obsolete, but still flourish in the records of fame. They are likely to live on when most of the heroes of modern fiction <note>original: "fable"</note> will be extinct and forgotten.

The study of antiquities—so much criticized <note>original: "decried"</note> of late by Englishmen—is, in other words, a more accurate research into ancient history. The learned people of other countries believe this study cannot be carried to too great a height, because nothing in literature can be more useful. In traveling through the boundless region of knowledge <note>original: "science." In the 18th century, "science" referred to the broader scope of learning or any systematic body of knowledge, not just the physical sciences.</note>, we can never expect to come to our journey's end; rather, the more we know, the more we ought to realize remains still unknown to us. 

However, it is our misfortune that too many among us only learn enough to make them conceited with what they know; they then, with an air of importance, dismiss as useless whatever they cannot relish, imitate, or even understand. This is always a sure mark of a small mind, swelling itself within its own narrow sphere, and is well described by the poet:

<summary>The author defends the study of antiquities as a vital part of ancient history and criticizes those who dismiss it out of intellectual conceit or a lack of understanding.</summary>
<keywords>antiquities, ancient history, education, scholarship, intellectual humility, classical studies</keywords>

## That casting weight pride adds to emptiness.
<note>original: "casting weight." This refers to a weight added to one side of a scale to tip the balance; here it suggests that pride heavily tips the scales of an empty mind.</note>

*Sir William Temple calls it* **Sufficiency** <note>original: "Sufficiency." In the late 17th century, this term referred to a self-satisfied conceit or the arrogant belief that one's own knowledge is entirely adequate.</note>*, the worst combination <note>original: "composition"</note> of the pride and ignorance of mankind. If any good effect can come from this evil, it must be that of putting an author on his guard; so that when he falls into the hands of these merciless enemies, they may have less of a grip to pull him to pieces. By this means, learning may for some time hold its ground against such superficial opponents. If the nation, through a natural decay, is sinking into a state of ignorance—as some are inclined to think—this may be one symptom of the disease <note>original: "distemper"</note>, but it can never contribute to its cure, any more than every quack doctor <note>original: "quack pretender to physick"</note> can restore a body <note>original: "constitution"</note> broken by age and infirmities.*

*For the better part of a century, we have had periodic accounts of the* **State of Literature** *in our island; and judgment has been passed on books and authors in* **Abstracts**, **Compendiums**, **Abridgments**, *etc., which were originally written with decency and good manners. These set forth the merits of each work <note>original: "performance"</note> and were sometimes written, perhaps, with too much*

*His former Essay was designed to show <note>original: "shew"</note> that the first gods of the Greeks <note>original: "Grecians"</note>, Uranus and Saturn, were not natives of their country, as was afterwards claimed, but came from the North. He now presents the public with two more figures of as great fame as the others; who, as he imagines, came from the same country, but distinguished themselves at first in India, long before their names were known to Greece or Egypt.*

*The author has always paid great regard to the judgment of the learned, and will ever abide by their decision. He is aware <note>original: "sensible"</note> that ancient prejudices are not easily eradicated, and therefore he did not expect a general or immediate agreement with his opinion. But he admits <note>original: "owns"</note> that he expected something more than general objections—especially those uttered in whispers and in the dark. Until the objectors shall deign <note>original: "vouchsafe"</note> to appear a little more open and explicit, he humbly hopes, in his turn, to be indulged in the common bias <note>original: "prejudice"</note> of an author in favor of his own work <note>original: "performance"</note>. If it should still be condemned, he hopes the fault may not be attributed <note>original: "imputed"</note> so much to the work itself—*

The following essay consists, for the most part, of ideas <note>original: "hints"</note> that arose from those *Enquiries into the Ancient <note>original: "Primitive"</note> State of Europe*; and if they now and then differ from them, they must be considered as "second thoughts." The author had no intention of sending it to the press, having suffered too much already on that account. But he drew it up some years ago, in its current form, for his own amusement and the entertainment of a few friends, who were pleased to think the previous work <note>original: "former piece"</note> was incomplete <note>original: "defective"</note> without this necessary supplement. 

If, therefore, the reader gains <note>original: "reaps"</note> any benefit from reading <note>original: "the perusal"</note> it, his thanks are due to the friendly persuasion <note>original: "kind compulsion"</note> and generous contributions of those friends; for it would never have seen the light of day if not for their intervention <note>original: "mediation"</note>. It was rejected by the bookseller, was beneath the patronage of the nobility <note>original: "the great"</note>, and was above seeking a charitable subscription <note>original: "eleemosynary subscription"; a method of funding books through small donations</note>.

# THE HISTORY AND CHRONOLOGY OF THE FABULOUS AGES.

It is an observation of Plutarch’s (1) that to make mythology useful, *we must cleanse it of its impurities <note>original: "dross"; worthless matter or the scum on top of molten metal, used here metaphorically for irrational elements of myths</note> and bring it to sense and reason before it can take on the appearance of history.*

The fabulous age <note>original: "fabulous age"; the period of ancient history dominated by myths and legends rather than documented facts</note> is of such great extent that we hardly know where it begins or where it ends. No general rule can be established that will serve to explain every part and purpose of it. Once the habit of storytelling <note>original: "humour of romancing"; the cultural inclination toward inventing or embellishing fictional tales</note> prevailed, no bounds could be set to it. In time, the gullibility of nations became so universal that people

---

(1) "May it be our fortune, then, to purify the mythological element through reason, making it submit and take on the appearance of history." <note>original Greek: "Εἴη μὲν οὖν ἡμῖν ἐκκαθαιρόμενον λόγῳ τὸ Μυθῶδες ὑπακοῦσαι, καὶ λαβεῖν ἱστορίας ὄψιν." from Plutarch's *Life of Theseus*.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the author's approach to studying the "fabulous age" of mythology, citing Plutarch's belief that myths must be refined by reason to be historically useful.</summary>
<keywords>Plutarch, mythology, fabulous age, chronology, historiography, reason vs. myth</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion on the susceptibility of ancient people to religious absurdities and the branding of reformers as atheists. It transitions into an investigation of the origins of heathen gods and the nature of fables.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>The History and Chronology</header>
<sig>A</sig>

were everywhere prepared to believe the grossest absurdities in religion; every attempt to open their eyes was branded with the hateful name of atheism. There was certainly a time when the heathen gods first began to be worshiped, but this time has never yet been clearly stated. However, to enter upon the subject systematically, we ought to inquire who the first gods were and for what reasons they were worshiped. Although we may succeed in this inquiry, we will find their actions so intermixed with those who came after them and went by the same names that extracting their true history from such an abyss of fables may seem a task little inferior to the labors of a hero <note>likely referring to the legendary "Labors of Hercules"</note>.

<margin>The progress of fable.</margin>
Fable may be called a picture of truth, drawn with the goal of raising our attention and admiration. It appears in a form somewhat more than human, with a demeanor <note>original: "mein"; a person's appearance or manner</note> approaching divinity, and in a dress as splendid as the imagination can frame. But if it can be stripped of these ornaments, the truth itself would appear naked and undisguised. The first sort of fables—parables or apologues <note>original: "apologues"; moral fables, especially those with animals as characters, such as Aesop's</note> in which birds, beasts, or trees were introduced as speakers and actors—conveyed moral instructions for the conduct of human life. In these cases, it was easy to perceive that the characters brought onto the scene were imaginary; the solution, therefore, was obvious, and the true design was quickly seen through the fiction. To this extent, fable was both lawful and useful. But when it grew so unchecked <note>original: "luxuriant"; growing excessively or overgrown</note> that it interfered with the records of past times and events, it became very harmful to history and cast almost as great a darkness over it as time itself could have done. The truth sank deeper

<summary>The author argues that while moral fables like parables are useful, the over-embellishment of historical events with mythological "ornaments" has obscured the true history of the first worshiped gods.</summary>
<keywords>heathen gods, atheism, fables, parables, apologues, historiography, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion on how the historical truth behind ancient myths became obscured over time, making it difficult to separate real people and events from legendary fables.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>Of the Fabulous Ages.</header>
<sig>A 2</sig>

...further into the fable, and could not be brought to light without much greater difficulty and insight. Real facts, dates, and people were all mixed together in one diverse <note>original: "heterogeneous"</note> mass; it required almost a divine power to organize them into any manageable order.

<margin>Fables explained through allegory.</margin>
This led seekers of truth to use different methods of investigation. Some of the ancients (1) took the shortcut of cutting the knot <note>a reference to the "Gordian Knot," an impossible problem solved by a single bold stroke</note> rather than untying it, by dismissing everything as fiction. They argued that things were not to be understood literally; instead, by boldly exploring the minds of the ancient poets, they found a hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> meaning in almost everything they wrote. They reduced gods and heroes to the active and passive qualities of the mind, or to simple physical elements—earth, air, water, and fire—viewing the wars of the gods as the clashing of these elements. In this way, they created a beautiful picture based on their own imaginations; however, they only layered new fables onto the old ones, without ever searching for the historical truth hidden at the bottom.

---

(1) Among these was Metrodorus of Lampsacus, who, in his treatise on Homer, treated not only the gods and goddesses but also the heroes of the poem as allegorical figures. "Even Metrodorus of Lampsacus, in his book about Homer, argues very foolishly by turning everything into allegory. He claims that Hera, Athena, and Zeus are not what those who established their temples and sacred sites believe them to be, but are instead natural forces and arrangements of the elements. He also says that Hector, Achilles, Agamemnon, and all the Greeks and foreigners alike, including Helen and Paris, are of this same nature and were only introduced for the sake of the story's structure—none of these people ever actually existed." <note>original Greek: Καὶ Μητρόδωρος δὲ ὁ Λαμψακηνὸς, ἐν τῷ περὶ τοῦ Ὁμήρου λίαν εὐήθως διείλεκται, πάντα εἰς ἀλληγορίαν μετάγων. οὔτε γὰρ Ἥραν, οὔτε Ἀθηνᾶν, οὔτε Δία τοῦτ᾽ εἶναί φησιν, ὅπερ οἱ τοὺς περιβόλους αὐτοῖς καὶ τεμένη καθιδρύσαντες νομίζουσι· φύσεως δὲ ὑποστάσεις, καὶ στοιχείων διακοσμήσεις. Καὶ τὸν Ἕκτορα δὲ, καὶ τὸν Ἀχιλλέα δηλαδὴ, καὶ τὸν Ἀγαμέμνονα, καὶ πάντας ἁπαξαπλῶς Ἕλληνάς τε καὶ Βαρβάρους, σὺν τῇ Ἑλένῃ, καὶ τῷ Πάριδι τῆς αὐτῆς φύσεως ὑπάρχοντας, χάριν οἰκονομίας ἐρεῖ τε παρεισῆχθαι, οὐδενὸς ὄντος προειρημένων ἀνθρώπων.</note> Tatian, *Address to the Greeks* §. 37.

The

<summary>This page explores how ancient scholars attempted to interpret confusing myths by turning them into allegories of nature or the human mind, often ignoring potential historical realities. It highlights the work of Metrodorus of Lampsacus, who argued that Homeric heroes and gods were merely symbolic representations rather than real individuals.</summary>

<header>The History and Chronology</header>
<meta>catchword: have</meta>

<margin>By facts in Scripture.</margin>
Modern scholars <note>original: "The moderns"</note> have adopted what appears to be a more rational method: by admitting that the foundation of these stories is true history, but rejecting the mythical <note>original: "fabulous"</note> additions. These scholars make the sacred writings the groundwork of Greek mythology and attempt to explain the fables using parallel stories from the Bible. It cannot be denied that there is a very striking resemblance between many Greek fables and events recorded in Scripture. However, we should not imagine that the people mentioned in the fables are the same individuals found in the sacred books; doing so would confuse all history, geography, and chronology. 

We may reasonably suppose that in the early, unsettled ages of the world, the memory of remarkable events was carried by colonies to many remote countries. Their descendants later adopted these facts as their own. We must judge, based on what remains of true history, how closely the likeness between the copy and the original has been preserved. I can readily allow that the Deluges of Ogyges and Deucalion in Greece—which I consider to be fables—arose from the ancient tradition of the universal deluge <note>the Great Flood of Noah</note>. Similarly, the attempt by the Giants to scale heaven <note>(1)</note> likely arose from the presumptuous enterprise at Babel. But I cannot so easily trace the Titan war back to the rebellion in heaven and the fallen angels, as <note>(2)</note> some...

---

<margin>(1) "They strove to set Mount Ossa upon Olympus, and upon Ossa leafy Mount Pelion, so that heaven might be scaled." <note>original Greek: Ὄσσαν ἐπ' Οὐλύμπῳ μέμασαν θέμεν, αὐτὰρ ἐπ' Ὄσσῃ Πήλιον εἰνοσίφυλλον, ἵν' Οὐρανὸς ἀμβατὸς εἴη. Homer, *Odyssey* Book 11, line 314.</note> See Stillingfleet, *Sacred Origins* <note>original: *Origines Sacrae*, a popular 17th-century work defending the historical accuracy of the Bible</note> Book 1, chapter 5.</margin>

<margin>(2) "But I will explain the Titans, as others do, as the Demons whom God cast into Tartarus <note>original Greek: ἐταρτάρωσε (etartarōse)</note>." 2 Peter 2:4. Zacharias Bogan, Preface to the Epistle of Dickinson’s *The Phoenician Delphi* <note>original: *Delphi Phœnicizantes*, a 1655 work attempting to prove Greek myths were derived from Hebrew history</note>, Oxford, 1655.</margin>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>Of the Mythical <note>original: "Fabulous"</note> Ages.</header>
<meta>catchword: for</meta>

...as some have done; because I consider it to be a matter of real, true history.

<margin>The earliest history of the Greeks is not mythical.</margin>
One may believe, without being considered too gullible <note>original: "credulous"</note>, that the Greeks preserved very old memoirs in this manner—though perhaps not a formal history—stretching as far back as their gods, who were their first kings. These memoirs have been expanded and heavily corrupted regarding dates, people, and events; yet this does not destroy the historical root of the account. They held an ancient tradition that Uranus (also known as Caelus) was the sovereign of Greece and other lands; that he had sons called Titans; that Saturn was one of these and succeeded his father Uranus; and that Jupiter was the son of Saturn, who fought and defeated his uncles, the Titans, etc. This is the start of their history, and there is nothing absurd, inconsistent, or unbelievable about it; therefore, I think no serious objection can be made to its truth. However, the supernatural <note>original: "marvelous"</note> incidents and literary flourishes of the story were the work of later ages—the inventions of poets who, with their unique creative license, ignored all the (1) laws of history and chronology.

<margin>Chronology is essential to history.</margin>
Chronology is the very essence of (2) true history; without it, history would appear to be nothing more than a fable or a mere imitation of truth. Where this guide is available, we find its benefits even within mythical history:

---

(1) "Let him not dare to say anything false." <note>original Latin: "Ne quid falsi dicere audeat."</note> Tacitus.
(2) "For among those where the record of time is disconnected, it is not possible for historical accounts to be true." <note>original Greek: "Παρ' οἷς γὰρ ἀσυνάρτητός ἐστιν ἡ τῶν χρόνων ἀναγραφή, παρὰ τούτοις οὐδὲ τὰ τῆς ἱστορίας ἀληθεύειν δυνατόν."</note> Tatian, *Oration to the Greeks*, Section 50.

<summary>The author argues that Greek myths about gods like Uranus, Saturn, and Jupiter are actually distorted historical accounts of early kings, which poets later embellished with supernatural elements. He emphasizes that chronology is the vital element that distinguishes true history from mere fable.</summary>
<keywords>Uranus, Caelus, Titans, Saturn, Jupiter, Chronology, Tacitus, Tatian, Greek Mythology, Historical Euhemerism</keywords>

<margin>
*The duration of the Titan Empire.*
</margin>
To be more clearly understood, I further declare my opinion that the Titan empire—from which Greek mythology began—was of short duration, lasting only three successive generations of princes. It began with Uranus, was severely shaken under Saturn, and restored to its full strength <note>original: "vigour"</note> by Jupiter; and there, I believe, the direct line of succession ended. For after him we hear of no universal sovereign deity, unless (1) Mercury might be thought to have exercised that authority for a while; we find several signs <note>original: "tokens"</note> of his administration after Jupiter's death <note>original: "decease"</note>. 

Uranus, the founder of the empire, was dethroned by his sons, a fact that is not improbable in itself. The manner of his death has been universally believed throughout the ages; what makes it even more probable is that all (2) mythologists agree the Titans were urged <note>original: "instigated"</note> by their mother, Titaea, to commit this inhuman act. But despite his untimely end, Uranus must have reigned for many years, leaving behind such a numerous offspring <note>original: "issue"</note>: twenty-seven children by other women and eighteen by his wife Titaea, the youngest of whom was old enough to take the reins of government into his hands. Some poets claimed <note>original: "feigned"</note> that Saturn suffered the same fate as his father, but it is

---

(1) "After the death of this same Picus, also called Zeus [Jupiter], his son Faunus, also called Hermes [Mercury], reigned over Italy for thirty-five years." <note>original Greek: Μετὰ δὲ τὴν τελευτὴν τοῦ αὐτοῦ Πήκου, τοῦ καὶ Διὸς, ἐβασίλευσεν ὁ υἱὸς αὐτοῦ Φαῦνος, ὁ καὶ Ἑρμῆς, τῆς Ἰταλίας, ἔτη τριάκοντα πέντε.</note> John Malalas, *Chronicle*, Oxford edition, Book 2, p. 22. <note>John Malalas was a 6th-century Byzantine chronicler who sought to reconcile pagan history with Christian chronology.</note>
(2) Hesiod, Sanchoniathon, Apollodorus, etc. <note>These are ancient Greek and Phoenician sources for mythology and early history.</note>
(3) See *Enquiries into Europe*, etc., p. 21.

<summary>The author argues that the Titan Empire was a brief historical dynasty spanning three generations—Uranus, Saturn, and Jupiter—rather than an eternal mythological era, and notes the large number of children Uranus fathered.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the myth of Saturn being violently overthrown by Jupiter was a later fiction, contrasting it with historical accounts of a peaceful transition of power due to Saturn's old age.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>Of the Fabulous Ages.</header>

It is easy to see that this was only a fiction based upon the former story <note>The "former story" refers to the account of Uranus being dethroned by his sons, mentioned on the previous page.</note>. Diodorus says, (1) that Jupiter obtained the empire lawfully, without violence. (2) Lucian says that Saturn resigned the government to his son on account of old age and infirmities; nor is history lacking to confirm his assertion. (3) "Saturn's son Jupiter was called Picus <note>Picus was an ancient king of Italy in Roman mythology, whom later writers often identified with the Greek god Zeus/Jupiter.</note> and governed the East; and coming to his father, who reigned at the same time in the West, Saturn delivered up to him the empire of the West also, being then grown old and weary of life. And Jupiter, or Picus, reigned over the West together with Italy for a full sixty-two years." (4) Jupiter died and gave the Western empire to his son Mercury, and lived in all one hundred and twenty years; so he was fifty-eight years old before he enjoyed the whole empire. If Saturn had grown old by the time Jupiter was fifty-eight, he probably reigned as long as his son; and if Uranus reigned only thirty years, this will give at least one hundred and fifty years to the Titan empire—which I still call a short duration, considering the long lives of men in those early ages.

<margin>Jupiter identified as the Babylonian Belus.</margin>
To do this, it will be necessary to inquire into the real person of this god, who plays such a significant role in mythical history. It is universally agreed that he was the son and successor of Saturn. Historians, following the lead of the poets, credit him with a vast dominion over many distant countries. All of this can be believed without much difficulty; however, the suggestion that he appeared in several very different ages of the world—which these authors also imply—can never be true. Authors seem to have been discouraged from pursuing his true history by the obvious absurdities found in the myths. Furthermore, I might add that they looked no further for him than in Greece or Egypt. The Romans <note>original: "Latines"</note> also had a king named Picus, who reigned over their country and was considered the son of Saturn. <meta>This page continues the author's argument from the previous page, where he used various ancient sources to identify the Babylonian Belus with the Greek Jupiter and the Assyrian Picus. The catchword from page 14, "Excusing," begins the first sentence here.</meta>

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

Excusing this author’s inaccuracies, it is clear that his Bilus is the same as Picus, who reigned for sixty-two years. From all these accounts, I believe we may conclude that Jupiter, Belus, and Picus were one and the same person; that he was the son of Saturn; that he fortified Babylon; and that he governed the East as a <term>viceroy</term> <note>A ruler exercising authority in a colony or province on behalf of a sovereign.</note> under his father. This is perhaps suggested by his Latin name, *Jupiter*, or "*Young Father*." <note>The author is likely deriving the name Jupiter from the Latin *Puer* (boy/young) and *Pater* (father), though modern linguists derive it from *Dyeus-Phter* (Sky Father).</note> The fact that the <term>heathens</term> <note>original: "heathen"; a term used historically by Christians to describe people who followed polytheistic religions.</note> could not see this was due to the blindness of their religion. I now leave it to others to determine the era in which Belus lived; once that is done, I believe we will not disagree much about the time of Jupiter’s death.

<margin>There was only one Belus</margin>
We can see from the passages quoted above how obscure and complicated the beginnings of pagan history are. A recently published work by a learned author (1) has, in my opinion, confused the matter even more by claiming there were two different men named Belus: the first a Babylonian and the second an Assyrian. *The Babylonian Belus, he says, was opposed by Haic, the giant king of Armenia; after a bloody battle, he was killed along with most of his Titans, as Moses of Choren relates, based on the writings of Maribas of Catina.* <note>original: "Moses Chorenensis"; an Armenian historian (c. 410–490s AD) whose work "History of Armenia" is a primary source for Armenian legends. "Maribas of Catina" is a source Moses claims to have used.</note> However, these are authorities that I cannot trust, as the story seems too much like a work of fiction. Therefore, to make the timeline more consistent, I recognize only one Belus and assume him to be Jupiter.

---

(1) Mr. Jackson’s *Chronological Antiquities*, Vol. 1, pages 240, 262, and 263. <note>John Jackson (1686–1763) was an English chronologist who published "Chronological Antiquities" in 1752.</note>

<summary>The author concludes that Jupiter, Belus, and Picus are the same historical figure and argues against contemporary chronologists who claim there were two distinct men named Belus.</summary>
<keywords>Belus, Jupiter, Picus, Saturn, Babylon, John Jackson, Moses of Choren, Armenian history, Titans, chronology, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion of ancient historians and the identification of various deities and kings across different cultures. The text begins mid-word, completing the term "historians" from the previous page.</meta>

historians. Let me add that Ninus, the supposed son and successor of Belus, can be placed almost as early in time as the date I will establish for the death of Jupiter.

<margin>The reign of Ogyges: the first era in Greek history.</margin>
The Greeks could not believe that anything had happened in this world before the appearance of the gods; nor could they imagine their gods being limited by time. The reign of Ogyges in Boeotia, according to the Roman author Censorinus, is the first era in their legendary <term>fabulous</term> <note>original: "fabulous"; in this context, it refers to the age of myths and fables rather than "wonderful."</note> antiquity, and must be placed soon after the time of the gods. We need no other proof for this than the fact that everything so ancient that its origin could not be traced was called (1) "Ogygian." After the death of Jupiter, the Titan empire—like a meteor that has reached its full height—naturally burst into a thousand small sparks: governments, kingdoms, dynasties, and so on. All of these claimed their authority from Jupiter, for (2) royal power, or the "divine right of kings," was always believed to be derived from him. 

Homer beautifully illustrates this by introducing the (3) Scepter, the symbol of royal authority. He says it was given by Jupiter to Mercury, by Mercury to Pelops, and so, through a succession of kings, it eventually came into the hands of Agamemnon. This, incidentally, was the underlying reason for making Jupiter the father of so many gods and heroes. Around that time, it seems to me, the country of Boe-

---

(1) Ogygian: "Ancient, old, or vast; or named so because Ogyges was the first to rule Thebes." <note>original Greek: "ΩΓΥΓΙΟΝ. Αρχαῖον, παλαιὸν, ἢ ὑπερμέγεθες. ἢ διὰ τὸ Ωγυγον πρῶτον ἄρξαι τῶν Θηβῶν."</note> Suidas, under that entry.
(2) "Kings come from a line of kings reaching back to Jupiter." <note>original Greek: "Βασιλεῖς εἰσὶν ἐκ βασιλέων μέχρι Διός."</note> Plato in his dialogue *Alcibiades*.
(3) See *Iliad*, Book 2, line 104. <note>original: "Vid. Iliad. B. v. 104."</note>

<summary>This page discusses the chronological transition from the age of gods to the age of legendary kings, specifically the reign of Ogyges and the dispersal of the "Titan empire" into smaller human kingdoms. The author argues that the "divine right of kings" was established by tracing royal lineage back to Jupiter, as exemplified by Homer's description of Agamemnon's scepter.</summary>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the early rulers of Greece following the death of Jupiter and the fragmentation of the Titan empire. The text begins mid-word, completing "Boeotia" from the previous page.</meta>

### Of the Fabulous Ages.

---

-tia, by some means or other, fell to the share of (1) Ogyges, king of the Ectenes and supposed founder of Thebes. He is called an *Autochthon*, or *Aboriginal* <note>original: "Autochthon," a term for the original inhabitants of a land, often literally meaning "sprung from the earth."</note>, which I understand to mean a (2) *Scythian* or *Scytho-Grecian*.

<margin>
*The Ogygian Deluge is an uncertain era*
</margin>
Since this flood is merely a fable, it must necessarily be a very vague and unreliable era <term>epoch</term> <note>original: "epoch"; a period of time used as a starting point for history.</note> that the Greeks could never precisely determine. I believe the only thing we can be sure of is that if such a flood did occur, it must have happened later than the Great Flood of Noah. Personally, I find it hard to believe that any flood occurred in (3) Attica or Boeotia during the time of Ogyges. I suspect that, originally, they meant nothing more by it than the universal Great Flood; they called it "Ogygian" simply because it happened before all their other historical records. Later writers, wishing to claim the event as their own, could find no better place to set it than...

---

(1) "They say that the Ectenes were the first to inhabit the land of Thebes, and that their king was an aboriginal man named Ogyges; because of him, many poets refer to Thebes as 'Ogygian.'" <note>original Greek: Γῆν δὲ τὴν Θηβαΐδα οἰκῆσαι πρῶτον λέγουσιν Ἐκτῆνας, βασιλέα δὲ εἶναι τῶν Ἐκτήνων ἄνδρα Αὐτόχθονα Ὤγυγον· καὶ ἀπὸ τούτου τοῖς πολλοῖς τῶν ποιητῶν ἐπίκλησις ἐς τὰς Θήβας ἐστὶν Ὠγύγιαι.</note> Pausanias in *Boeotia*.

(2) See *Enquiries Concerning the First Inhabitants of Europe*, pages 18, 57.

(3) It might be asked: how did the Athenians come to have a part in this flood? I would answer that Ogyges is recognized as the oldest king on record, and he lived in Boeotia, where the city of Thebes stood as a lasting monument. Because of this, the Thebans would have a claim to a much greater antiquity than the Athenians—something the Athenians could never tolerate. Therefore, out of a sensitive sense of honor, they were willing to be included in the same disaster as their neighbors, the Boeotians; as proof of this, they even called their own country Ogygia. "All of Attica was also called Ogygia, as Charax says in his chronicles." <note>original Greek: Ἐλέγετο καὶ ἡ Ἀττικὴ πᾶσα Ὠγυγία, ὡς Χάραξ ἐν τοῖς χρονικοῖς.</note> Stephanus of Byzantium in his entry for *Ogygia*. I also suspect that because Ogyges was called king of the *Ectenes*, they invented the legend of their king *Actaeus* to strengthen their own claims. Cecrops married the daughter of *Actaeus*, after whom their country *Attica* was formerly called *Actaea* <note>original: Ἀκταία</note>, *Actaea*, or *Attica*. See Pausanias in *Attica*, page 4; *The Parian Chronicle* <note>original: "Chron. Marm. Oxon."; a reference to the Arundel Marbles housed at Oxford which contain ancient Greek chronological records.</note>, Era 1.

<margin>States that arose out of the Titan Empire.</margin>
Of those little states that rose out of the ruins of the Titan empire in Greece, the oldest of which we have any records were this one of Ogyges, in Attica and Boeotia, and those of Sicyon and Argos, in the Peloponnesus. The ancient fable of Phoroneus, preserved by Hyginus <note>Gaius Julius Hyginus (c. 64 BC – AD 17) was a Latin author who compiled popular myths and genealogies.</note>, gives us an obscure account of the beginning of these kingdoms and says: (1) "Inachus, son of Oceanus, had a son named Phoroneus by his sister Archia. Phoroneus is said to be the first mortal king that reigned. Long before his time, men lived dispersed about the country without laws and speaking one common language under the empire of Jupiter. But when Mercury had interpreted the different languages of men—from which an interpreter is called a *Hermeneutes* <note>original: "Hermeneutes"; a Greek term for an interpreter or translator, derived from the god Hermes (Mercury).</note>—he divided the nations. Then discord began among mortals because Jupiter was not appeased. Then he conferred the regal power first on Phoroneus, because he first appointed sacrifices to Juno." This discord among mortals—

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

<margin>Mercury established the borders of nations after Jupiter’s death.</margin>
If any credit is due to this fable, then after Jupiter’s death, it was the duty of Hermes (or Mercury) to settle the boundaries of peoples and nations. I imagine this gave rise to the notion that he was the inventor of (1) geometry <note>original: "Geometry"; the term literally translates to "earth-measurement," which fits the context of marking land borders.</note>. This power, I suppose, extended no further than the Western Empire, which it is said was bequeathed to him by his father. Evidence of him exercising such a power could formerly be seen in many countries, either through heaps of stones or four-sided stone pillars, which were the most ancient (2) boundaries of land. His primary residence was in Egypt. Those "Mercurial pillars" with inscriptions—from which so much history and learning of all kinds are thought to be derived—seem to me to have been nothing more than these boundary stones that marked out the larger districts of land. Those pillars which the Egyptians later mythically attributed to Sesostris <note>Sesostris was a legendary king of ancient Egypt said to have led military expeditions to the ends of the earth, marking his conquests with inscribed pillars.</note> were likely of this same sort. When the Greeks adopted Egyptian customs <note>original: "became Egyptianized"</note>, among other practices they learned how to set up square stones as landmarks to secure their property. They called these (3) Hermae <note>The *Hermae* (or Herms) were stone pillars topped with a head of the god Hermes, used in Ancient Greece as boundary markers and protective symbols.</note> in honor of the deity, as if their legal title to the land was founded upon his appointment. This custom, I

This practice, I suppose, was begun by Cecrops <note>The legendary first king of Athens, often depicted as half-man, half-serpent.</note>, for I find that the (1) Athenians set the example for the rest of the world. When the arts of statuary and sculpture became popular, they decorated these pillars with a human head. In more refined times, they added (2) drapery down to the middle, but no lower; the figure ended in a square stone. Some artists added the (3) genital parts <note>original: "obscene parts"</note>, but the legs and feet were always missing. The Romans developed their god Terminus <note>The Roman god of boundaries and landmarks.</note> from the Greek model; his name (4) implies that his origin came from Hermes. His appearance is so well known and matches the "Hermae" mentioned above so closely that it needs no further description.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of early Greek chronology, specifically focusing on the mythical "Fabulous Ages" and the founding of the Sicyonian and Argive kingdoms.</meta>

## Of the Fabulous Ages.

*Autochthones* <note>original: *Autochthones*; refers to the original inhabitants of a land, often believed to have been born directly from the earth.</note>, and the (1) flood <note>original: "deluge"</note> is said to have happened in the time of Phoroneus. By these circumstances, it appears to me that all these kingdoms began about the same time. Yet, by a reckoning of kings and the years of their reigns, the (2) Sicyonian kingdom began nearly 400 years before the Argive kingdom. Either the Greeks had raised their antiquities many centuries too high—which is (3) Sir Isaac Newton’s opinion—or perhaps, through some defect in the catalogue of kings, they had brought them down too low in the case of the Argive kingdom. One of these two suppositions must be granted before we can bring the time of Aegialeus to coincide with that of his brother Phoroneus.

Just as the Jews preserved genealogies and successions, the Greeks certainly had some method of keeping records of their kings and the years of their reigns. Otherwise, (4) Castor Rhodius, the chronologer in Julius Caesar’s time, could not have collected the catalogues of both the Argive and Sicyonian kings, as we find he did. And may we not think that these records were more carefully preserved in some kingdoms than in others? The Thebans seem to have lost all of theirs between the time of Ogyges and Cadmus, and the Athenians appear to have forgotten all that passed before the time of Cecrops. Nor did the Arcadians, who boasted of such great antiquity, have any records left before Lycaon, whom (5) Pausanias makes contemporary with Cecrops.

---

(1) *In his [Ogyges'] time, the first great flood occurred in Attica, while Phoroneus was king of the Argives, as Acusilaus records.* <note>original Greek: "Ἐφ' ὧ [Ogyge] γέγονον ὁ μέγας καὶ πρῶτος ἐν τῇ Αττικῇ κατακλυσμὸς, Φορωνέως Αργείων βασιλεύοντος, ὡς Ακουσίλαος ἱστορεῖ."</note> Eusebius, *Preparation for the Gospel*, Book X, chapter 10.
(2) See Mr. Jackson’s *Chronological Antiquities*, Vol. 3.
(3) See the Introduction to his *Chronology*.
(4) Eusebius, *Chronicon*, Greek page 19, 25.
(5) *I believe that the age of Cecrops, who ruled the Athenians, and Lycaon, was the same.* <note>original Greek: "Δοκῶ δ' ἔγωγε Κέκροπι ἡλικίαν τῷ βασιλεύσαντι Αθηναίων, καὶ Λυκάονι, εἶναι τὴν αὐτήν."</note> Pausanias in *Arcadics*, page 456. Sylburg edition.

contemporary with Cecrops. For lack of better evidence, we are forced to accept these thin <note>original: "jejune"; meaning lacking substance or interest</note> accounts of time, where, it must be admitted, we have little more than a simple list of kings' names without enough facts to prove them. If the (1) Sicyonian Era <note>original: "Æra"; a system of chronology dating from the founding of the city of Sicyon</note> is the starting point from which we must calculate, it falls around the year 2170 BCE, or about 1400 years before the first Olympiad <note>The Olympiads were four-year periods used by the Greeks for dating, starting with the first Olympic games in 776 BCE.</note>. If we calculate from the time of (2) Inachus and Phoroneus, it falls around 1700 BCE, and about 1000 years before the Olympiads. 

Which of these two is correct, I do not have enough authority to decide; the reader must judge for himself. It seems to me that Censorinus intended to calculate by the former method, by placing the Ogygian flood 400 years before Inachus. The main standard I use is the religious rites of Greece. I believe these could not have begun while any of the gods were still alive, despite the reports of Saturn sacrificing to Uranus, and Jupiter to Saturn. The Cabiric mysteries <note>original: "Cabiric mysteries"; ancient secret religious ceremonies held in honor of the Cabiri, a group of enigmatic deities</note> were admittedly their oldest form of worship; I believe they could not have been established as religious rites until after Jupiter's death. (2) Although we present it as the common opinion that Inachus lived about 1000 years before the Olympiads, this was not universally accepted. For Eusebius observes that, "According to many, there were 413 years from the first Inachus to the ninth king of Argos, Sthenelus." <note>original Greek: "Ἔστι δὲ κατὰ τοὺς πολλοὺς ἀπὸ μὲν πρώτου Ἰνάχου, ἕως τοῦ Θ Σθενέλου ἐτῶν υιγ."</note> Eusebius, *Chronicon*, page 24. Adding these 413 years to the time of Danaus—who overthrew Gelanor, the son of Sthenelus—places the age of Inachus 1200 years before the Olympiads and nearly 2000 BCE. This matches the calculations of Censorinus.

He was the first (1) idolater we read of; and (2) Eusebius and (3) St. Epiphanius date pagan <note>original: "gentile"</note> worship no earlier than the time of Serug, the grandfather of Terah. Serug's age is determined by (4) scripture, and part of his life coincides with the Sicyonian Era <note>The Sicyonian Era refers to the chronological system based on the founding of Sicyon, traditionally regarded as the oldest city in Greece.</note>; therefore, I could not place the beginning of Greek idolatry any earlier than his time. 

From the times of Ogyges and Inachus, the Autochthones <note>original: "Autochthones"; the indigenous or original inhabitants of a land, literally "sprung from the earth"</note> and Pelasgians <note>original: "Pelasgians"; an ancient group of people who inhabited the Greek mainland and Aegean islands before the arrival of the later Hellenic tribes</note> were the masters of Greece. This continued until new colonies arrived under Cecrops and Danaus from Egypt, Deucalion from Scythia, and Cadmus from Phoenicia. At that point, the Pelasgians began to decline in power and were eventually forced to seek new homes in Italy and other countries. 

Different eras are assigned to these new colonies; however, I cannot believe there was a distance of even one century between the first and the last. Furthermore, all of them must have occurred later than the migration of the children of Israel from Egypt <note>referring to the Exodus</note>. Deucalion brought with him another flood <note>original: "deluge"</note> into Greece, which, by the most accurate account, may be placed 400 years before the destruction of Troy. We are also told that there was an (5) interval of 600 years between Ogyges and Deucalion. This calculation places Ogyges 1,400 years before the Olympiads;

<margin>
*A summary timeline leading to the Christian Era.*
</margin>
As few readers will likely want to memorize the specific details of my calculations, I will provide a table or timeline below for their convenience, drawn from the authorities quoted above. The lack of historical materials and the differing calculations of various authors may excuse a lack of precision for a period of years that begins so far before the Christian Era. However, if we can come within fifty, or even a hundred years, of the true date of these various events, it is certainly more satisfying than knowing nothing at all about the time when they happened. For this reason, I avoid using all fractions, as they are quite unnecessary, and calculate using only whole numbers. We are doing well if we can come near to the truth by this way of calculating; I am afraid that those who have tried to be more exact, despite all their efforts, will be found to have come no closer to the truth.

From Varro's "On Agriculture" (De Re Rustica), Book 3.</note>

This method of calculation may be accepted as accurate until someone finds a more precise one. In the meantime, I leave these authorities to stand or fall on their own merit. I only propose what I believe cannot be disputed: that Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), a Roman scholar and writer known for his works on history and chronology.</note> and Censorinus <note>A 3rd-century AD Roman grammarian and author of 'De Die Natali,' a work on time and history.</note> could have meant nothing more by the *Fabulous Age* than the period that began with the first gods or kings of Greece and ended at the first Olympiads. 

The Ogygian deluge <note>A legendary flood occurring during the reign of Ogyges, often considered the earliest major disaster in Greek myth.</note>—from which Censorinus begins his calculation, as it is the first event recorded in history—must therefore be the beginning of what I call the Titan empire. He places this 1,600 years before the Olympiads. This results in a difference of 50 years between his tables and the other comparative tables; these fifty years must be added to the years of the gods, as they cannot be accounted for anywhere else. If the reader finds this consistent with the era of idolatry, he may follow the reckoning of Censorinus.

<margin>*These calculations compared with Scripture.*</margin>

To test our calculations, we must compare them with sacred history. Here, we face some initial difficulty, caused by the current Hebrew text differing from the Greek translation of the Septuagint <note>The Septuagint is the earliest Greek translation of the Old Testament, which often contains different chronological dates for the early patriarchs compared to the standard Hebrew Bible.</note>. The Hebrew...

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>The History and Chronology</header>

The Hebrew genealogies state that Abraham lived only 292 years after the flood, while the Greek <note>Referring to the Septuagint, the Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible, which provides different chronological data.</note> genealogies state he lived 1,072 years after it; so there is a difference of 780 years. I believe it cannot be supposed that Abraham lived as early as 292 years after the flood, because this timeframe does not allow sufficient time for the human population to grow. For this reason alone, the Hebrew chronology must be rejected—though there are other good reasons to think it has been intentionally corrupted by the Jews. 

I am aware that (1) learned scholars have calculated the increase of mankind to be many millions around that time. However, even if we admit their calculations are accurate, they do not explain how those millions were distributed across the face of the globe; it seems to me that few of them would have reached Chaldea, Mesopotamia, Arabia, or Canaan. Although we find the earth populated during Abraham's actual lifetime, at least as far as Egypt—and we find cities and countries governed by kings who were at war with one another—if we judge by the size of the forces they brought into the field, those regions were still only thinly inhabited even then. 

I have prepared a short chronological scheme based on a reliable (2) authority, which I believe the reader will find strictly aligns with the timeline of the scriptures. The reader may then judge whether the facts mentioned by Greek authors fit properly, and without being forced, into the places I have assigned them in this obscure period of secular <note>original: "profane history"; in this context, it refers to non-religious or world history rather than something disrespectful.</note> history.

(1) See Bishop Cumberland’s *The Most Ancient Origins of Nations* <note>original: "Orig. Gent. Antiquiss." or *Origines Gentium Antiquissimae*.</note>, page 150.

(2) Mr. Masson’s *Sacred Chronology of the Pentateuch*, published with Mr. Parker’s *Library of the Bible* <note>original: "Biblioth. Biblica."</note> Oxford <note>original: "Oxon."</note>, 1727.

<meta>This page continues the author's chronological exploration of the "Mythical Ages," moving from the earliest post-flood empires to the specific migrations that shaped early Greek history and the methods used to preserve records before the invention of the alphabet.</meta>

<margin>*A new era in Grecian history.*</margin>
After this epoch, I place the various migrations of Cecrops, Danaus, Deucalion, and Cadmus <note>These are legendary figures in Greek mythology: Cecrops was the mythical founder of Athens; Danaus fled from Egypt to Argos; Deucalion was the survivor of a great flood; and Cadmus was the Phoenician prince who supposedly brought the alphabet to Greece.</note>. I believe that these events happened at no great distance of time from each other. We are not told, as I recall, by any historian what caused such an influx of foreigners. It appears that they were driven from other countries, but the question is how they came to take refuge in Greece. Greece seems to have been in a defenseless state at that time, either through the laziness or the lack of inhabitants—or perhaps both. 

The Pelasgians <note>The Pelasgians were the indigenous people who inhabited the Aegean region before the rise of the Hellenic Greeks.</note>, who were then in possession of the land, were intruders at first, but were gradually naturalized to the country; and everything related to it was now in their hands. The newcomers neglected the ancient Pelasgian history. As for the natives, wherever any remained, they may be assumed to have lost their spirit along with their liberty, and they took no further interest in preserving their history. 

It has been argued that the Greeks could have no credible history or chronology before this time because they lacked letters <term>a written alphabet</term>. This is an objection that could be made to all histories written before that period; yet this lack might have been supplied by other means, currently unknown to us. The history and chronology of the world before Moses was carefully preserved, but not by written letters. The hieroglyphics of the Chinese and Egyptians maintained the history of those nations without any other assistance. Among the Greeks, songs passed down from father to son—as has been observed of their northern ancestors—might have been enough to preserve facts, successions, generations, and so on. Or, if something more than mere memory was required, the Greeks were not of such dull intellect that they could not invent...

# The History and Chronology

<margin>
*A new era in Greek history.*
</margin>

After this era <note>original: "Epoch"</note>, I place the various migrations of Cecrops, Danaus, Deucalion, and Cadmus, and I believe they occurred within a short time of one another. I do not recall any historian explaining what caused such an influx <note>original: "conflux"</note> of foreigners. It appears they were driven out of other countries, but the question remains: why did they seek refuge in Greece? At that time, Greece appears to have been defenseless, either due to the laziness <note>original: "indolence"</note> or the scarcity <note>original: "want"</note> of inhabitants, or perhaps both. The Pelasgians <term>the indigenous people of the Greek lands before the arrival of the Hellenes</term>, who were in possession of the land then, were originally intruders but gradually became naturalized to the country; everything related to the region was eventually in their control. The newcomers neglected the ancient Pelasgian history. As for the natives who remained, we can assume they lost their spirit along with their liberty and took no further interest in preserving their heritage.

It has been argued that the Greeks could have no reliable history or chronology before this time because they lacked writing <note>original: "want of letters"</note>. This objection could be raised against all histories written before that period, yet this deficiency might have been addressed by other means that are unknown to us today. The history and chronology of the world before the time of Moses was carefully preserved, though not through an alphabet. The hieroglyphics <term>a system of writing using pictures or symbols</term> of the Chinese and Egyptians maintained the history of those nations without any other help. Among the Greeks, songs passed down from father to son—as was observed among their northern ancestors—might have been enough to preserve facts, royal successions, and genealogies. Or, if something more than mere memory was required, the Greeks were not so lacking in intelligence <note>original: "of so dull a genius"</note> that they could not have invented

*The origin of the Scythians.*

Where pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note> authors do not contradict it, they may be believed; and where they have misapplied any parts of it, we know where to turn to find the truth. This history is a sketch, or summary, of the history of the world; it was not intended to be a complete history, for that would have been too vast. Its primary purpose is to show the descent of the Jews from their great ancestor Abraham, and his descent from Shem <note>original: "Sem"</note>. For this reason, the beginning of the Assyrian Empire is briefly mentioned because it included Babylonia, Chaldea, and Mesopotamia—countries where Abraham is said to have lived <note>original: "sojourned"</note>. However, little notice is taken of Japheth <note>original: "Japhet"</note>, though there are several reasons to believe he was the eldest son of Noah. His family seems to have been the most numerous, with seven (2) of his sons being mentioned as the fathers of nations. (1) <note>The most ancient records of things were likely preserved by engravings on stones or pillars, but what we are told about the contents of these monuments is highly suspicious. (1) The most ancient memorials of things were probably preserved by engravings on stones or pillars; but what we are told of the contents of these monuments is highly suspicious. <meta>This page continues the investigation into whether the northern nations' identification as descendants of Japheth stems from ancient oral tradition or later historical influences.</meta>

...what to think of them. What followed was not as easily forgotten. The repopulation of the world by the three sons of Noah was a historical matter that every person had an interest in remembering. It is difficult to imagine that their names could be forgotten until after their descendants had largely spread across the earth; even then, their names were perhaps remembered longest in the regions where the head of the family first settled.

A question must occur to every curious reader: "Where did the northern nations get this general belief that they were the descendants of Japheth <note>Japheth: The son of Noah traditionally associated with the peoples of Europe and Northern Asia.</note>?" Did they possess this knowledge through ancient <note>original: "primeval"</note> tradition, or from the books of Moses <note>The books of Moses: The Pentateuch, or the first five books of the Bible.</note>? Perhaps some will answer, "From neither," but rather from their southern neighbors: the Arabians, Medes, and Persians. 

But when could this have happened? **Answer:** The Medes and Persians might have learned it from the Ten Tribes of Israel, who were scattered among them by (1) Shalmaneser <note>original: "Salmanasser"; Shalmaneser V was the Assyrian king who conquered the Kingdom of Israel.</note>. Furthermore, the Arabians, who brought their armies into Tartary <note>Tartary: A historical name used by Europeans for a vast region in North and Central Asia inhabited by Turco-Mongol peoples.</note>, might be assumed to have had some knowledge of the sacred books from the time of Muhammad <note>original: "Mahomet"</note>. 

But where do the sacred books explicitly state that Japheth was the father of these northern nations? **Answer:** The migrations of Shem <note>original: "Sem"</note> and Ham are charted toward the East, South, and West; therefore, no other path except the North is left for Japheth. However, this does not solve the difficulty; it does not answer the question, but rather assumes the very thing it is trying to prove <note>original: "begging the question"; a logical fallacy where the premise of an argument assumes the truth of the conclusion.</note>.

---

->(1) 2 Kings, chapter 17, verse 6.<-

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

<margin>
*What grounds there are for such an opinion.*
</margin>

Ever since the revival of learning <note>original: "revival of letters"; a reference to the Renaissance</note>, reconciling sacred history <note>sacred history: The historical accounts found in the Bible.</note> with secular <note>original: "profane"</note> history has been a task for European scholars, who knew very little about these northern regions of Asia. The Scriptures indeed suggest that the sons of Japheth—Gomer, Magog, and others—were located north of Palestine. However, this is not enough to explain the widespread belief that prevailed throughout the vast region we are discussing. The only ancient author who could provide modern scholars with any insight was Josephus <note>Josephus: A first-century Romano-Jewish historian whose works provide key links between Jewish and Greco-Roman history.</note>, and he did indeed place the sons of Japheth north of Palestine, but in southern regions well known to the Jews, Greeks, and Romans. This was perhaps precisely because those areas were familiar; he seems just as ignorant as modern people about the lands beyond the Caspian Sea. All he says on the matter (1) is, "Magog founded the Magogians, who are called Scythians by the Greeks." The Scythians made (2) several invasions <note>original: "irruptions"</note> into southern Asia and established settlements on that side of the Caspian and Euxine <note>Euxine: The ancient name for the Black Sea.</note> seas, reaching as far as Cappadocia <note>Cappadocia: An ancient region in central Asia Minor, modern-day Turkey.</note>. Their country might be

---

<margin>
(1) "Magog founded those who were named Magogians after him, but were called Scythians by the Greeks." <note>original: "Μαγώγης δὲ τοὺς ἀπ' αὐτοῦ Μαγώγας ὀνομασθέντας ᾤκισε, Σκύθας δὲ ὑπ' αὐτῶν προσαγορευομένους."</note> *Antiquities of the Jews, Book 1, Chapter 6.* Some learned modern scholars believe that the name and memory of Magog are preserved in the word "Moguls" or "Mogures," who are north-eastern Tartars. "Regarding the fact that [Hayton] says the Tartars were originally called 'Moguls,' I feel that a clear origin for the race is indicated. For it comes from Magog, son of Japheth; nor do I doubt that the name 'Moguls' has remained since then, just as we mentioned above that the Persians still call the Turks 'Mogures.'" <note>original: "Quod autem Tartaros principio MOGLOS vocatos ait [Haithonus] eo non obscuram gentis originem denotari sentio. called Scythia. These then seem to be the Magogians, or Scythians, mentioned by Josephus; but they are extremely different from the Scythians located beyond the Caspian Sea. These latter people must have been as unfamiliar with Josephus as they were with biblical <note>original: "sacred"</note> history. Therefore, there is more reason to believe that this was an opinion maintained by local tradition among the inhabitants of the country, rather than originating from this single and imperfect testimony by Josephus. For although the ancient history of the postdiluvian <note>postdiluvian: referring to the period after the Great Flood (the Deluge) mentioned in the Bible.</note> world is now found in only one book preserved by the Jews, there is reason to believe that its contents were once common knowledge among other nations—especially those in the East. This is evident from some of their legends <note>original: "fables"</note> quoted by modern travelers.

<margin>
*The Turks and Tartars are descended from Japheth.*
</margin>

Regardless of where they got their information, it is certain that the Turks and Tartars <note>Tartars: Historically, a name used by Europeans for various Mongol and Turkic peoples of Central Asia; now usually spelled "Tatars."</note>, who make up the majority of the inhabitants of this vast region, (1) claim that, "They are descended from Turk, the eldest son of Japhis, who was the youngest son of Nui." This genealogy may not be entirely without foundation, even though it disagrees with Scripture (which seems to identify Japheth as the eldest son of Noah, and Gomer as the eldest son of Japheth). This is especially true if the lineage originally read as: *Turk, the youngest son of Japhis, who was the eldest son of Nui.* If we are to be guided by the similarity of names—and we seem to have little else to go on at present—I would suggest that Thyras, Tyras, or Turas is the person from whom the

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

The Turks are descended from him, although he is the last in order of the sons of Japheth and is commonly considered the father of the (1) Thirasians—or, as the Greeks called them, the Thracians. The similarity of the names <note>original: "Affinity of sound"</note>, as we see, is the basis for both opinions; and I think it supports the connection to the Turks at least as much as to the Thracians. Besides, I have the good fortune here to agree with the Jewish rabbis: for the <term>Targums</term> <note>Targums: Ancient Aramaic translations or paraphrases of the Hebrew Bible.</note> of Jonathan and Jerusalem, when listing the colonies and provinces of the various founders in Genesis 10:2, write תרקי (*Turki*) <note>original: תרקי</note> instead of the Hebrew *Tiras* (or *Thiras*), which others translate as *Thracia*. Elias Levita <note>Elias Levita: A 15th-16th century Renaissance Hebrew scholar and teacher.</note> understands this *Turki* (2) to mean Turkey <note>original: "Turcia"</note>. There is a way to reconcile this difference by supposing that the Thracians originally had the same name as the Turks, which is not improbable. The *Turcae* mentioned by (3) Pomponius Mela, who seem to be the (4) *Iurcae* of Herodotus, are placed in Asiatic Scythia, beyond the Don River <note>original: Tanais</note> and the Sea of Azov <note>original: Palus Mœotis</note>; they were likely the first people to enter and settle in Thrace.

(1) "Tiras called those over whom he ruled Thirasians; however, the Greeks renamed them Thracians." <note>original Greek: Θειρᾶς δὲ Θείρας μὲν ἐκάλεσεν ὧν ἦρξεν· Ἕλληνες δὲ Θρᾷκας μετονόμασαν.</note> Josephus, *Antiquities of the Jews*, Book 1, Chapter 6.

(2) See Elias Levita's *Chaldaic Lexicon* and Buxtorf's *Chaldaic Lexicon*.

These also live by hunting in the following manner." <note>original Greek: Νέμονται Θυσσαγέται, ἔθνος πολλὸν καὶ ἴδιον· ζῶσι δὲ ἀπὸ θήρης. συνεχέες δὲ τούτοισι κατοικημένοισί εἰσὶ τοῖσι οὔνομα κεῖται Ἰύρκαι. καὶ οὗτοι ἀπὸ θήρης ζώοντες τρόπῳ τοιῷδε.</note> Herodotus, Book 4, Chapter 22. Herodotus and Mela certainly meant the same people, as they are described as neighbors to the Thyssagetae and as living in the same manner by hunting. The difference in the name must be blamed on an error in the copies of Herodotus, caused by a damaged letter "T" in *Iurcae* <note>The author argues that a damaged capital T looked like an I (or J), changing "Turcae" to "Iurcae."</note>. The learned reader may perhaps remember an inscription on the reverse of a coin of Carausius <note>Carausius: A 3rd-century Roman military commander who usurped power in Britain and northern Gaul.</note> which, not many years ago, produced what some antiquarians thought was a very great discovery; others, however, thought it was only due to a similar damage to the same letter.

# The History and Chronology

<margin>*The Russians of Asia are descendants of Japheth.*</margin>
The Russians of Asia, according to Eastern writers, are descended from (1) Russ, the fourth son of Japheth. Here another difficulty arises: where could the Eastern writers have obtained this idea? It was not from the Ten Tribes of Israel, nor from the Arabians—unless they had copies of the Hebrew text different from those we have today. As for the historian Josephus, he is completely silent on this matter. It is certain that the name "Russ" has no clear connection <note>original: "affinity"</note> to any biblical name among the sons of Japheth. However, it does seem to support the general opinion that the northern nations descended from him. The word in the original Hebrew means a "head" or "chief," but it represents a person in the Septuagint <note>Septuagint: The earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible, created in the 3rd and 2nd centuries BCE.</note> version of Ezekiel: (2) *“Son of man, set your face against Gog, in the land of Magog, the prince of Rosh <note>original: "Rhos"</note>, Meshech, and Tubal.”* Whether this "Rosh" was the person intended by the Eastern writers, I do not know. He is omitted from the list of Japheth's sons in the books of Moses, but because he is grouped here with Magog, Meshech, and Tubal, he seems to have a claim to be one of them.

<margin>*Russians and Muscovites descended from Rosh and Meshech.*</margin>
Based on the assumption that the sons of Japheth were located in Southern Asia, the learned Samuel Bochart <note>Bochart: A 17th-century French biblical scholar and geographer.</note> devised what seems to me a preposterous theory for the origin of the Russians. He found in Hebrew and Arabic authors that the River Araxes, which flows into the Caspian Sea from the south, was called the Rhos, and consequently the... <meta>catchword: country</meta>

***

(1) See Abü’l-Ghāzī’s *History of the Turks, etc.*, page 668. <note>Abü’l-Ghāzī: A 17th-century Khan of Khiva and historian who wrote about the genealogy of the Turks and Mongols.</note>

(2) "Son of man, set your face against Gog, and the land of Magog, the prince of Rosh, Meshech, and Tubal." <note>original Greek: Υἱὲ ἀνθρώπε στήρισον τὸ πρόσωπόν σου ἐπὶ Γὼγ, καὶ τὴν γῆν τοῦ Μαγὼγ, ἄρχοντα ΡΩΣ, Μοσὸχ, καὶ Θοβέλ.</note> Ezekiel, Chapter 38, verse 2.

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

The country Araxene <note>Araxene: A region near the Araxes River, located in modern-day Armenia and Azerbaijan.</note> had the same name. From this, he <note>Referring to Samuel Bochart, mentioned on the previous page.</note> conjectured that these southern Scythians, or the people of Rosh <note>original: "Rhos"</note> and Meshech, entered the Tauric Chersonesus <note>Tauric Chersonesus: The ancient name for the Crimean Peninsula.</note>, which he finds was likewise called Rosh, and gave their names to the *Russi* and *Moschi*, or the Russians and Muscovites in Europe. But is it not more probable that these people had those names before this imaginary return of the Scythians, and even before their first raids into southern Asia? I could not help but caution my readers against such a doubtful hypothesis, even though it is secondary to our main question: how did the Eastern writers come by the notion that *Russ* was the fourth son of Japheth and the father of the Asiatic Russians? But to return to our subject.

<margin>The difficulty of treating Fabulous History.</margin>
Diodorus <note>Diodorus Siculus: A Greek historian of the 1st century BCE.</note> says (2) "The dates of the Fabulous Age, before the Trojan War, cannot be precisely settled because there is no fixed point to be depended on from which they can be calculated." The ancient historians, therefore, wisely avoided the task, because they could derive no consistent timeline of events from that era. For instance, no man could set out to write a complete history of Jupiter without being aware of the thousand chronological errors <note>original: "parachronisms"</note> he would be guilty of in the course of the narrative. Differ—

---

<margin>(1) "It is credible that the Russians and Muscovites, nations most famous in European Scythia, descended from Rosh and Meshech—that is, from the neighboring Rossian and Moschian peoples around the Araxes mentioned by Ezekiel. It appears that the Russians, having crossed the Araxes, first occupied the Tauric Chersonesus and called it Rosh after their own name." <note>original Latin: *Credibile est ex Rhos & Mesech...*</note> Bochart, *Phaleg*, Book 3, ch. 18.</margin>

<margin>(2) "Regarding the periods of time included in this work, we do not define those before the Trojan War with certainty, because we have received no reliable chronological table <note>original Greek: *parapegma*</note> concerning them." <note>original Greek: *Τῶν δὲ χρόνων τούτων...*</note> Diodorus Siculus, Book 1, p. 4.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the difficulties of writing about the "Fabulous Age," specifically addressing how mythological confusion and the Greek claim of Egyptian origins have clouded historical truth.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>The History and Chronology</header>

...different names for the same gods, together with different gods with the same names, must often be very perplexing; and the scene of the same actions, set in different times and countries, must cause endless confusion. In addition to all this, the marvelous and incredible way of relating these stories was utterly inconsistent with the modest gravity of history. The Philosophers, on the other hand—who were the professional guardians of virtue and religion—were shocked by the vices that the gods shared with the worst of mortals. They frequently touched upon these facts but called in allegory to their assistance and explained them to have a more inoffensive meaning. Thus, though they preserved the facts, they destroyed <note>original: "annihilated"</note> all the history.

<margin>
*The first religion of Greece did not come from Egypt.*
</margin>

But modern historians are not under the same constraints; for they know that the heathen gods were men, subject to human passions, and they can distinguish truth from fiction in these fables. What has primarily hindered <note>original: "embarrassed"</note> them in searching into the antiquity of these fables is that deep-seated <note>original: "inveterate"</note> error of the Greeks themselves: "That all their religion came from Phoenicia and Egypt." 

However, it is very clear to me—and I believe it will appear so to others, based on the Greeks' own descriptions—that Uranus, the Titans, and the Cabiri <note>Cabiri: A group of enigmatic deities or spirits honored in mystery cults, often associated with the Pelasgians.</note>, their first deities, came from the Pelasgians, Thracians, or Scythians. Furthermore, the Cabiric worship was known and established in Greece long before the arrival of Cecrops <note>Cecrops: The legendary founder and first king of Athens, often said to have brought Egyptian culture to Greece.</note> or any other Egyptian strangers. Indeed, had it been known in Phoenicia, Cadmus <note>Cadmus: A Phoenician prince in mythology who was said to have founded Thebes and brought the alphabet to Greece.</note> would not have needed to go as far as Greece or Thrace to be initiated into these mysteries.

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

<margin>*Regarding the origin of the Pelasgians.*</margin>

At this point, it may be appropriate to compare the ancient history of other nations with that of the Greeks and to show the connection they share with each other. After the Autochthones <note>original: "Autochthones"; meaning: People believed to be indigenous or born from the earth itself.</note>—whom I will venture to call Cimmerians or Scythians—the Pelasgians <note>An ancient, pre-Greek people mentioned by various classical historians.</note> must be considered the most ancient people of Greece. However, they were not aboriginal natives of the soil, as the later Greeks fondly imagined. These people, as we observed before, were so named because they were a (1) seafaring people <note>The author derives the name "Pelasgian" from the Greek word *pelagos*, meaning "sea."</note>, and they originally came from (2) Thrace <note>A region in Southeast Europe, north of Greece.</note>. I suppose they came from the maritime coasts on both sides of the Bosphorus <note>The narrow strait connecting the Black Sea and the Sea of Marmara.</note>. According to some writers, they wandered over and conquered almost the (3) whole world. They seem to have been a mixture of people who grew very numerous through the gradual addition of men who took up that way of life. They were all called Pelasgians because of their profession, even though they might have come from different nations—much like the northern people who invaded the western parts of Europe in the ninth and tenth centuries, whom our historians call by the general name of *Pirates* <note>The author is referring to the Vikings or Northmen.</note>.

(3) "But as for the Pelasgians, having wandered over most of the inhabited world and having conquered most men, they settled there." <note>original Greek: "Ἀλλ' οἱ μὲν, Πελασγοὶ ἐπὶ πλεῖστα τῆς οἰκουμένης πλανηθέντες, ἀνθρώπων τῶν πλείστων κρατήσαντες, αὐτόθι κατοικῆσαι." from Plutarch’s *Life of Romulus*.</note>

### The Pelasgian Antiquities and Deities.

The Pelasgians (1) established the Cabiric mysteries <note>Secret religious rites dedicated to the Cabiri, ancient underworld deities or spirits often associated with fertility and the protection of sailors.</note> on the island of Samothrace, which is the earliest record we have of Greek religion. Herodotus says (2), "The Pelasgians originally sacrificed and prayed to the gods without calling them by specific names, as they had not yet heard of any; but a long time later, they learned these names from Egypt." This is partly true; for although the Cabiri <note>original: "Cabiri"</note> undoubtedly had their own proper names, these were always kept secret during the religious mysteries. Cadmus and his followers, when they arrived in Boeotia <note>A region in central Greece, famous for its capital, Thebes.</note>, continued to practice the Cabiric rites. However, other colonies that arrived in Greece around the same time—led by figures such as Cecrops, Danaus, and Deucalion—seem to have introduced different forms of worship. The worship of (3) Minerva <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Athena.</note> in the region of Attica began during the reign of Cecrops; Danaus built a temple to (4) Apollo Lycius <note>An epithet of the god Apollo, often interpreted as "Wolf-Apollo" or "Apollo the Light-Bringer."</note> in Argos; and Deucalion is said to be the first person who (5) built temples and altars to the Twelve Gods <note>The twelve primary deities of the Greek pantheon who resided on Mount Olympus.</note>.

---

<margin>(1) For these Pelasgians, who lived among the Athenians, previously inhabited Samothrace, and the people of Samothrace received the sacred Rites from them. <note>original: Τὴν γὰρ Σαμοθρηΐκην οἴκεον πρότερον Πελασγοὶ οὗτοι, τοί περ Ἀθηναίοισι σύνοικοι ἐγένοντο, καὶ παρὰ τούτων Σαμοθρήϊκες τὰ Ὄργια παραλαμβάνουσι.</note> Herodotus, Book 2, chapter 51.</margin>

<margin>(2) The Pelasgians formerly offered all their sacrifices and prayers to the gods without giving any of them a title or name, for they had not yet heard of any. ... But after a long time had passed, they learned the names of the gods that had been brought from Egypt. <note>original: Ἔθυον δὲ καὶ πάντα πρότερον οἱ Πελασγοὶ θεοῖσι ἐπευχόμενοι, ἐπωνυμίην δ' οὐδ' οὔνομα ἐποιεῦντο οὐδενὶ αὐτέων· οὐ γὰρ ἀκηκόεσάν κω· — Ἔπειτα δὲ χρόνου πολλοῦ διελθόντος, ἐπύθοντο ἐκ τῆς Αἰγύπτου ἀπιγμένα τὰ ὀνόματα τῶν Θεῶν.</note> Herodotus, Book 2, chapter 52.</margin>

<margin>(3) During the reign of Cecrops, the first olive tree grew on the citadel; and from the name of Minerva, who is called Athena in Greek, the city was named Athens. <note>original: Cecrope regnante primum in arce Oliva orta est: & ex Minervæ nomine, quæ Græce Athena dicitur, Athenæ nuncupatæ.</note> Eusebius, Chronicon, Year 460.</margin>

<margin>(4) For the people of Argos, the most famous site in the city is the temple of Apollo Lycius. The statue there in our time was made by Attalus the Athenian, but the original was dedicated by Danaus; both the temple and the wooden image were his offering. <note>original: Ἀργείοις δὲ τῶν ἐν τῇ πόλει τὸ ἐπιφανέστατον ἐστὶν Ἀπόλλωνος ἱερὸν Λυκίου· τὸ μὲν οὖν ἄγαλμα τὸ ἐφ' ἡμῶν, Ἀττάλου ἦν Ἀθηναίου, τὸ δὲ ἐξ ἀρχῆς Δαναοῦ, καὶ ὁ ναὸς καὶ τὸ ξόανον ἀνάθημα ἦν.</note> Pausanias, Description of Greece: Corinth, Book 2, p. 118.</margin>
