# Of the Ages of Fable

I have endeavored to prove that these Twelve Gods were the Cabiri <note>The Cabiri were a group of enigmatic deities worshiped in mystery cults across the ancient Greek world, often associated with fertility and protection from danger.</note> and that they were contemporaries of Jupiter; I see no reason yet to change my opinion. This, then, seems to be the time when the number of gods increased in Greece, when the Cabiri were worshiped separately and their names were made public. We may place this era more than 1,500 years before the Christian era, and at least 600 years after the death of Jupiter. 

Jupiter, from all the evidence available to me, was the first monarch in the Western world to receive divine honors after his death <note>The author is following "Euhemerism," a theory that ancient gods were actually historical kings who were deified by later generations.</note>. Once such reverence was paid to him, it was natural to deify his parents, Uranus and Saturn, and his other relatives. These figures might all have been worshiped by name in other countries while the Greeks practiced only the Cabiric mysteries. However, it remains impossible to prove that the Egyptians, Syrians, or even the Assyrians had any gods before these Titan deities. 

The mists that always surround such distant antiquity make objects appear not only blurry but larger than life; the guides who are meant to lead us through them are neither infallible nor entirely honest. If they have, in some cases, dated gods and heroes later than their actual time, they have in others placed them much too early. This often makes the same figure appear twice to us; however, upon closer inspection, they will prove to be one and the same. I can find no arguments to convince me that Jupiter Belus of Babylon or Jupiter Ammon of Libya were 

<margin>
Hellanicus says in the first book of the *Deucalionia* that Deucalion reigned over Thessaly, and in the same work he says that Deucalion established altars to the Twelve Gods. — Ancient commentator on the passage. <note>original: "Ὅτι δὲ καὶ ὁ Δευκαλίων ἐβασίλευσε Θεσσαλίας, Ἑλλάνικος ἐν πρώτῳ Δευκαλίωνίας φησὶν, καὶ ὅτι Δώδεκα Θεῶν βώμους Δευκαλίων ἱδρύσατο..."</note>
</margin>

He suggests that these gods were originally historical monarchs, such as Jupiter, who were deified long after their deaths.</summary>

<header>The History and Chronology</header>

...who are older than the Greek Jupiter; much less can I think that both of them came originally from (1) Egypt. Jupiter, as well as Uranus before him, is said to have (2) reigned over all the world; which, at this time, we think is too incredible to be believed. But if we can convince ourselves to think that he governed a large empire extending from the borders of India westward as far as Egypt—consisting of territories on both sides of the Caspian, Euxine <note>The Euxine Sea is the ancient name for the Black Sea.</note>, and Mediterranean seas, an empire such as Tamerlane <note>Tamerlane (1336–1405) was a Turco-Mongol conqueror who founded the Timurid Empire.</note> possessed in later times—this might have been the "whole world" then known to the Greeks. At the same time, this may account for the worship of Jupiter in so many distant countries. Later historians use the same hyperbole <note>Hyperbole refers to intentional exaggeration for emphasis.</note> when speaking of Alexander the Great and Tamerlane, saying they *conquered the whole world*. The case is exactly parallel in the latter instance; for it is certain that Tamerlane was master of this very empire of Jupiter, in even more than its full extent, as there was then no power able to control him.

Egypt made up only a small part of this empire, and can no more be believed to be the birthplace of the gods than that Jupiter reigned over the entire world. From all this, I conclude that the Egyptian gods belonged to the Titan race <note>In Greek mythology, the Titans were the deities who preceded the Olympian gods; here the author uses the term to categorize a specific era of "hero-gods."</note> and had no greater claim to antiquity than the Greek gods. When authors say the gods were derived from Egypt, they should be understood as referring to the series of ceremonies that always accompanied Egyptian religious worship. We acknowledge that these (2) came from Egypt, and I believe they can be no older than the time of Cecrops <note>Cecrops was the legendary first king and founder of Athens.</note>, who was a contemporary of Deucalion <note>Deucalion is the figure in Greek mythology who survived a great flood, similar to the biblical Noah.</note>. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Phoenician antiquity, comparing their claims of being the first nation to those of the Egyptians and analyzing the Phoenician accounts of gods like Saturn and Uranus.</meta>

### Of the Fabulous Ages.

...before Egypt. It begins with the formation of the world; and, not to be outdone <note>original: "behind hand"</note> by the Egyptians, it claims that the first gods and men were natives of Phoenicia <note>original: "Phenicia"</note>. This claim alone is enough to destroy the credibility of the entire account. Uranus and Saturn are said to be gods of Phoenicia; and the overthrow and death of the former are related with all their circumstances, just as they are in Greek history. It also mentions the parents of Uranus and Titea, namely Eliun and (1) Beryth—a historical detail that the Greeks were entirely unaware of. Whether this is true or false is not very important, as nothing more is said of Eliun except that he was a contemporary of the Cabiri <note>The Cabiri were a group of enigmatic underworld deities or spirits worshiped in ancient Greece and Asia Minor.</note>, was killed by wild beasts, and was afterwards deified. 

Saturn plays the largest part in this history. He is represented as a powerful king who reigned in Phoenicia, built cities there, and governed other kingdoms and countries; among other things, he gave Attica <note>The region of Greece surrounding Athens.</note> to his daughter Minerva. How this last detail ended up in Phoenician history is questionable; it seems to me to have been taken from the Greeks. Saturn had Hermes Trismegistus, or Taaut, as his secretary and appointed him to be king of Egypt. This must have been a great humiliation <note>original: "mortification"</note> to the Egyptians, if they could actually believe it. The author mentions the Titans and Cabiri, but so confusedly and superficially that it is clear he knew nothing more of them than their mere names. It

(1) "During the time of these [the Cabiri] there was a certain man called Eliun, named the Most High, and a female called Beruth; and they lived near Byblos." <note>original Greek: Κατὰ τούτους [Cabiros] γίνεται τις Ελιοῦν καλούμενος Ὕψιστος, καὶ θήλεια λεγομένη Βηροὺτ, οἳ καὶ κατῴκουν περὶ Βύβλον.</note> —Sanchoniathon, as quoted in Eusebius. There can be no doubt that by *Berout*, or *Beryth*, the author means the great deity of Berytus called *Baal Beryth* (Judges, chapter 8, verse 33) and that the author intended to show that the Greek gods were descended from the Phoenicians.

<page-num>50</page-num>
->The History and Chronology<-

It is remarkable that in all this history, no mention is made of Bacchus; whether this was by design or out of ignorance is uncertain.

<margin>
*Bacchus is a name common to several gods.*
</margin>
When examining the history of a person, the first thing to investigate is the time in which they lived. In the case of a god or hero, this can only be discovered with the utmost difficulty. We are told that (1) "The Pelasgians <note>The Pelasgians were the legendary indigenous people of Greece who lived there before the arrival of the Hellenes.</note> had not heard of Bacchus until long after they had received the names of the other gods from Egypt. (2) Melampus, the son of Amythaon <note>original: "Amytheon"</note>, was the first to bring Bacchus's name and sacred rites from Egypt into Greece," according to Herodotus <note>Herodotus (c. 484–425 BC) was a Greek historian often called the "Father of History."</note>. From this, we can infer only that the Greeks worshipped other gods before Bacchus. The poets were our first historians, and their task was often to disguise the truth; one of the methods they used was to create gods and heroes at their own whim. Tully <note>Tully is the traditional English name for Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the famous Roman orator and statesman.</note> mentions no fewer than five individuals who went by the name of Bacchus. (3) "We have many Dionysuses <note>Dionysus was the Greek name for Bacchus.</note>: the first born of Jupiter and Proserpina; the second from the Nile, who is said to have killed Nysa; the third with Caprius as his father, whom they say ruled as king of Asia, and for whom the Sabazian rites were instituted <note>original: "Dionysos multos habemus: Primum e Jove & Proserpina natum: Secundum Nilo, qui Nysam dicitur interemisse: Tertium Caprio patre, eumque regem Asiæ præfuisse dicunt, cui Sabazia sunt in-"</note>—"

---

(1) — "Then, after a long time had passed, they learned the names of the other gods brought from Egypt; but they learned of Dionysus much later." <note>original Greek: Επειτα δὲ χρόνῳ πολλῷ διελοόντος ἐπύθοντο ἐκ τῆς Αἰγύπτου ἀπηγμένα τὰ ὀνόματα τῶν θεῶν τῶν ἄλλων· Διονύσε δὲ ὕστερον πολλῷ ἐπύθοντο.</note> Herodotus, Book 2, chapter 52.

(2) "For Melampus was the one who explained to the Greeks the name of Dionysus, his sacrifice, and the phallic procession." <note>original Greek: Ἕλλησι γὰρ δὴ Μελάμπους ἐστὶ ὁ ἐξηγησάμενος τοῦ Διονύσου τό τ᾽ ὄνομα, καὶ τὴν θυσίαν, καὶ τὴν πομπὴν τοῦ φαλλοῦ.</note> Same author, chapter 49. Yet he seems to think that Melampus received his instructions from Cadmus <note>Cadmus was the legendary founder of Thebes and credited with bringing the alphabet to Greece.</note> and the Phoenicians <note>original: "Phenicians"</note> who settled in Boeotia <note>Boeotia is a region in central Greece.</note>. "It seems to me most likely that Melampus learned about Dionysus from Cadmus the Tyrian and those who came with him from Phoenicia to the region now called Boeotia." <note>original Greek: Πυθέσθαι δέ μοι δοκέει μάλιστα Μελάμπους τὰ περὶ τὸν Διόνυσον παρὰ Κάδμου τοῦ Τυρίου, καὶ τῶν σὺν αὐτῷ ἐκ Φοινίκης ἀπικομένων ἐς τὴν Βοιωτίην καλεομένην χώρην.</note> Same author, same place. However, it is more probable that Bacchus was brought from Egypt, as will be shown later; this shows how ignorant the Greeks were concerning the countries where their gods originated.

(3) Cicero, *On the Nature of the Gods*, Book 3, chapter 23.

...instituted. The fourth was born to Jupiter and the Moon <note>original: "Luna"</note>, to whom the Orphic rites <note>Mystical religious rituals associated with the legendary poet Orpheus.</note> are thought to be dedicated. The fifth was born to Nisus and Thyone <note>original: "Thione"</note>, by whom the Triennial festivals <note>original: "Trieterides"</note> are thought to have been established.

The first, the son of Jupiter and Proserpina, was surnamed Zagreus; he was killed and (1) torn to pieces by the Titans. The second, the son of Nilus <note>The god of the Nile River.</note>, seems to be the Egyptian Osiris; although why he is said to have killed Nysa, I cannot guess. I find a (2) female warrior of that name who fought under Bacchus, and another who was his nurse, but neither of them was likely to be killed by him. I therefore suspect an (3) error in the text. The third, the son of Caprius, seems to be the great conqueror of Asia, but I have never encountered his father’s name in any other author. The fourth, the son of Jupiter and the Moon, to whom the Orphic rites were dedicated, is called by others the son of Ceres (4), who is sometimes identified as the moon. The fifth, the son of Nisus and (5) Thyone, who established the triennial festivals, *Trieterica*, is the Theban Bacchus—the son of Semele—who is so famous among the Greeks. (1) "Regarding Dionysus, also called Zagreus, the son of Jupiter and Proserpina: the Titans tore him limb from limb, but Minerva carried away his still-beating heart." <note>original Greek: Διόνυσον δὲ, τὸν καὶ Ζαγρέα καλέμβνον... from Tzetzes's *Allegories*.</note>
(2) "He broke the wound of Nysa's newborn face, sprinkling the lake's drink with a bloody wound, pouring it here and there on her cheek with white gypsum." <note>original Greek: ΝΥΣΗΣ δ’ ἕλκος ἔθραυσε... from Nonnus’s *Dionysiaca*, Book 29, line 272.</note>
(3) Bacchus is said to have built a city called Nysa; and perhaps Tully <note>Cicero</note> wrote, "He who is said to have founded Nysa" <note>original Latin: *Qui Nyſam dicitur inſtruxiſſe.*</note>
(4) "Virgil, knowing that Father Liber <note>An Italian god of fertility and wine, often identified with Bacchus.</note> was the Sun, and Ceres was the Moon." <note>original Latin: *Virgilius ſciens Liberum Patrem Solem eſſe, & Cererem Lunam.* Macrobius, *Saturnalia*, Book 1, chapter 18.</note>
(5) Bacchus made his mother a goddess and called her Thyone: "And he, having brought his mother up from the underworld <note>original: "Hades"</note> and naming her Thyone, ascended into heaven with her." <note>original Greek: ὁ δὲ ἀναγαγὼν ἐξ ᾅδου τὴν μητέρα... from Apollodorus’s *Library*, Book 3, chapter 5.</note>

...instituted: The fourth by Jupiter and the Moon <note>original: "Luna"</note>, to whom the Orphic rites <note>Mystical religious rituals associated with the legendary poet Orpheus.</note> are thought to be performed; the fifth born to Nisus and Thyone <note>original: "Thione"</note>, by whom the Triennial festivals <note>original: "Trieterides"; these were festivals celebrated every three years in honor of Bacchus.</note> are thought to have been established.

The first, the son of Jupiter and Proserpina <note>original: "Proserpine"</note>, was surnamed Zagreus; he was killed and (1) torn to pieces by the Titans. The second, the son of Nilus <note>The god of the Nile River.</note>, seems to be the Egyptian Osiris; although why he is said to have killed Nysa, I cannot guess. I find a (2) female warrior of that name who fought under Bacchus, and another who was his nurse, but neither of them was likely to be killed by him. I therefore suspect an (3) error in the text. The third, the son of Caprius, seems to be the great conqueror of Asia, but I have never encountered his father’s name in any other author. The fourth, the son of Jupiter and the Moon, to whom the Orphic rites were dedicated, is called by others the son of Ceres (4), who is sometimes identified as the moon. The fifth, the son of Nisus and (5) Thyone, who established the triennial festivals, *Trieterica*, is the Theban Bacchus—the son of Semele—who is so famous among the Greeks. Diodorus <note>Diodorus Siculus (1st century BC), a Greek historian who wrote a universal history.</note> mentions two more: one, the son of Jupiter and Io, the daughter of Inachus, who was probably the Egyptian

# The History and Chronology

Bacchus; the other, the son of Ammon and Amalthea, about whom we shall speak at length later. Despite these different genealogies, one and the same god is meant by all of them; or to speak more clearly, they were all branches from one common origin.

<margin>On the age of Bacchus.</margin>
Here we are left, as usual, without any historical dates to distinguish these gods, and I fear we shall never be able to fix the age of the most ancient among them. However, it is appropriate to proceed as far as we can toward that goal. It will be necessary to establish a point in time from which we may calculate; let it be the common Christian Era <note>original: "vulgar Christian æra"</note>, or the year 752 since the founding of Rome <note>original: "A. V. C. 752," abbreviation for *Ab Urbe Condita*.</note>. So that we may gradually go further back in time, we must establish a few supporting eras from this point that are generally agreed upon by scholars and which are required by the materials we are examining. The reader, therefore, should keep in mind:

| | Before Christ |
|---------------------------------------------------|----------------|
| The reign of Amasis in Egypt — — — | 570 |
| Herodotus, the father of History — — — | 450 |
| The reign of Alexander the Great — — — | 330 |

To begin with the Egyptian accounts of time: the priests informed (1) Herodotus that "Bacchus lived 15,000 years before the reign of Amasis." If we convert these into years of (2) twelve months each, they might amount to—

---

> (1) "For Dionysus, the fewest of these [years] is given; they calculate fifteen thousand years for him until the reign of King Amasis." <note>original Greek: Διονύσῳ δ' ἐλάχιστα τούτων· καὶ τούτῳ Πεντακισχίλια καὶ Μύρια λογίζονται εἶναι ἐς Ἄμασιν Βασιλέα.</note> Herodotus, Book 2, chapter 145.
> (2) The Egyptians, according to Eudoxus <note>A Greek astronomer and mathematician (c. 390–337 BC).</note>, called a monthly revolution a year. "And if what Eudoxus says is true, that the Egyptians called the month a year." <note>original Greek: Εἰ δὲ καὶ ὅ φησιν Εὔδοξος ἀληθὲς, ὅτι Αἰγύπτιοι τὸν Μῆνα Ἐνιαυτὸν ἐκάλουν.</note> Proclus, *On Plato's Timaeus*, Book 1. "For [Varro] says that among the Egyptians, months are held to be years." <note>original Latin: *Ait enim [Varro] apud Ægyptios pro Annis Menses haberi.*</note> Lactantius, *On the False Religion*, Book 2, chapter 12.

...about 1250, and with the time of Amasis, 1820 <note>These figures refer to years before the Christian Era, calculated on the previous page.</note>. But Herodotus, giving his opinion of the Greek gods, (1) says, "From Bacchus son of Semele to his own time, were 1,600 years," which will make 2050 BCE. This indeed goes vastly beyond the time of Cadmus and Semele; and therefore we must suppose that there is an error in the text of Herodotus, or that he confounded the Theban Bacchus with the Egyptian one. 

Diodorus (2) says, "According to some accounts, Osiris, or the Egyptian Bacchus, lived 23,000 years before Alexander." These reduced, as before <note>likely referring to a calculation where "years" are interpreted as months or shorter cycles</note>, make about 1,917, and with the time of Alexander, 2,247 BCE. But the Egyptians, as (3) Sir Isaac Newton rightly observes, by inventing new kings and reigns, had raised their antiquities some hundreds of years between the times of Herodotus and Diodorus. 

Arrian, in his Indian history (4), says, "The Indians reckoned from Bacchus to Sandrocottus <note>Sandrocottus is the Greek name for the Indian Emperor Chandragupta Maurya.</note>, who lived in the time of Alexander, 153 kings, who reigned 6,042 years." This exceeds all belief and is inconsistent with all accounts of time, both sacred <note>biblical</note> and profane <note>secular</note>. According to Sir Isaac Newton's method of computation,

"Where there is no doubt that sixty should be read instead of six hundred." <note>original Latin: *Ubi nullum est dubium, quin pro Ἑξακόσια legendum fit Ἑξήκοντα, pro Sexcenti, inquam, Sexaginta.*</note> *Notes on the Parian Chronicle* <note>original: *Not. ad Chron. <header>The History and Chronology</header>

The Ara-bians worshipped only (1) Uranus and Bacchus, laying no claim, that we know of, to the birth of the former; but either they, or the Egyptians on their behalf, affirmed that Bacchus was born at Nysa in <term>Arabia Felix</term> <note>original: "Arabia Felix"; Latin for "Happy Arabia," referring to the fertile southern region of the Arabian Peninsula, modern-day Yemen.</note>. From here the Egyptians received him and made him their (2) Osiris and Sesostris <note>Sesostris was a legendary king of ancient Egypt who was said to have conquered the world; historians often debate whether he was based on a real Pharaoh like Senusret III or Ramses II.</note>, whom they celebrated as the greatest of conquerors. To Egypt, then, we should attribute that multitude of fables relating to the expeditions of Bacchus: of his leading an army from one end of the world to the other, through India, Arabia, Ethiopia, Egypt, and Libya—to which we may add Thrace, though that seems to be an addition by the Greek poets, as it lies too far off the other route.

<margin>*Alexander a rival of Bacchus.*</margin>
Alexander the Great came many ages after the gods, and yet he desired to be considered one of their number. Making allowance for his faults and foibles, he was certainly possessed of many shining qualities and was worthy of being called a hero. His thirst for knowledge encouraged the Greeks of his time to make very useful discoveries in many arts and sciences; and I question whether we should have known anything at all of the true history of Bacchus if this Grecian hero had not been fired with a spirit of emula-

***

(1) The story goes that Alexander heard the Arabs worshipped only two gods: Uranus and Dionysus <note>original: "Λόγος δὲ κατέχει, ὅτι ἤκυεν [Alexander] Ἄραβας δύο μόνον τιμᾶν θεὸς, τὸν Οὐρανόν τε καὶ τὸν Διόνυσον." Arrian, *Anabasis of Alexander*, Book 7.</note>. Herodotus makes Uranus a female deity, calling her Urania. "They believe Dionysus is the only god, along with Urania—they call Dionysus Orotalt and Urania Alilat" <note>original: "Διόνυσον δὲ θεὸν μῦνον, καὶ τὴν Οὐρανίην ἡγέονται εἶναι — ὀνομάζουσι δὲ τὸν μὲν Διόνυσον Οὐροτάλτ τὴν δὲ Οὐρανίην Ἀλιλάτ." Herodotus, *Histories*, Book 3, Section 8.</note>.

(2) "For the Egyptians do not all worship the same gods in the same way, except for Isis and Osiris, whom they say is Dionysus" <note>original: "Θεὸς γὰρ δὴ οὐ τοὺς αὐτοὺς ἅπαντες ὁμοίως Αἰγύπτιοι σέβονται, πλὴν Ἴσιός τε καὶ Ὀσίριδος, τὸν δὴ Διόνυσον εἶναι λέγουσι." Herodotus, Book 2, Section 42.</note>. "For the Egyptians say that the god who is named Osiris among them is the same one called Dionysus by the Greeks" <note>original: "Αἰγύπτιοι μὲν γὰρ τὸν παρ' αὐτοῖς θεὸν Ὄσιριν ὀνομαζόμενόν φασιν εἶναι τὸν παρ' Ἕλλησι Διόνυσον καλούμενον." Diodorus Siculus, Book 3, page 174.</note>. Diodorus Siculus, Book 1.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Bacchus, focusing on Alexander the Great’s attempt to emulate his legendary conquest of India and the historical accounts provided by Diodorus Siculus.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
# Of the Fabulous Ages.

<sig>H</sig>

ambition of equaling his exploits. Anyone who has read the history of Alexander must know that he made it a point to follow in the footsteps of Bacchus. Therefore, I believe he is the most reliable guide we can follow after all this time. He likely gathered everything that was remembered about Bacchus's history and seems to point us toward the actual route of his expedition by advancing his own armies into India.

<margin>The first, or Indian, Bacchus.</margin>
Diodorus, in his second book, provides a description of India and (1) "thought it appropriate to provide a summary of what the most learned among the Indians had recounted regarding their ancient history. They say that in the most ancient times, when their people lived only in villages, Bacchus arrived from the western regions with a

---

> (1) The most learned among the Indians relate myths which it is appropriate to recount briefly. They say that in the most ancient times, when people still lived in villages, Dionysus arrived from the western regions with a significant force. He overran all of India, as there was no notable city capable of resisting him. However, when great heat arose and Dionysus’s soldiers were being destroyed by a plague, this leader, who was distinguished by his wisdom, led the army out of the lowlands into the mountains. There, with cold winds blowing and pure spring waters flowing right at their sources, the army recovered from the disease. This mountainous place was called <term>Meros</term> <note>original: "Μηρὸν"; in Greek, *meros* means "thigh".</note>, the place where Dionysus protected his forces from the plague. From this event, the Greeks handed down to later generations the story that Dionysus was "nurtured in a thigh." Afterward, he took care to cultivate crops and shared them with the Indians; he also introduced the discovery of wine and other things useful for life. In addition, he became the founder of notable cities, moving people from villages to suitable locations. He also taught them to honor the divine and introduced laws and courts. Overall, having introduced many fine works, he was considered a god and received immortal honors. They also record that he led a multitude of women with his army and used drums and cymbals in battle formations, as the trumpet had not yet been invented. After reigning over all of India for fifty-two years, he died of old age. His sons succeeded him in power, always leaving the rule to their descendants. Finally, many generations later, the monarchy was dissolved, and the cities became democracies. These are the myths related by the inhabitants of the mountainous regions of India concerning Dionysus and his descendants. <note>original: "Μυθολογοῦσι δ' παρὰ τοῖς Ἰνδοῖς οἱ λογιώτατοι, περὶ ὧν καθῆκον ἂν εἴη συντόμως διελθεῖν· φασὶ γὰρ, ἐν τοῖς ἀρχαιοτάτοις χρόνοις, παρ' αὐτοῖς ἔτι τῶν ἀνθρώπων κωμηδὸν οἰκούντων, παραγενέσθαι τὸν Διόνυσον ἐκ τῶν πρὸς ἑσπέραν τόπων, ἔχοντα δύναμιν ἀξιόλογον· ἐπελθεῖν δὲ τὴν Ἰνδικὴν ἅπασαν, μηδεμιᾶς οὔσης ἀξιολόγου πόλεως τῆς δυναμένης ἀντιτάξασθαι· ἐπιγενομένων δὲ καυμάτων μεγάλων, καὶ τῶν Διονύσου στρατιωτῶν λοιμικῇ νόσῳ διαφθειρομένων, συνέσει διαφέροντα τὸν ηγεμόνα τοῦτον ἀπαγαγεῖν τὸ στρατόπεδον ἐκ τῶν πεδινῶν τόπων εἰς τὴν ὀρεινήν. ἐνταῦθα δὲ πνεόντων ψυχρῶν ἀνέμων, καὶ τῶν ναματιαίων ὑδάτων καθαρῶν ῥεόντων πρὸς αὐταῖς ταῖς πηγαῖς, ἀπαλλαγῆναι τῆς νόσου τὸ στρατόπεδον· ὀνομάζεσθαι δὲ τῆς ὀρεινῆς τὸν τόπον τοῦτον Μηρὸν, καθ' ὃν ὁ Διόνυσος ἐξέτρεψε τὰς δυνάμεις ἐκ τῆς νόσου. ἀφ' οὗ δὴ καὶ τοὺς Ἕλληνας περὶ τοῦ θεοῦ τότε παραδεδωκέναι τοῖς μεταγενεστέροις, τετράφθαι τὸν Διόνυσον ἐν μηρῷ. μετὰ δὲ ταῦτα τῆς παραθέσεως τῶν καρπῶν ἐπιμεληθέντα, μεταδιδόναι τοῖς Ἰνδοῖς, καὶ τὴν εὕρεσιν τοῦ οἴνου, καὶ τῶν ἄλλων τῶν εἰς τὸν βίον χρησίμων παραδοῦναι. πρὸς δὲ τούτοις, πόλεών τε ἀξιολόγων γεννηθῆναι κτίστην, μεταγαγόντα τὰς κώμας εἰς τοὺς εὐθέτους τόπους, τιμᾶν τε καταδεῖξαι τὸ θεῖον, καὶ νόμους εἰσηγήσασθαι καὶ δικαστήρια· καθόλου δὲ πολλῶν καὶ καλῶν ἔργων εἰσηγητὴν γενόμενον, θεὸν νομισθῆναι, καὶ τυχεῖν ἀθανάτων τιμῶν. ἱστοροῦσι δ' αὐτὸν καὶ γυναικῶν πλῆθος μετὰ τοῦ στρατοπέδου περιάγεσθαι καὶ κατὰ τὰς ἐν τοῖς πολέμοις παρατάξεις τυμπάνοις καὶ κυμβάλοις κεχρῆσθαι, μήπω σάλπιγγος εὑρημένης. βασιλεύσαντα δὲ πάσης τῆς Ἰνδικῆς ἔτη δύο πρὸς πεντήκοντα γήρᾳ τελευτῆσαι. διαδεξαμένους δὲ τοὺς υἱοὺς ἡγεμονίαν, ἀεὶ τοῖς ἐφ' ἑαυτῶν ἀπολιπεῖν τὴν ἀρχήν· τὸ δὲ τελευταῖον, πολλαῖς γενεαῖς ὕστερον καταλυθείσης τῆς ἡγεμονίας, δημοκρατηθῆναι τὰς πόλεις. Περὶ μὲν οὖν τοῦ Διονύσου καὶ τῶν ἀπογόνων αὐτοῦ τοιαῦτα μυθολογοῦσιν οἱ τὴν ὀρεινὴν τῆς Ἰνδικῆς κατοικοῦντες. Diodor. Lib. 2. p. 87."</note>

# The History and Chronology

"...great force and invaded all of India; there was no significant city able to withstand him. But because of the great heat, his soldiers died of a plague-like illness, until that wise general led his troops from the plains to the mountains. There, the gusts of cool winds and pure waters flowing from the springs relieved the army from the plague. The place where Bacchus refreshed his forces was called (1) *Meros*. Because of this name, the Greeks invented the story of his being nourished in a **thigh** <note>original: "Thigh"; the Greek word *meros* (μηρός) means "thigh," leading to the myth that Dionysus was sewn into the thigh of Zeus.</note>.

After this, he applied himself to the cultivation of fruit and shared his knowledge with the Indians. He taught them the invention of wine and other things useful for living. Besides this, he was the founder of many great cities, moving the inhabitants from villages to more conveniently situated locations. He also taught them religion, established laws, and created courts of justice. After many famous deeds, he was regarded as a god and received divine honors.

They also report that he led a group of women within his army and that in battle he used drums and cymbals, as the trumpet had not yet been invented. After he had reigned over all of India for fifty-two years, he died of old age. His sons took over the government and passed it to their own sons, until many ages later, the monarchy was abolished and the cities were ruled as a democracy. These are..."

---

(1) <note>original Latin: "Urbium quas incolunt, Nyſa eſt clarisſima & maxima; Montium Meros, Jovi Sacer; Famam hinc præcipuam habent, in illa genitum, in hujus ſpecu Liberum arbitrantur eſſe nutritum: unde Græcis autoribus, ut Femori Jovis inſitum dicerent, aut materia ingesſit aut error."</note> "Of the cities which they inhabit, Nysa is the most famous and largest; of the mountains, Meros is sacred to Jupiter <note>the Roman name for Zeus</note>. From this they derive their primary fame: they believe that Liber <note>another name for Bacchus/Dionysus</note> was born in the former and nurtured in a cave of the latter. From this, the Greek authors were led to say he was sewn into the thigh of Jupiter, either because the subject matter suggested it or because of an error." — Pomponius Mela, Book 2, Chapter 11.

# Of the Mythical Ages <note>original: "Fabulous Ages"; here meaning the era of myths and legends</note>

> "...the things reported of Bacchus and his descendants by the inhabitants of the mountainous parts of India."

To this I must add that the mountain dwellers were the most likely people to preserve the tradition in its greatest purity. Although I will not guarantee the truth of every detail in this account, I still view it as the best and most authentic record of Bacchus's actions. All other accounts—whether Arabian, Egyptian, African, or Greek—may, with my agreement, be included in the history of Bacchus as long as they agree with this one. Where they differ, they should be rejected as purely mythical <note>original: "fabulous"</note> and without any historical foundation.

<margin>*Why Bacchus is said to have conquered the world.*</margin>
The same historian in his third book says, (1) "Some affirmed that there was only one Bacchus, while others said there were three. They believe these three lived at different times and attribute specific deeds to each one. They say the oldest was born in India—a country which, due to its fertility, naturally produces vines in abundance. There, he first discovered how to press grapes and make wine. This was the Bacchus who marched through the whole world with an army and taught people how to plant vines." He likewise

(1) <note>Translation of the original Greek: "For some declare that he was one and the same person—but others, as I said before, suggesting that there were three born at separate times, attribute specific deeds to each. They say that the oldest was an Indian, and as the land spontaneously produced many vines due to its temperate climate, he was the first to press the clusters of grapes and devise the use of the product of the vine. This was the Dionysus who traveled through the whole inhabited world with an army and taught the cultivation of the vine." Diodorus Siculus, Book 3, pages 138, 139.</note>

"...the things reported about Bacchus and his descendants <note>original: "posterity"</note> by the inhabitants of the mountainous regions of India."

To this I must add that the mountaineers were the most likely people to preserve the tradition in its purest form. Although I will not guarantee <note>original: "vouch for"</note> the truth of every detail in this account, I still consider it the best and most authentic record of Bacchus's actions. All other accounts—whether Arabian, Egyptian, African, or Greek—may be included in the history of Bacchus as long as they agree with this one. Where they differ, they should be rejected as purely mythical <note>original: "fabulous"</note> and lacking any historical foundation.

<margin>
*Why Bacchus is said to have conquered the world.*
</margin>

In his third book, the same historian <note>Diodorus Siculus, a Greek historian of the 1st century BC.</note> says: (1) "Some claimed <note>original: "affirmed"</note> that there was only one Bacchus, while others argued there were three. They believe these three lived at different times and attribute specific deeds to each. They say the oldest was born in India, a country which, due to its fertility, naturally <note>original: "spontaneously"</note> produces vines in abundance. It was there that he first discovered how to press grapes and make wine. This was the Bacchus who marched through the entire world with an army and taught the cultivation <note>original: "planting"</note> of vines." He also

(1) <note>original Greek: Ἔνιοι μὲν γὰρ ἕνα καὶ τὸν αὐτὸν ἀποφαίνονται γενέσθαι... (Diodor. Lib. 3. p. 138, 139). Translation: "For some declare that Bacchus was one and the same person; but others, as I said before, suggesting there were three who lived at different times, assign to each his own actions. They say that the most ancient was Indian, and that since the country, because of its mild climate, produced many vines of its own accord, he was the first to press grapes and discover the use of wine... This Dionysus, then, having traveled over the whole inhabited world with an army, taught the cultivation of the vine."</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 59 regarding the Greek god Bacchus (Dionysus) and his legendary origins in India, examining whether his "conquests" were historical events or allegories for the spread of wine.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>

...says (1) "This god left so many signs <note>original: "tokens"</note> of his kindness <note>original: "beneficence"</note>, and of his presence in so many parts of the world, that it is no wonder everyone thinks that their own town and country has some relation to Bacchus." The belief that Bacchus traveled through the entire world with an army of men and women was a widely spread opinion, though it appears absurd on its very surface. He certainly came from another country when he invaded India; but their history gives no suggestion <note>original: "intimation"</note> of him traveling from there to any other parts of the world. 

This, then, was originally only a myth <note>original: "fable"</note> of the Egyptians, which was necessary to support the stories of their own figures, Osiris and Sesostris. Like other myths of the same kind, it must not be understood literally if we want to make sense of it. The historian <note>Diodorus Siculus</note> may perhaps be of service to us in this case: (2) "Some authors," he says, "held that Bacchus never actually appeared in human form, but that this god was simply a personification of the invention and gift of wine." Let us suppose, then, that wherever the use of wine became common—as it did through almost the entire world—the fame of Bacchus followed. This, and nothing else, can explain the legend of him conquering the world with an army.

***

(1) "In general, because the god left signs of his own kindness and presence in many places of the inhabited world, it is not surprising that each group thinks their own city and country has a certain kinship with Dionysus." <note>original Greek: Καθόλε δ' ἐν πολλοῖς τόποις τῆς οἰκεμένης ἀπολελοιπότος τοῦ θεοῦ σημεῖα τῆς ἰδίας εὐεργεσίας ἅμα καὶ παρεσίας, οὐδὲν παράδοξον ἑκάστους νομίζειν οἰκειότητά τινα γεγονέναι τῷ Διονύσῳ τὴν ἑαυτῶν πόλιν τε καὶ χώραν. Idem. Ibid.</note>
(2) "There are some who claim that no human-shaped birth ever occurred at all, but they believe that the gift of wine is Dionysus." <note>original Greek: Εἰσὶ δ' οἵ γένεσιν μὲν τότε ἀνθρωπόμορφον μὴ γεγονέναι τὸ παράπαν ἀποφαινόμενοι, τὴν δὲ τοῦ οἴνου δόσιν Διόνυσον εἶναι νομίζοντες. Diod. Lib. 3. p. 137.</note>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the origins of Bacchus (Dionysus), moving from the symbolic interpretation of wine to a geographic and historical theory that the "true" Bacchus originated in Scythia or Bactria before invading India.</meta>

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

<margin>
*The Indian Bacchus was likely a Scythian.*
</margin>

Since the Indian Bacchus is acknowledged to be the most ancient, he is consequently the true one; and we must view all others as fictitious and counterfeit. Taking (1) Alexander the Great as our guide, we come to Mount Meros, located not very far from the source of the Indus River, and to the city of Nysa (or Nyssa), which was built by Bacchus. This, then, seems to be the beginning of his expedition, at least as far as the Greeks could determine; but his first departure was likely further north, from nearby Bactria or (2) Southern Scythia—the homeland of the first and greatest conquerors. 

I am more inclined to this opinion because of the women warriors in his army, called by the poets *Bacchae*, *Bacchides*, or Amazons, whom I believe were Scythians: (3) "For in that nation the women, as well as the men, are trained for war, and in strength and courage they are not inferior to men." The great antiquity of the histories of Uranus and Bacchus is another reason for me to think that they both led their "swarms" <note>This refers to large groups of migrating people or invading armies.</note> from the same mother country, which was large and populous enough to send out several massive armies at once. One went conquering eastward while the other went westward; which of the two began his progress first is a question that cannot easily be resolved.

***

(1) See Arrian, *The Campaigns of Alexander*, Book 5; and Quintus Curtius Rufus, *The History of Alexander*, Book 8, chapters 32 and 33. <note>original: Vide Arrian. Exped. Alex. Lib. 5. Q. Curt. De Reb. Gest. Alex. Lib. 8. c. 32, 33.</note>

(2) "Near the Indus river, the Southern Scythians dwell." <note>original Greek: Ἰνδὸν πὰρ ποταμὸν Νότιοι Σκύθαι ἐνναίουσιν.</note> — Dionysius Periegetes, line 1088.

(3) "For among these nations [the Scythians], women are trained for war just like men, and in courage they are in no way inferior to the men." <note>original Greek: Ἐν τούτοις γὰρ ἔθνεσι [Scythis] γυμνάζονται πρὸς πόλεμον γυναῖκες παραπλησίως ἀνδράσι, καὶ ταῖς ἀνδρείαις οὐδὲν λείπονται τῶν ἀνδρῶν.</note> — Diodorus Siculus, Book 2, page 90.

<summary>The author argues that the historical Bacchus originated in Scythia or Bactria rather than India, using the presence of female warriors (Amazons) and Alexander the Great's geographical records as evidence.</summary>

<summary>The author argues that the historical figure behind the myth of Bacchus was likely a Scythian conqueror who invaded India from the north. --- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Bacchus's origins, moving from the theory that he was a Scythian conqueror to examining the specific geographical evidence found in India during Alexander the Great's campaign.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>
<header>The History and Chronology</header>

<margin>*Alexander followed the path of Bacchus.*</margin> From Nysa in India, and from Mount Meros, located to the north of it, begins the history of the true Bacchus. It must have been a commonly accepted opinion that Bacchus (1) built this city when he invaded India. If the Indians believed he was born there, it was due to the deep affection they had for their god; for this was a common weakness <note>original: "foible"</note> among all nations. Arrian (2) says, "The Nyseans were not native Indians, but a colony of Greeks left there by Bacchus." I do not doubt that this was reported and believed by the Greeks. Arrian complied here with the popular traditions of his countrymen concerning their Bacchus, but he seems not to give much credit to them; for he had said in another place (3), "Who this Bacchus was, from where he came, or at what time, I am not able to determine <note>original: "conjecture"</note>;" and shortly after, "We ought not to be too strictly inquisitive into the ancient fables of the gods." Nysa lies between the Cophes River <note>The modern Kabul River.</note> on the west and the Indus on the east. Other rivers, such as the Hydaspes <note>The modern Jhelum River.</note>, Hydraotes <note>The modern Ravi River.</note>, and Acesines <note>The modern Chenab River.</note>, which fall into the Indus, lie even further to the east. Alexander passed all these rivers with his army until he came to the (4) Hypanis <note>The modern Beas River; also referred to by Arrian as the Hyphasis.</note>, or—

***

(1) <note>original: "Διόνυσον ἢ κτίσαι τὴν Νύσσαν, ἀφ᾽ ὅ γε Ἰνδὸς ἐχειρώσατο."</note> "That Dionysus founded Nysa after he had subdued the Indians." Arrian, *The Campaigns of Alexander*, Book 5. <note>original: "Ἐκ δὲ τῶν τοιούτων Νυσαίους δή τινας ἔθνος προσηνόμασαν, καὶ πόλιν παρ᾽ αὐτοῖς Νύσσαν Διονύσου κτίσμα, καὶ ὄρος τὸ ὑπὲρ τῆς πόλεως Μηρόν."</note> "From such accounts they named a certain people Nyseans, and their city Nysa, a foundation of Dionysus; and the mountain above the city Meros." Strabo, Book 15, p. 689.

(2) <note>original: "Νυσαῖοι δὲ οὐκ Ἰνδικὸν γένος ἐστὶν, ἀλλὰ τῶν ἅμα Διονύσῳ ἐλθόντων ἐς τὴν γῆν τῶν Ἰνδῶν."</note> "The Nyseans are not an Indian race, but belong to those who came into the land of the Indians with Dionysus." Arrian, *Indica*.

(3) <note>original: "Ὅστις δὴ οὗτος ὁ Διόνυσος, καὶ ὁπότε ἢ ὅθεν ἐπ᾽ Ἰνδοὺς ἐστράτευσεν, οὐκ ἔχω συμβαλεῖν. — Πλὴν γε δὴ ὅτι οὐκ ἀκριβῆ ἐξεταστὴν χρὴ εἶναι τῶν ὑπὲρ τοῦ θείου ἐκ παλαιοῦ μεμυθευμένων."</note> "Who this Dionysus was, or when or from where he marched against the Indians, I am not able to determine. Except that one ought not to be a too strict examiner of the ancient myths concerning the divine." Arrian, *The Campaigns of Alexander*, Book 5.

(4) <note>original: "Ὅτι ὁ πρόσω τοῦ Ὑφάσιος ἦλθεν Ἀλέξανδρος."</note> "That Alexander went as far as the Hyphasis." Arrian, *Indica*, p. 170.

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

The Hyphasis River <note>The modern Beas River in India.</note>, which was the furthest extent <note>original: "utmost bounds"</note> of his conquests. We may conclude that this was the path of Bacchus; and we may assume that he went even further eastward, based on the passionate outburst of Alexander the Great when his soldiers refused to go any further: (1) "That he would owe them no thanks for what they had accomplished so far, but would consider himself defeated if he were not permitted to cross the Ganges." For, besides other signs, this serves as a kind of proof to me that Bacchus's conquests extended beyond that river. And there I think we must take our leave of him, as it is impossible to follow him into a country that was unknown to ancient European travelers.

<margin>Indian traditions regarding Bacchus.</margin>
In the part of India on this side of the Ganges, we are assured that he was worshiped as a god. The inhabitants, it is said, had a tradition that he was the (2) son of the Indus River; that he was born at Nysa, and was raised in a cave on Mount Meros. When Alexander approached the city of Nysa, the (3) Nyseans sent an embassy to him—

***

(1) <note>original: Τὸ μὲν πρῶτον, ὑπὸ δυθυμίας καὶ ὀργῆς αὑτὸν εἰς τὴν σκηνὴν καθείρξας ἔκειτο, χάριν οὐδεμίαν εἰδὼς τοῖς διαπεπραγμένοις εἰ μὴ περάσειεν τὸν Γάγγην, ἀλλ' ἐξομολόγησιν ἥττης τιθέμενος τὴν ἀναχώρησιν.</note> "At first, out of despondency and anger, Alexander shut himself in his tent and lay there, feeling no gratitude for his past achievements unless he could cross the Ganges; instead, he viewed the retreat as an admission of defeat." Plutarch, *Life of Alexander*. See also Arrian, *Expedition of Alexander*, Book 5. Near the Ganges was a place called the *Sacred Nysean Way*, in memory of Bacchus.

<header>The History and Chronology</header>
<meta>catchword: Hyphasis,</meta>

<margin>*Alexander followed the path of Bacchus.*</margin> From Nysa <note>The OCR reads "Nyfa," using the "long s" (f) common in early printing.</note> in India, and from Mount Meros, located northward from there, begins the history of the true Bacchus. It must have been a commonly held belief that Bacchus (1) built this city when he invaded India. If the Indians believed he was born there, it was due to the affection they had for their god—for this was a common weakness <note>original: "foible"</note> of all nations. Arrian (2) says, "The Nyseans were not native Indians, but a colony of Greeks left there by Bacchus." I have no doubt that this was reported and believed by the Greeks. Arrian here followed the common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> tradition of his countrymen regarding their Bacchus, but he does not seem to give it much credit. In another place, he says, (3) "Who this Bacchus was, where he came from, or at what time, I am unable to guess," and shortly after, "We should not be too strictly inquisitive about the ancient legends of the gods." Nysa lies between the Cophes River <note>Now the Kabul River.</note> on the west and the Indus on the east. Other rivers, such as the Hydaspes, Hydraotes, and Acesines, which flow into the Indus, lie even further to the east. Alexander crossed all these rivers with his army until he reached the (4) Hypanis, or

---

<margin>(1) Bacchus founded Nysa after he had subdued the Indians. <note>original: Διόνυσον δὲ κτίσαι τὴν Νύσαν, ἀφ’ οὗ τε Ἰνδὸς ἐχειρώσατο.</note> Arrian, *Expedition of Alexander*, Book 5. From such accounts, they called a certain people Nyseans, and their city Nysa, a creation of Dionysus, and the mountain above the city Meros. <note>original: Ἐκ δὲ τῶν τοιούτων Νυσαίους δή τινας ἔθνος προσηνόμασαν, καὶ πόλιν παρ’ αὐτοῖς Νύσαν Διονύσου κτίσμα, καὶ ὄρος τὸ ὑπὲρ τῆς πόλεως Μηρόν.</note> Strabo, Book 13, p. 689.</margin>
<margin>(2) The Nyseans are not an Indian race, but are among those who came with Dionysus into the land of the Indians. <note>original: Νυσαῖοι δὲ οὐκ Ἰνδικὸν γένος ἐστὶν, ἀλλὰ τῶν ἅμα Διονύσῳ ἐλθόντων ἐς τὴν γῆν τῶν Ἰνδῶν.</note> Arrian, *Indica*.</margin>
<margin>(3) Who this Dionysus was, and when or from where he marched against the Indians, I cannot determine. — Except that one should not be a strict critic of what has been mythologized from ancient times regarding the divine. <note>original: Ὅστις δὴ οὗτος ὁ Διόνυσος, καὶ ὁπότε ἢ ὅθεν ἐπ’ Ἰνδοὺς ἐστράτευσεν, οὐκ ἔχω συμβαλεῖν. — Πλὴν γε δὴ ὅτι οὐκ ἀκριβῆ ἐξεταστὴν χρὴ εἶναι τῶν ὑπὲρ τοῦ θείου ἐκ παλαιοῦ μεμυθευμένων.</note> Arrian, *Expedition of Alexander*, Book 5.</margin>

praying that he would continue to grant them their ancient liberties, laws, and customs received from Bacchus, which he confirmed. Other nations, such as the (1) Oxydracae <note>An ancient tribe in the Punjab region of India/Pakistan known for resisting Alexander the Great; they claimed to be descendants of the god Dionysus/Bacchus.</note> in particular, boasted that they were descended from him. And in general it is said, that (2) "The Indians show to this day the place where he was born; that many cities were called by his name in their own language; and that there are many other remarkable tokens of his being born in India, which it would be tedious to mention." Megasthenes <note>A Greek historian and diplomat (c. 350–290 BC) who lived at the court of the Indian Emperor Chandragupta Maurya and wrote the "Indica," a primary source for ancient Indian history.</note>, who lived some time after Alexander and under Seleucus, went to the court of Sandrocottus <note>The Greek name for Chandragupta Maurya, founder of the Mauryan Empire.</note>, a powerful king of India on this side of the Ganges, and to that of Porus <note>A regional king who famously fought Alexander at the Battle of the Hydaspes.</note>, a much greater one. From Sandrocottus he learned the previously mentioned long succession of Indian kings and says that, (3) "When Bacchus left India, he appointed Spartembas, one of his friends, to succeed him, who reigned fifty-two years; [which I must observe is the very term of Bacchus's reign]. Budyas his son succeeded him, reigned twenty years, and left the kingdom to his son Cradevas; and so the government descended for many ages from father to son." This succession probably came in a direct line from the true Bacchus; but his leaving India must be considered fabulous <note>original: "fabulous"; meaning legendary or mythical rather than historically factual.</note>—

---

(1) "The Oxydracae are descendants of Dionysus." <note>original: Διονύσου δ' ἀπογόνους τοὺς Ὀξυδράκας.</note> Strabo, Book 15, p. 687. "And the Oxydracae, whom we said were mythically related to Dionysus." <note>original: Ὀξυδράκαι δὲ, οὓς τοῦ Διονύσου συγγενεῖς ἔφαμεν μεμυθεῦσθαι.</note> Ibid. p. 701.

(2) "The place where the god was born is shown among the Indians to this day, as well as the names of cities given by him in the local dialect, and many other memorable proofs of his birth in India, about which it would take a long time to write." <note>original: Δείκνυσθαι δ' παρ' Ἰνδοῖς μέχρι τοῦ νῦν τὸν τε τόπον, ἐν ᾧ συνέβη γενέσθαι τὸν θεὸν, καὶ προσηγορίας πόλεων ὑπ' αὐτοῦ κατὰ τὴν τῶν ἐγχωρίων διάλεκτον· καὶ πολλὰ ἕτερα ἀξιομνημόνευτα τεκμήρια τῆς παρ' Ἰνδῶν γενέσεως, περὶ ὧν μακρὸν ἂν εἴη γράφειν.</note> Diodorus Siculus, Book 3, p. 139.

(3) "When he departed from the land of the Indians, after he had organized these matters, he appointed Spartembas, one of his companions and most devoted to the rites of Bacchus, as king of the country. The kingship then passed to his son, Cradevas." <note>original: Ἀπιόντα δὲ ἐκ τῆς Ἰνδῶν γῆς, ὡς οἱ ταῦτα κεκοσμέατο, καταστῆσαι βασιλέα τῆς χώρης Σπαρτέμβαν τῶν ἑταίρων ἕνα βακχωδέστατον· τελευτήσαντος δὲ Σπαρτέμβα, τὴν βασιληίην ἐκδέξασθαι Βυδύαν τὸν τούτου παῖδα· καὶ τὸν μὲν πεντήκοντα καὶ δύο βασιλεῦσαι τὰ πατέρᾱ· τὸν δὲ παῖδα εἴκοσιν ἔτεα· καὶ τούτου παῖδα ἐκδέξασθαι τὴν βασιληίην Κραδεύαν.</note> Arrian, *Indica*.

...fabulous <note>original: "lous"; continuing "fabulous" from the previous page</note>; as it in no way agrees with the authentic account of the Indians.

<margin>
*The actions of the true Bacchus <note>Dionysus, the Greek god of wine and ecstasy, whom the Greeks believed had conquered India in ancient times.</note> are unknown to the Greeks.*
</margin>
We must be content to remain ignorant of the majority of Bacchus's actions, because we do not know for certain where he originally came from. Furthermore, his exploits were performed in a country (1) unknown to the Greeks until the time of Alexander the Great, and even then their knowledge was very imperfect, barely reaching beyond the Indus River and the smaller rivers that flow into it. Strabo <note>A Greek geographer and historian (c. 64 BC–24 AD).</note> says, (2) "We ought to make allowances for what is reported about India because it is a country so far distant, and few of our people have seen it. Those who have seen it have only seen certain parts of it, and they speak mostly from hearsay. What they did see, they saw only in passing; for this reason, they disagree when reporting the same things, even though they seem to present them after careful inquiry. Some of these men were soldiers on the same expedition, such as those who assisted Alexander..."

---

(1) <note>original Greek: Εἰ τοίνυν ταῦτ' ἀφείς τις...</note> "If, therefore, one disregards these things and looks at the accounts of Alexander's expedition, one would find them even more obscure than these." — Strabo, Book 15, p. 686. "The regions to the south, including Pattala and the mouths of the Indus, were seen by Alexander, the Macedonians, and many Greeks. But Alexander did not visit the regions to the east, those beyond the Hyphasis river. Only a few have written about the areas as far as the Ganges, where the mouth of the Ganges is located and where Palibothra, the largest city of the Indians, sits by the river." — Arrian, *Indica*.

(2) <note>original Greek: Δεῖ δ' εὐγνωμόνως ἀκούειν...</note> "One must listen with indulgence to reports about India, for it is very far away, and few of our people have observed it closely. Even those who have seen it have only seen parts, and they report most things from hearsay. Even what they saw, they learned only in passing; therefore, they do not even report the same things about the same subjects, even when they write as if they have investigated carefully. Some of them were even on the same campaigns and traveled together, like those who helped Alexander conquer Asia; yet they often contradict one another. If they disagree so much about things they actually saw, what should we think of their reports based on hearsay?" — Strabo, Book 15, p. 685.

<summary>This page argues that the Greek accounts of Bacchus's (Dionysus's) exploits in India are largely unreliable due to the extreme distance of the region and the contradictory, hearsay-based reports provided by Alexander the Great's soldiers.</summary>

<header>Of the Fabulous Ages.</header>
<sig>I</sig>
<meta>catchword: in</meta>

...fabulous <note>original: "lous"; continuing "fabulous" from the previous page. Here, "fabulous" refers to the era of myths and fables rather than factual history.</note>; as it in no way agrees with the genuine account of the Indians.

<margin>
*The actions of the true Bacchus <note>Dionysus, whom the Greeks associated with an ancient conquest of India.</note> are unknown to the Greeks.*
</margin>
We must be content to remain ignorant of the majority of Bacchus's actions, because we do not know for certain where he originally came from. Furthermore, his exploits were performed in a country (1) unknown to the Greeks until the time of Alexander the Great, and even then, their knowledge was very imperfect. Their understanding of it barely reached beyond the Indus River and the smaller rivers that flow into it. Strabo <note>A Greek geographer and historian (c. 64 BC – 24 AD) known for his work "Geographica."</note> says, (2) "We ought to make allowances for what is related about India, because it is a country so far distant, and few of our people have seen it. Those who have seen it have only seen some parts of it, and they speak mostly from hearsay. What they did see, they saw only in passing; for this reason, they disagree when reporting the same things, even though they seem to present them after mature inquiry. Some of these men were soldiers on the same expedition, such as those who assisted Alexander..."

---

<margin>(1) <note>original Greek from Strabo, Lib. 15, p. 686: "Εἰ τοίνυν ταῦτ᾽ ἀφείς τις..."</note> "If, therefore, one disregards these things and looks at the accounts of Alexander's expedition, one would find them even more obscure than these." — Strabo, Book 15, p. 686. <note>original Greek from Arrian, Indic.: "Τὰ μὲν πρὸς μεσημβρίαν..."</note> "The regions to the south, including Pattala and the mouths of the Indus, were seen by Alexander and the Macedonians, as well as by many Greeks. But Alexander did not visit the regions to the east, those beyond the Hyphasis River. Only a few have written about the regions as far as the Ganges, where the mouths of the Ganges and the city of Palibothra <note>Now identified as Patna, the capital of the Maurya Empire.</note>, the largest city of the Indians on the Ganges, are located." — Arrian, *Indica*.</margin>

<margin>(2) <note>original Greek from Strabo, Lib. 15, p. 685: "Δεῖ δ' εὐγνωμόνως ἀκούειν..."</note> "We must receive the accounts of India with indulgence, for it is very far away, and few of our people have sailed there. Even those who have seen it saw only parts of it, and most of what they say is from hearsay. What they did see, they learned only in passing. Therefore, they do not even report the same things about the same subjects, even though they have written them down as if they were carefully examined. Furthermore, even those who campaigned together and traveled together, like those who helped Alexander conquer Asia, often contradict each other. And if they differ so much about what they saw, what must we think about what they heard from others?" — Strabo, Book 15, p. 685.</margin>

<summary>This page discusses the historical unreliability of Greek accounts regarding Bacchus's exploits in India, citing the geographer Strabo to explain that distance and limited exploration led to conflicting reports.</summary>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>Of the Fabulous Ages.</header>
<sig>I</sig>
<meta>catchword: in</meta>

fabulous <note>original: "lous"; the word "fabulous" began on the previous page. In this context, it refers to the "Age of Fables" or mythology rather than something wonderful.</note>; as it in no way agrees with the genuine account of the Indians.

<margin>*The actions of the true Bacchus <note>The Greeks identified their god Dionysus with an ancient conqueror of India they called Bacchus.</note> are unknown to the Greeks.*</margin>

We must be content to remain ignorant of the greatest part of Bacchus's actions; because we do not know for certain where he came from originally; and his exploits were performed in a country (1) unknown to the Greeks until the time of Alexander the Great, and even then but very imperfectly. Their knowledge of it scarcely reached beyond the Indus River and those rivers that flow into it. Strabo <note>A Greek geographer and historian, c. 64 BC–24 AD.</note> says, (2) "We ought to make allowances for what is related of the Indies, because it is a country so far distant; and few of our people have seen it; and those who have seen it, have only seen some parts of it; and they speak mostly upon hearsay; and what they saw, they saw only in a passing view; for which reason they disagree in relating the same things, though they seem to deliver them upon mature inquiry. Some of these were soldiers in the same expedition, such as those who assisted Alexander in his conquest of Asia; but they often contradict each other. Since they differ so much regarding things they actually saw, what should we think of their reports based on hearsay?"

---

<margin>(1)</margin> <note>original Greek: Εἰ τοίνυν ταῦτ' ἀφείς τις... (Strabo. Lib. 15. p. 686 / Arrian. Indic.)</note> "If, therefore, one disregards these things and examines the records of Alexander’s expedition, he would find them much more obscure. The regions toward the south, and Patala, and the mouths of the Indus, were seen by Alexander and the Macedonians, as well as many Greeks. However, Alexander did not reach the regions to the east, going only as far as the Hyphasis River. Only a few people have written about the regions as far as the Ganges River, where the mouths of the Ganges are located, and the city of Palibothra <note>Modern-day Patna, India.</note>, the greatest city of the Indians on the Ganges." — Strabo, Book 15; Arrian, *Indica*.

<margin>(2)</margin> <note>original Greek: Δεῖ δ' εὐγνωμόνως ἀκούειν... (Strabo. Lib. 15. p. 685)</note> "We must listen with indulgence to accounts regarding India; for it is extremely far away, and not many of our people have sailed there. Those who have seen it saw only parts of it, and for the most part, they speak from hearsay. Even what they saw, they learned only in passing; therefore they do not report the same things about the same subjects, even though they wrote them as if they had been carefully examined. Some of these men were even on the same campaigns together, such as those who helped Alexander conquer Asia; yet they often say the opposite of one another. Where they differ so much about what was actually seen, what must we think of what they report from hearsay?" — Strabo, Book 15, p. 685.

<summary>This page discusses the historical unreliability of Greek accounts concerning Bacchus’s exploits in India. Using the testimony of the geographer Strabo, the author explains that because India was so distant and the Greeks' travels were so limited, even eyewitness accounts from Alexander the Great’s soldiers are often contradictory and based on hearsay.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of ancient Greek historians and their accounts of India. It focuses on the testimony of Megasthenes, a Greek ethnographer who traveled to India, as cited by the geographer Strabo, to argue that most stories of ancient conquests of India (like those of Bacchus or Semiramis) are legendary rather than historical.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>The History and Chronology</header>

"... in the conquest of Asia; and yet they frequently contradict each other. Now, if they differ so much about things which they witnessed firsthand, what must we think of those things which they relate only through hearsay?"

<margin>Mythological stories <note>original: "Fabulous stories"</note> of Bacchus should not be blamed on the Indians.</margin>
Megasthenes (1) "tells us not to believe the ancient stories about the Indians; for the Indians never sent an army out of their country, nor did any foreign army ever enter and conquer India, except for the armies of Hercules and Bacchus, and most recently, the Macedonians <note>The army of Alexander the Great.</note>." Regarding the armies that supposedly entered India, I suppose he meant those under Semiramis <note>A legendary queen of Assyria.</note> and Cyrus <note>Cyrus the Great, founder of the Achaemenid Persian Empire.</note>, both of whose accounts (2) Strabo treats as legendary <note>original: "fabulous".</note>. Regarding the armies that supposedly left India, I think he must be referring to the story of Bacchus traveling across most of the known world with an Indian army. However, he could not have meant to blame the Indians themselves for these reports. They no doubt boasted of many great deeds performed by Bacchus in their own country, and they might have added many more that never actually happened. However, they are not responsible for any external myths. Megasthenes's criticism, then, applies instead to the Arabian, Egyptian, and Greek poets and historians. It was only fair of them—

---

(1) "Megasthenes also seems to agree with this view, urging us to distrust the ancient stories about the Indians; for he says that no army was ever sent out by the Indians, nor did any army from outside ever enter and conquer them, except for the expeditions of Hercules and Dionysus, and the recent one by the Macedonians." <note>original: Συμφαποφαίνεται δέ πως καὶ Μεγαθένης τῷ λόγῳ τούτῳ... Strabo, Book 15, p. 686.</note>

(2) "And what just grounds for belief could we have concerning Indian affairs from such an expedition as that of Cyrus or of Semiramis?" <note>original: Ἡμῖν δὲ τίς ἂν δικαία γένοιτο πίϛις... Strabo, Book 15.</note>

<summary>This page argues that ancient accounts of foreign conquests of India, specifically those by Bacchus, Hercules, Cyrus, and Semiramis, are largely unreliable myths invented by Western poets rather than historical Indian records. It uses the testimony of Megasthenes and Strabo to contrast these legends with the historically verified invasion of Alexander the Great.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the legends surrounding Bacchus (Dionysus) and his global conquests were likely inventions of Western writers—such as Greeks, Arabians, and Egyptians—rather than genuine Indian historical traditions.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>

### Of the Legendary Ages <note>original: "Fabulous Ages," meaning the era of myths and fables.</note>

...when they had led their heroes as far as the Indies, only to bring them back again in triumph to their respective countries. But this was not the case with the Indians; for they could never have dreamed of Bacchus leading an army into (1) Libya; of his conquering (2) Lycurgus, king of Thrace; or of his (3) returning to Thebes mounted on an elephant.

<margin>Uranus and Bacchus: the two oldest deities.</margin>

It is clear from history that the Arabians had a chief deity who was understood to be Dionysus, or Bacchus. However, there are several reasons to think that this was not the true Bacchus, if he was indeed a native of Arabia. First, there is the general persuasion of the Indians that he was born at (4) Nysa in their country, which lies at too great a distance to be mistaken for the Arabian Nysa. Again, it is not probable that Bacchus would leave India—a fertile country large enough to find employment for more than one conqueror for their entire lives—to make incursions into the deserts of Arabia. To these points, I must add my own private opinion,

---

(1) Regarding the Libyan Bacchus, son of Ammon, and his wars with Saturn, and his other exploits in Libya, we shall give a brief account hereafter.

(2) Dionysus, having transported his army, defeated the Thracians in battle; he took Lycurgus alive, blinded him, and after inflicting every kind of torture, crucified him. <note>original Greek: Τὸν δὲ Διόνυσον περαιώσαντα τὴν δύναμιν μάχῃ κρατῆσαι τῶν Θρᾳκῶν, καὶ τὸν Λυκοῦργον ζωγρήσαντα τυφλῶσαί τε καὶ πᾶσαν αἰκίαν εἰσενεγκάμενον ἀνασταυρῶσαι.</note> Diodorus Siculus, Book 3, page 139. But he says just after that some of the poets, including Antimachus, thought Lycurgus was not king of Thrace, but of Arabia. Some of the poets, among whom is Antimachus, declare that Lycurgus was not the king of Thrace, but of Arabia. <note>original Greek: Τῶν δὲ ποιητῶν τινὲς, ὧν ἐστὶ καὶ Ἀντίμαχος ἀποφαίνονται τὸν Λυκοῦργον οὐ Θρᾴκης, ἀλλὰ τῆς Ἀραβίας γεγονέναι βασιλέα.</note> Ibid. <note>Latin: "In the same place."</note>

(3) They say that Dionysus, having punished the ungodly but having behaved kindly toward other men, made his return from India to Thebes mounted on an elephant. <note>original Greek: Τὸν δ' οὖν Διόνυσον φασὶ κολάσαντα μὲν τοὺς ἀσεβεῖς ἐπιεικῶς δὲ προσενεχθέντα τοῖς ἄλλοις ἀνθρώποις, ἐκ τῆς Ἰνδικῆς ἐπ' ἐλέφαντος τὴν εἰς Θήβας ἐπάνοδον ποιήσασθαι.</note> Ibid.

(4) For Dionysus is called "Nysian" after the city of Nysa in India, both by the Indians and by all the nations toward the East <note>literally "toward the sun's rays"</note>. <note>original Greek: Νύσιος γὰρ ὁ Διόνυσος, ἀπὸ τῆς ἐν Ἰνδοῖς Νύσης, Ἰνδοῖς ὀνομάζεται, καὶ πᾶσι τοῖς πρὸς ἀκτῖνα ἔθνεσιν.</note> Philostratus, *Life of Apollonius*, Book 2, chapter 2.

<summary>The author argues that the mythological tales of Bacchus conquering distant lands like Thrace and Libya are Western inventions, as the Indians themselves claimed Bacchus was born in India at Nysa and would have had no reason to leave such a fertile land for the deserts of Arabia.</summary>

<page-num>68</page-num>

# The History and Chronology

...that Bacchus flourished before the Arabians could be considered a nation. Ishmael, who was destined to be the father of a great nation, is the person from whom they claim their descent; yet I place Bacchus before Abraham, the father of Ishmael. Strabo also suggests that the name of the country, (1) Arabia, was unknown during the "era of the gods." The ancient Arabians kept no records of time—at least, none have survived to us. They only preserved the general reputation of the god, which they might have initially received from the Indians. I believe we may conclude that their version of Bacchus could not be older than the period when they first began to worship him.

For (2) Herodotus says, "Men calculated the genealogies of their gods from the time they first became known to them." This specific time cannot now be discovered; however, they seem to have been acquainted with him before the Egyptians were. The Egyptians could trace their history no further back into antiquity than to their god Osiris, whose actions are a mixture of what had been previously performed by Uranus and Bacchus, as an observant reader may see by examining his history. His eastern conquests are derived from Bacchus, and his western ones from Uranus. His wife and sister, Isis, seems to be nothing more than a second Titæa, <note>Titæa: In mythology, the mother of the Titans; the author suggests Isis is a reimagining of this older figure.</note>

---

(1) Strabo says it might have been named after a certain Arabaeus, the son of Hermes (or Mercury), according to the poet Stesichorus; however, he notes that this name could not be as old as the Heroic times or the Mythic age.

> "And the noble Arabaeus, whom the gracious Hermes fathered." <note>original Greek: Καὶ κόρδω Ἀραβαίο ἳ Ἑρμάων ἀκάκηλα Γείνατο.</note>

"So says Stesichorus: One may conjecture that the country was already named Arabia after him at that time, though perhaps not yet in the Heroic Age." <note>original Greek: Οὕτω δὲ καὶ Στησίχορος λέγει· Εἰκάζειν ἔν ἔστιν, ὅτι ἀπὸ τούτου καὶ ἡ χώρα Ἀραβία ἤδη τότε ὀνομάζετο, κατὰ δὲ τοὺς Ἥρωας τυχὸν ἴσως ἔπω.</note> — Strabo, Book 1, page 42.

(2) "From the time they learned of them, from that point they trace the genealogy of their birth." <note>original Greek: Ἀπ' οὗ δὲ ἐπύθοντο χρόνου, ἀπὸ τούτου γενεηλογέουσι αὐτέων γένεσιν.</note> — Herodotus, Book 2, chapter 146.

<summary>The author argues that the figure of Bacchus predates Arabian and Egyptian national histories, suggesting that the Egyptian god Osiris is actually a composite character based on the earlier legends of Uranus and Bacchus.</summary>

<page-num>69</page-num>

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

<margin>The Egyptians' claims regarding Bacchus.</margin>

Titea, or Terra <note>Terra: The Roman personification of the Earth, equivalent to the Greek Gaia.</note>, under a different name; and the murder of Osiris is simply that of Uranus, with the details slightly expanded. The Phoenician <note>original: "Phenician"</note> history, or that of the translator Philo <note>Philo of Byblos, a scholar who claimed to translate the works of the ancient Phoenician historian Sanchuniathon.</note>, provides more specific details about the death of Uranus, but does not mention Bacchus at all. Some might consider this a reason to date Bacchus later than the time of Uranus; however, it only proves that his worship was not known in Phoenicia as early as that of the other. Both seem to me to have lived around the same time, which I have placed approximately 2,300 years or more before the Christian Era <note>original: "Æra"</note>. If I am mistaken, I am ready to yield to better information.

We do not know what the Arabians alleged regarding Bacchus’s birth in Arabia Felix <note>Arabia Felix: Latin for "Happy Arabia," referring to the fertile southern region of the Arabian Peninsula, modern-day Yemen.</note>, nor indeed if they claimed any such thing. If they did, the Egyptians' claim to him is clearly founded upon theirs, as is apparent from the history of Osiris. It is (1) said:

> "That he marched by the Red Sea through Arabia as far as the Indies <note>India</note>, the very edge of the world. He built many cities in the Indies, including one he named Nysa <note>original: "Nyfa". Nysa was the traditional birthplace of Bacchus/Dionysus in Greek myth.</note>, wishing to leave a memorial of the place near Egypt where he was raised. He also planted ivy [which is sacred to Bacchus] in Nysa..."

---

(1) <note>original: Ἔπειτα ποιήσασθαι τὴν πορείαν δι' Ἀραβίας παρὰ τὴν Ἐρυθρὰν Θάλασσαν... (Diodorus Siculus, Library of History, Book I, p. 12)</note> "Then he made a journey through Arabia along the Red Sea, as far as the Indians and the edge of the inhabited world. He founded many cities among the Indians, one of which he named Nysa, wishing to leave a memorial of that Nysa in Egypt where he was raised. He also planted ivy in the Nysa among the Indians, and this plant remains in that place alone of all the regions of India and the neighboring country. He also left behind many other signs of his presence in that land, which led later generations of Indians to claim the god as their own, saying that he was of Indian descent." — **Diodorus Siculus, Book 1, p. 12.**

<summary>The author argues that the myths of Osiris, Uranus, and Bacchus are interconnected and likely date back to around 2300 BCE, suggesting that Egyptian claims to Bacchus were adapted from Arabian or Indian traditions.</summary>

Diodorus Siculus, Library of History, Book I, p. 12.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the myths of Bacchus, Osiris, and Uranus are chronological parallels dating to roughly 2300 BCE. It highlights the legend of Osiris/Bacchus's military and cultural expedition to India as recorded by the historian Diodorus Siculus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's analysis of the origins of the god Bacchus (Dionysus), specifically debating whether he was an Egyptian, an Indian, or—as the author argues—a Scythian conqueror.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>

# The History and Chronology

...of the Indies <note>India</note>, which continued to grow only in that region and the nearby areas. He left many other signs of his presence in that country. Because of this, later Indians became involved in a dispute about that god, claiming that he was an Indian by birth." The story is told quite convincingly and seems truthful. However, even based on this account, I believe the reader will anticipate my judgment: I would side with the Indians rather than the Egyptians. Though ultimately, I cannot accept either of their claims <note>original: "pretensions"</note>, as I still believe he was a Scythian <note>Scythian: An ancient nomadic people from the Eurasian steppes, often described as powerful conquerors in early histories.</note> conqueror. It would be trivial to mention the fleets and naval victories of Osiris <note>Osiris: The Egyptian god of the afterlife, often identified with Bacchus/Dionysus by ancient scholars.</note> and Sesostris <note>Sesostris: A legendary king of ancient Egypt said to have led massive military expeditions across the world.</note> as if they were related to Bacchus, because we are told (1) that Bacchus's invasion was carried out by land, not by sea.

<margin>The claims of the Greeks.</margin>
The Greeks must have been obsessed with myths <note>original: "infatuated with the love of fable"</note> to establish a Bacchus in their own country, especially since they already had the use of writing <note>original: "letters"</note> and could be so easily proven to have forged the story. They claimed with just as much confidence as the Egyptians (though less convincingly) that the Indian Bacchus was originally from Greece. However, they do not explain how he reached India, nor which nations he conquered on the way there. Whatever deeds he performed in Greece might be their own made-up stories, but the accounts of foreign conquests must have been borrowed from...

---

(1) "Before that time, horses had never been seen on ships in the land of the Indians; for indeed, it was not recorded that Dionysus's expedition against the Indians was a naval one." <note>original Greek: Οὐ πρόσθεν ἵπποι ἐπὶ νεῶν ὀφθέντες ἐν τῇ Ἰνδῶν γῇ· καὶ γὰρ καὶ τὸν Διονύσῳ ἐπ' Ἰνδοὺς στόλον οὐκ ἐμέμνητο γενέσθαι ναυτικόν.</note> Arrian, *The Anabasis of Alexander*, Book 6, page 124, Stephanus Edition. <note>Arrian: A 2nd-century Greek historian who wrote the definitive account of Alexander the Great's campaigns.</note>

<summary>The author weighs the competing claims of the Indians, Egyptians, and Greeks regarding the origin of Bacchus, ultimately arguing that Bacchus was likely a Scythian conqueror who invaded India by land.</summary>
<keywords>Bacchus, Dionysus, India, Egypt, Scythia, Osiris, Sesostris, Arrian, mythology, ancient history</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the Greek version of Bacchus (Dionysus) was a later adaptation of much older Egyptian and Scythian traditions, critiquing the accounts of ancient historians like Philostratus.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>Of the Fabulous Ages.</header>

from the Egyptians. Philostratus says, (1) "The Greeks differed from the Indians, and the Indians from one another, about this god; for we," he says, "affirm that he was a Theban who conquered India." For the truth of this, he appeals to a disc of Indian silver, preserved as an offering at Delphi. And yet he seems to provide an argument against his own claim when describing the temple of Bacchus at Nysa, where he says, (2) "There was a statue of the god remaining of white marble, in the form of an Indian youth."

But this entire account by Philostratus is a poorly judged fiction, not based on the slightest authority. The Greeks could have had no knowledge of India except for what they received from the Egyptians. Osiris, who was himself a counterfeit of the true Bacchus <note>The author believes the "true" Bacchus was an ancient Scythian king, and that the Egyptian Osiris and Greek Dionysus were later mythological versions of him.</note>, was the pattern from which they copied. In order to conform to this pattern, they were forced to (3) transport the son of Semele <note>Semele: In Greek mythology, the mortal mother of Dionysus.</note> from Thebes to Nysa in Arabia before he could be considered a true Bacchus. It would be an endless and useless task to follow this god through all the myths of the Greeks. I shall only observe that after traveling across Europe, Asia, and Libya, they finally brought him to his grave and (4) buried him at Delphi, where his rites and mysteries...

> "...of the Indies, which continued to grow in that part 
> only and the adjacent places. He also left many 
> other signs of having been in that country. Because of 
> this, the later Indians were drawn into a controversy 
> about that god, claiming that he was an Indian by

The story is told very convincingly and with a seeming air of truth. However, even from this state of the case, I believe the reader will anticipate my verdict and award the case to the Indians rather than the Egyptians. Yet, after all, I can accept neither of their claims, as I still believe that he was a Scythian conqueror <note>The Scythians were ancient nomadic people from the Eurasian steppes; the author argues the "real" Bacchus was one of their kings.</note>. It would be trivial to mention the fleets and naval victories of Osiris and Sesostris <note>Osiris was the Egyptian god of the afterlife; Sesostris was a legendary king of Egypt said to have conquered the world.</note> as though they related to Bacchus in any way, because we are (1) told that Bacchus's invasion was not made by sea, but by land.

<margin>
The claims of the Greeks.
</margin>
The Greeks must have been infatuated with a love of myths to establish a Bacchus in their own country—especially after they had gained the use of the alphabet and could be so easily proven to have forged the story. They asserted with as much confidence (though not with as much elegance) as the Egyptians that the Indian Bacchus was a native of Greece. However, they do not tell us how he reached India, nor what nations he conquered on his way there. Whatever actions he performed in Greece might be their own fables, but the stories of foreign conquests must have been borrowed...
