# Of the Fabulous Ages

The Greeks learned about this figure from the Egyptians. Philostratus says, (1) "The Greeks differed from the Indians, and the Indians differed from one another, regarding this god; for we," he says, "affirm that he was a Theban who conquered India." To prove the truth of this, he points to a silver Indian disc preserved as an offering at Delphi. 

Yet he seems to offer an argument against his own claim when describing the temple of Bacchus at Nysa: he says, (2) "There was a remaining statue of the god made of white marble, in the form of an Indian youth." 

But this entire account by Philostratus is a poorly judged fiction, not based on the slightest authority. The Greeks could have had no knowledge of India except what they received from the Egyptians. Osiris—who was himself a mythical imitation of the true Bacchus <note>The author believes the "real" Bacchus was a historical Scythian king whose identity was later obscured by myths in Egypt (as Osiris) and Greece (as Dionysus).</note>—was the pattern from which they copied. To match this model, the Greeks were forced to (3) relocate the son of Semele <note>Semele was a princess of Thebes and the mortal mother of Bacchus (Dionysus) in Greek mythology.</note> from Thebes to Nysa in Arabia <note>Nysa was a legendary mountain or region where Bacchus was said to have been raised by nymphs.</note> before he could be considered a "true" Bacchus. 

It would be an endless and pointless task to follow this god through all the myths of the Greeks. I will only observe that after he traveled through Europe, Asia, and Libya, they finally brought him to his grave and (4) buried him at Delphi; it was there that his religious rites and mysteries...

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of how the Greeks adopted and rebranded foreign deities, specifically focusing on the transition of the Egyptian Osiris into the Greek Bacchus through the influence of Orpheus.</meta>

"...[set] down the birth of the ancient Osiris at a much later time; and to gain favor with the Thebans, he established new religious rites. In these, the initiates were taught that Bacchus was the son of Jupiter and Semele. The people, being deceived in this way—partly through their own ignorance and partly because of the high regard they had for Orpheus’s expertise in religious matters, but mostly because they very much wanted this god to be considered a Greek—readily accepted his secret mysteries."

Though I strongly doubt whether Orpheus was ever actually in Egypt or initiated into the mysteries of Osiris, I can easily accept the Egyptians' accusation against the Greeks: that their Bacchus was nothing more than Osiris. 

However, if the Egyptians had been asked from where they received Osiris, I believe they would have had to answer, "from Arabia." And if the same question had been asked of the Arabians, they would have said, "from India." In short, the practice of nations stealing gods from one another was so common that it was never considered a crime, but rather something praiseworthy <note>original: "meritorious"</note>.

By my calculations, the time between Cadmus <note>The legendary Phoenician prince who founded Thebes and introduced the alphabet to Greece.</note> and the Trojan War is a period of about 400 years. The deification <note>The act of treating or worshipping a person as a god.</note> of Bacchus can be placed within this period, perhaps around the middle of it. The dates for the elder Orpheus are uncertain, but he must belong to this same period. I also believe the story of Jupiter and Semele must have already become an established legend among the Greeks before Orpheus could impose <note>original: "obtrude"</note> his new religious rites upon them.

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

<margin>Thymœtes' account of the Libyan Bacchus.</margin>
Thymœtes, son of Thymœtes, was another ancient poet said to be (1) a contemporary of Orpheus; he also composed a poem about the elder Bacchus in the Pelasgic <note>The Pelasgians were the indigenous people of Greece before the arrival of the Hellenes; "Pelasgic letters" refers to an early form of the Greek alphabet.</note> dialect and alphabet <note>original: "letters"</note>. This seems to have been written before the Greeks thought of a Bacchus of their own, or at least while they were preparing the way for one. The substance of this poem was included by Dionysius of Miletus <note>Dionysius Milesius, a Greek historian and mythographer</note> in his history and seems designed to undermine the credibility of the Egyptian Bacchus by inventing another, more ancient one in Libya. Thymœtes was careful to set the scene far away for his own security, but he blended it with the oldest Greek mythology. Since it provides an example of the endless recycling <note>original: "circulation"</note> of fables, I shall summarize most of the details preserved in prose by Diodorus <note>Diodorus Siculus, a historian whose work "Bibliotheca historica" is a major source for ancient myths</note>, as the full work is too long for this space.

<margin>The Libyan Bacchus, son of Ammon and Amalthea.</margin>

# The History and Chronology

"...dated the birth of the ancient Osiris to a much later time. To win favor with the people of Thebes <note>original: "Thebans"</note>, he established new religious rites, in which the initiates were taught that Bacchus was the son of Jupiter and Semele. The people were easily deceived, partly because of their ignorance and partly because of the high respect they had for Orpheus’s expertise in religious <note>original: "sacred"</note> matters. However, they mainly accepted his secret rituals <note>original: "mysteries"</note> because they very much wanted this god to be considered a Greek."

Although I strongly doubt that Orpheus was ever in Egypt or initiated into the secret rites of Osiris, I can easily accept the Egyptians' accusation against the Greeks: that their Bacchus was nothing more than Osiris renamed. But if the Egyptians had been asked where they got Osiris from, I believe they would have had to answer "Arabia"; and if the same question had been asked of the Arabians, they would have said "India." In short, the practice of nations stealing gods from one another was so common that it was never considered a crime, but rather a praiseworthy achievement.

The time between Cadmus <note>A legendary Phoenician prince credited with bringing the alphabet to the Greeks.</note> and the Trojan War, by my calculation, is a period of about 400 years. The deification of Bacchus can be placed within this period, perhaps around the middle of it. The timeframe for the elder Orpheus is uncertain, but it must fall within this same period. I believe the story of Jupiter and Semele must have already become an established legend among the Greeks before Orpheus could impose <note>original: "obtrude"</note> his new religious rites upon them.

# The History and Chronology

> "...afterwards taking Saturn and Rhea prisoners, he pardoned them; and Rhea loved him ever after as her own son; but Saturn's friendship was not to be trusted. About this time Saturn and Rhea had a son born to them, named Jupiter, on whom Bacchus conferred great honors. He then built a temple to his father Ammon, and set up an oracle. He conquered Egypt, and made Jupiter king of it, though he was still only a youth. He taught the Egyptians the use of wine, and in the barren countries where vines would not grow, he taught them to make a (1) drink of barley <note>This refers to beer, an ancient staple of the Egyptian diet.</note>, little inferior to wine in strength, taste, or flavor. He is likewise said to have returned by hasty marches from India to the Mediterranean, and found the Titans passing over into Crete against Ammon. Jupiter raised <note>original: "levied"</note> forces in Egypt and brought them to the assistance of Ammon; Bacchus too, with Minerva, brought over their forces, when a battle was fought in which the Titans were all killed to a man. When Ammon and Bacchus became immortal and the Titans were utterly destroyed, Jupiter obtained the empire of the world, none daring to be so wicked <note>original: "impious"</note> as to dispute it with him. This is what the Libyans related of the first Bacchus, son of Ammon and Amalthea."

If this was a report of the Libyans, I easily guess from where <note>original: "whence"</note> they learned it; and

# Of the Mythological Ages <note>original: "Fabulous Ages"</note>

Do not wonder that the Greeks and Africans were in such agreement about it. This wild and incoherent story <note>original: "romance"</note> deliberately avoids all mention of Osiris, as if there had been no such king in Egypt. It begins with the era of Saturn and reconciles him with his deadly enemies, the Titans. But its primary purpose seems to be to make Bacchus the son of the Libyan Ammon and a deity older than Jupiter. From this, the historian takes the opportunity—though I think unnecessarily—to conclude that there must have been more than one Bacchus, and that one of them was older than the Theban Bacchus. 

> "Some (1)," he says, "claim that there were several Bacchuses. Among other proofs, they cite the war against the Titans. Since everyone agrees that Bacchus fought alongside Jupiter in this war, they say it would be absurd to place the era of the Titans after the time of Semele <note>Semele was the mortal mother of the Greek Dionysus (Bacchus), suggesting a much later date for his birth than the Titan war.</note>, or to suggest that Cadmus, the son of Agenor, was older than the Olympian gods."

The conclusion may be very accurate, but I must disagree with him on the main point: neither this nor any other history can convince me that any Bacchus ever joined with Jupiter against the Titans. This is because I believe Jupiter to be at least two generations younger than Bacchus.

***

(1) <note>original Greek: "Ὑπὲρ δὲ τοῦ πλείους Διονύσους γεγονέναι, σὺν ἄλλαις ἀποδείξεσι πειρῶνται φέρειν καὶ τὴν ἐκ τοῦ Τιτανομαχίας. συμφωνουμένου γὰρ παρὰ πᾶσιν, ὅτι Διόνυσος τῷ Διὶ συνηγωνίσατο τὸν πρὸς τοὺς Τιτᾶνας πόλεμον, οὐδαμῶς πρέπειν φασὶ τὴν Τιτάνων γενεὰν τιθέναι μετὰ τοὺς τῆς Σεμέλης χρόνους, οὐδὲ Κάδμον τὸν Ἀγήνορος ἀποφαίνεσθαι πρεσβύτερον εἶναι τῶν Ὀλυμπίων θεῶν."</note> Regarding the existence of multiple Bacchuses, they attempt to offer proofs, including the War of the Titans <note>original: "Titanomachy"</note>. Since everyone agrees that Bacchus fought with Jupiter in the war against the Titans, they argue it is impossible to place the generation of the Titans after the time of Semele, or to claim that Cadmus, the son of Agenor, was older than the Olympian gods. Diodorus Siculus, Book 3, p. 145.

# The History and Chronology

<margin>*Summary of the history of Bacchus.*</margin>
To provide a clear summary of what has been said about Bacchus and to remove the inconsistencies so common in mythological <note>original: "fabulous"</note> history, we must first give priority to the Indian Bacchus; for I believe that is a point that cannot be contested. As for the Arabian Bacchus, about whom we know so little, every reader must use their own judgment. Some may think he came from Egypt; others that he was the same as the Indian Bacchus, which I think is the most probable opinion. Because he was worshipped alongside Uranus <note>The ancient Greek personification of the sky.</note>, he may be as old as, and probably an older deity than him. 

Of all the other figures called Bacchus, Osiris is undoubtedly the most ancient, though his era cannot be placed as far back as the Titan empire. The (1) Egyptians claimed <note>original: "feigned"</note> that he was the son of Saturn, as if they wanted to make him equal to Jupiter. The poet Thymœtes seems to have had this in mind when he asserts that Bacchus appointed Jupiter (the son of Saturn) to reign over Egypt; in my view, by "Jupiter," he likely means Osiris. 

The Greek poets felt they had the right to invent a Bacchus for any age or country. However, by examining their genealogies, we find them all to be later than Osiris, or at most, the very same person. Thus, one Bacchus is called the son of Jupiter and Proserpine; another the son of Nilus; a third the son of Jupiter and Io, or of Jupiter and Ceres. All of these may be traced back to a single king of Egypt, and that king must be Osiris. Only the son of Caprius, as—

***

<margin>(1) "After this, Saturn ruled, and having married his sister Rhea, he fathered—according to some mythologists—Osiris and Isis, but according to most, Jupiter and Juno." <note>original Greek: Μετὰ δὲ ταῦτα τὸν Κρόνον ἄρξαι, καὶ γήμαντα τὴν ἀδελφὴν Ῥέαν, γεννῆσαι κατὰ μέν τινας τῶν μυθολόγων Ὄσιριν καὶ Ἶσιν, κατὰ δὲ τοὺς πλείστους, Δία τε καὶ Ἥραν. Diodorus Siculus, Book 1, page 13.</note></margin>

<summary>The author reconciles the various regional identities of Bacchus—Indian, Arabian, and Greek—by arguing that most are actually later reinterpretations of the Egyptian king Osiris.</summary>
<keywords>Bacchus, Osiris, Jupiter, Saturn, Indian Bacchus, Egyptian mythology, Diodorus Siculus, Thymœtes, chronology, Titan empire</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the various historical and mythological identities of Bacchus, specifically focusing on his links to Egyptian, Libyan, and biblical figures.</meta>

# Of the Mythological <note>original: "Fabulous"</note> Ages.

As Cicero <note>original: "Tully"</note> calls him, he can be placed before him; and he, as we said above, must be the Indian Bacchus, the conqueror of Asia. The Theban Bacchus was born several ages after Osiris, at a time when history began to dawn and poetic fictions began to lose their influence; therefore, his era is determined by historical circumstances. The Libyan Bacchus hardly deserves to be mentioned, except on account of his alleged father Ammon. The name of (1) Hammon, or Ammon, may be derived from Ham, the son of Noah; but he can by no means be considered a deity of greater antiquity than the Thracian or Greek gods, because he is said to be a contemporary of Saturn and Jupiter. Despite this, the poet has succeeded so well in his design as to gain belief from the gullible Greeks, and even to deceive the great (2) conqueror of the world <note>Alexander the Great.</note> by making him appeal to the oracle in order to be recognized as the son of Ammon.

<margin>Bacchus compared with Noah.</margin>
In the legend of such an ancient deity as Bacchus, whose name also spread through all nations, we may naturally expect some references to facts recorded by Moses; and the use we make of them should be to confirm the authority of the Bible <note>original: "sacred writings"</note>. Thus, I think we may conclude that the history of Noah is genuine and original—

...that he (1) lived before and after the flood; that he was a husbandman <note>original: "huſbandman," a farmer or tiller of the ground</note> and taught the people agriculture; that he planted the vine, made wine, and was (2) overcome by its power; since all these circumstances are transferred to Bacchus. And Bacchus, for all I know, may be one of the best arguments to prove that India and China were settled <note>original: "planted"</note> by Noah and part of his family—an opinion strongly maintained by some (3) modern scholars. Not that I would make him the same identical person as Noah, as others have done, because I suppose that he lived nearly a thousand years after the flood.

***

(1) It was a common opinion that Bacchus was born twice, and the allegorizers <note>scholars who interpret myths as symbolic stories</note> who took him to represent wine itself accounted for it in several ways. For instance, they noted that the leaf of the vine sprouts first, and then the grape. Some thought that the "second birth" referred to the custom of boiling the wine after it was made to increase its strength and flavor. But others said, "The vine, along with other fruits, being destroyed in the flood of Deucalion <note>the Greek mythological equivalent of the Great Flood</note>, it sprouted again after the flood; which is to be taken for the second appearance of this god among men." <note>original: "Δὶς δ' αὐτῶ τὴν γένεσιν ἐκ Διὸς παραδεδόσθαι, διὰ τὸ δοκεῖν μετὰ τῶν ἄλλων, ἐν τῷ κατὰ τὸν Δευκαλίωνα κατακλύσμῳ φθαρῆναι καὶ τούτους τοὺς κάρπους, καὶ κατὰ τὴν ἐπομβρίαν πάλιν ἀναφύεντων, ὡσπερεὶ δευτέραν ἐπιφανείαν ταύτην ὑπάρξαι τοῦ θεοῦ παρ' ἀνθρώποις." translation: "It is handed down that his birth from Zeus occurred twice, because it seemed that these fruits, along with others, were destroyed in the flood during the time of Deucalion; and when they grew again after the heavy rains, it was as if this were a second appearance of the god among men."</note> Diodorus Siculus, Book 3, p. 138. This is no forced or obscure allusion to the general deluge and the circumstances of Noah’s life.

(2) Bacchus was represented as overcome with wine in the public processions of the Greeks, as appears from Athenaeus: <note>original: "Οὐ καλῶς δ' οἱ πλάττοντες [Bacchum] ἐπὶ τῆς ἁμάξης διὰ μέσης τῆς ἀγορᾶς οἰνωμένον· ἐπιδείκνυνται γὰρ τοῖς θεαταῖς, ὅτι καὶ τοῦ θεοῦ κρείττων ἐστὶν ὁ οἶνος." translation: "Those who portray Bacchus drunk on a wagon in the middle of the marketplace do not do well; for they show the spectators that wine is more powerful than the god himself."</note> *The Deipnosophists*, Book 10, p. 428.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of mythological syncretism—the blending of different beliefs—shifting from the comparison of Bacchus and Noah to the parallels between the Greek god and the biblical figures Moses and Nimrod.</meta>

### Of the Fabulous Ages.

<margin>Bacchus compared with Moses.</margin>
Though the Israelites kept themselves unmixed with the heathens <note>non-Jewish nations, particularly those practicing polytheism</note> for several ages, the Phoenicians and Egyptians must have in time become acquainted with their history and traditions. These nations first appropriated those stories for themselves and then communicated them to the Greeks. This is the source of the similar events found in the lives of Moses and Bacchus. For example, both were born in Egypt and were described as beautiful children. Both had two mothers <note>Moses had his biological mother and Pharaoh’s daughter; in myth, Bacchus was born from Semele and then again from Jupiter's thigh</note> and were exposed upon the water in an ark or chest. Both were depicted with horns <note>a common symbolic depiction of "rays of light" on Moses's face, often seen in art like Michelangelo's Moses</note>. Bacchus is called the Lawgiver <note>original: "Legislator"</note>, and he passed through the Red Sea. One of the Bacchantes <note>original: "Bacchæ," the female followers of Bacchus</note> in the plays of Euripides brought out water by striking a rock with her thyrsus <note>a ritual staff tipped with a pinecone and wrapped in ivy</note>. These examples, and more, the reader will find (1) readily collected; it is enough to say that they are many branches of truth turned into fables by grafting them onto Pagan history.

<margin>Bacchus compared with Nimrod.</margin>
Bacchus has also been thought to be Nimrod, the "mighty hunter" mentioned in Scripture. What I suppose gave rise to this opinion was Nimrod's formation of a kingdom in the East. However, this kingdom does not seem to lie on the path of Bacchus's journey, nor does it seem to have been part of his Indian empire. Once this idea was proposed, critics endeavored to find proofs for it, and the Greek names for Bacchus are cited as plausible reasons. For instance—

(1) See Vossius, *On Idolatry* <note>original: "Vide Vossium De Idololatria"</note>, Book 1, chapter 30.

# The History and Chronology

->page 86<-

<margin>Of the names Dionysus, Iacchus, and Bacchus.</margin>
To make our history of this god more complete, we will spend a sentence or two on the different names under which he was worshiped. These are too numerous to be treated individually; therefore, I shall confine myself to only three, and those in most common use: <term>Dionysus</term>, <term>Iacchus</term>, and <term>Bacchus</term>. None of these can be proven to be his true Indian or Scythian name; instead, they all seem to have been given to him after he ceased to be a mortal man. The first name, <term>Dionysus</term>, is the one by which he was known to the oldest Greek poets, Homer and Hesiod; the Arabians and Egyptians probably used a word not very different from this. It was the general opinion of the Greeks, as well as of other nations, that this name related to the (2) city, so often mentioned, which he built or where he was born. The Indians are said to use the word (3) <term>Δεῦνος</term> <note>original: "Δεῦνος"</note>, or *Deun*, for a *King*; so that for them, it signified the *King of Nysa*. However, for the Arabians, it meant something more, if it is

---

> (1) "The land of Ogygia <note>an ancient name for Thebes or Egypt</note> calls me Bacchus; Egypt considers me Osiris; the mystics name me Phacen <note>likely Phanes, a primeval deity</note>; the Indians believe I am Dionysus; the Roman rites call me Liber; the Arabian race calls me Adoneus; the Lucanians call me Pantheus." <note>original: "Ogygia me Bacchum vocat, Osirin Ægyptus putat, Mystæ Phacen nominant, Dionyson Indi existimant, Romana sacra Liberum, Arabica gens Adoneum, Lucanianus Pantheum. Auson. Epig. 29."</note>
> — "And they call him Bacchus, and Bromius, and Lyaeus, the Fire-born, the twice-born, and the only one with two mothers." <note>original: "Bacchumque vocant, Bromiumque, Dyæumque, Ignigenam, Satumque iterum, solumque Bimatrem. Ovid. Met. Lib. 4."</note>

<margin>(2) "There [at Nysa], the child, having been raised by nymphs, was named Dionysus from both his father and the place." <note>original: "Ἐνταῦθα [Nysæ] δ᾽ ὑπὸ νυμφῶν τραφέντα τὸν παῖδα προσαγορευθῆναι μὲν ἀπὸ τοῦ πατρὸς, καὶ τοῦ τόπου, Διόνυσον. ...derived from their (1) *Du*, or *Dy*, *Nysa*, which means *The Lord* or *God of Nysa*. Those who identify Bacchus with Moses have another theory: that (2) *Dy Nysae* is the same as *Dy Sinai*, or *God of Sinai*, created by transposing the syllables <note>a linguistic process called metathesis</note>. However, I think this is pushing the parallel too far. Etymological proofs are rarely convincing unless they are direct and undeniable, commanding our agreement. To offer reasons for a name's meaning based on an accident or a quality hidden within that word in another language shows a creative imagination, but it carries little weight with discerning readers. 

Nevertheless, when we consider the high status of this god, I believe we can easily agree with the opinion of some learned scholars regarding his name <term>IACCHUS</term>. This may be his oldest, or at least his most sacred name, as it was the word used in the (3) mysteries <note>secret religious rites of ancient Greece</note>. It is said that this name was never (4) applied to the Theban Bacchus, but only to the most ancient one. According to the best critics, such as Selden, Vossius, and others, <term>IACH</term> is the same as <term>JAH</term>, or *Jehovah*, the God of the Hebrews, which was sometimes written (5) <term>IAO</term>—a deity proclaimed by

## The History and Chronology

->page 90<-

The syllable *Fo* <note>a Chinese phonetic transcription of "Buddha"</note> does not seem to be his Indian name, but was given to him by the Chinese, perhaps on account of their first king *Fohi* <note>Fuxi, a mythical figure in Chinese tradition</note>. Nor was this his only name; for he was likewise called *Fwe Kyau*, *Fo Tse*; and by the Chinese themselves, *She*, or *She Kya*, and by the Japanese *Xaca*, or *Sha Ka*. Whether *Kyau*, *She Kya*, or *Sha Ka*, may have any affinity with *Jah*, *Jao*, or *Iach* <note>archaic transliterations of Hebrew names for God, including Jehovah</note>, I leave to the reader’s judgment.

<margin>
*Maidashuren, or the Bull-Giant, of Malabar.*
</margin>

The learned professor Bayer of Petersburg says he was informed by the Danish missionaries at Tranquebar <note>a Danish trading post in modern-day Tamil Nadu, India</note>, "That the Indians of Malabar have a tradition, that there formerly lived a giant called *Maidashuren* <note>likely Mahishasura, the buffalo demon of Hindu mythology</note>, or the *Bull-Giant*, born at *Nisadaburam*, near the mountain *Meru*: he wore bull’s horns; was fed with the flesh of cows, and of other animals; was often drunk with wine; and made war upon the gods. He had for his companions eight *Pudam*, or gigantic and malicious demons, of the family of Indian shep-

---

> (1) See Kircher, *China Illustrated*. Part 3, pages 152, 153, etc.
> (2) "The Tamil Indians relate that *Maidashuren* was a certain person, his name being derived from *Maidham* and *Asuren*, signifying, as it were, a BULL-GIANT (for they imagine their heroes to have been giants). He was born in the city of *Nisadaburam*, not far from mount *Meru*; he wore bull’s horns and fed upon the flesh of both other animals and cows (which among the Indians is the greatest crime); and, being accustomed to filling himself with wine to the point of drunkenness, he waged war against the gods. Furthermore, he had in his company eight *Pudam*, or gigantic and malicious demons, from the family of Indian shepherds, whom they call *Kobaler*, that is, Shepherds; he was carried in a chariot drawn by eight lions, or leopards, or tigers, or elephants." <note>original Latin: "Indi Tamuli narrant, MAIDASHUREN fuisse aliquem..."</note> Here you have *Nysa*, where some of the Greeks also say Bacchus was born; you have mount *Meru*, from which Jupiter's <term>thigh</term> <note>original Greek: μηρὸς (meros). This refers to the myth that Bacchus was sewn into Zeus/Jupiter's thigh</note> was the subject of Lucian's jests; you have the <term>kobalos</term> <note>original Greek: κοβάλους (kobalous); mischievous spirits or companions associated with Bacchus</note> of Bacchus, and the horns, and the chariot, and whatever else you might desire from the myths of ancient Greece. For even now, the Indians tell many stories about the Amazons, or warlike viragos, near the Ganges River. Bayer, *Bactrian History*, pages 2, 3.

I deliver this fable as it is handed down to us, and I recommend the reader's inquiry into whether the remains of *Nisadaburam* and *Meru* may not be found in the cities (1) *Nisa* and *Nishapur* <note>original: "Nischabour" — a major historical city in Khorasan, modern-day Iran</note>, and *Marv* <note>original: "Marou"</note>, or *Merv-rud* <note>original: "Merou Erroud" — an ancient city in what is now Turkmenistan</note>; they seem to be situated at about 38 degrees of latitude in ancient Bactria, the country from which I suppose Bacchus to have set out. I will not take it upon myself to say that this profane eating of the flesh of cows and other animals is an argument for Bacchus’s Tartarian or Scythian descent; but I cannot help but observe that the Greeks had some notion of him in this character. You have the (2) *Cobali* of Bacchus, his horns, and his chariot, and whatever else is required for the ancient myths of the Greeks. As for the Amazons, the Indians near the Ganges are still full of stories about their exploits."

I present this myth as it has been handed down to us, and I suggest the reader investigate whether the remains of *Nisadaburam* and *Meru* might be found in the cities of (1) *Nisa* and *Nishapur* <note>original: "Nifchabour"</note>, and *Marou*, or *Merv-rud* <note>original: "Merou Erroud"</note>. These seem to be located at about 38 degrees of latitude in ancient Bactria <note>a historical region in Central Asia, covering parts of modern Afghanistan, Uzbekistan, and Tajikistan</note>, the country from which I assume Bacchus set out.

I will not presume to say that this "profane" eating of cow's flesh and other animals is proof of Bacchus's Tartar or Scythian origins. However, I cannot help but observe that the Greeks had some concept of him in this role; they had a Bacchus called (3) *Omestes* <note>original: $\Omega\mu\eta\sigma\tau\acute{\eta}\varsigma$</note>, or the "Eater of Raw Flesh," to whom human victims were sacrificed. The age of this myth cannot be determined, but it must be admitted that it car—

<margin>The Indian god Ram.</margin>
Let us see whether we can find more respectable traces of him in the Mughal Empire <note>original: "Mogulistan"</note>. In the province of Bihar <note>original: "Becar"</note>, near the Ganges, the Indians had a god called Ram, whose character agrees fairly well with that of Bacchus; (1) Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit scholar</note> takes him to be Fo, or Shakyamuni <note>original: "Sha ka"</note>, mentioned above. "Ram," says (2) Mr. Thévenot <note>Jean de Thévenot (1633–1667), a French traveler to the East</note>, "was a great King, whose holiness and good deeds earned him a more direct connection with God than other saints have, and therefore they show him much more reverence." This tradition may prove that he was at first a mortal; but the word <term>Ram</term>, or <term>Ramas</term> with a Greek ending, seems to mean (3) *The Most High*, or God himself: and was probably the same as their <term>Brahma</term>, the creator of the universe. But this perhaps was a title which the wiser sort of the modern Indians—who are more like deists <note>believers in a creator God who does not intervene in the world</note> than idolaters—might think too great to be given to their god <term>Ram</term>.

<margin>Samonifu, or Shakyamuni <note>original: "Shakmonni"</note>.</margin>
From the Ganges, let us proceed to the more easterly parts of India and beyond. In Shaanxi <note>original: "Shen Si"</note>, one of the northern provinces of China which is said to be the first that was inhabited—at a city called Ganzhou <note>original: "Kam ju" or "Campion"</note>, and by Marco Polo <note>original: "M. Paul the Venetian"</note> (1) called Campition—the (2) ambassadors of Shah Rukh <note>original: "Sha Rokh"</note>, son of Tamerlane, to the Emperor of China, found in the year 1419:

"A temple, each side of which was 500 cubits in length, and in the middle an idol lying as if asleep, 50 feet in length; the hands and feet were nine feet long, and the head 21 feet in circumference. At his back and over his head were other idols of one cubit, looking as if they were alive. The great one was gilded all over and had one hand under his head, while the other was stretched down along his thigh. They called him SAMONIFU <note>a corruption of Shakyamuni Buddha</note> and ran in crowds to bow before him."

I have no doubt this was the Chinese idol <term>FO</term> <note>a Chinese name for the Buddha</note>. Ramusio, the collector of travel accounts, (3) says: 

"In Ganzhou, their temples are built like the churches at Venice, large enough to hold four or five thousand men; in them are seen the statues of a man and woman stretched on the ground, each forty feet long, all of one piece and gilded." 

Ramusio differs slightly from the previous account, which is no surprise, since he wrote not from his own knowledge but from the information of others.

<margin>The Indian god Ram.</margin>

He was likely the same as their *Brahma*, the creator of the universe. However, this was perhaps a title that the more enlightened modern Indians—who are more deists than idol-worshippers—might think was too grand to be given to their god Ram.

This description of the temples and images, together with the similarity of the names, proves that both were dedicated to one and the same god. <term>MONNI</term>, or <term>MONI</term>, is an added <note>original: "adscititious"</note> term that we can make nothing of; but <term>FU</term> seems to mean the same as <term>FO</term>, the great Indian idol. As we observed before, he was called *She Kia* or *Sha Ka*, from which come the names *Shack*, *Sha*, or *Sa*, as it might be differently pronounced by different people.

<margin>*Kiackiack in the kingdom of Pegu.*</margin>
Let us return from the north to the southern country of India toward the ocean. Captain Hamilton—whose diligence and curiosity in observing everything remarkable in his travels our traders would do well to imitate—(2) says, "That in the kingdom of Pegu <note>A historic kingdom in modern-day Myanmar (Burma)</note>, there are two large temples near Syriam <note>original: "Syrian," referring to the port of Thanlyin</note> so like one another in structure that they seem to be built by one model. One stands about six miles to the southward, called the temple of <term>Kiackiack</term> <note>Derived from the Mon word "Kyaik," meaning a pagoda or a holy being</note>, or *The God of Gods*. In it is an image twenty yards long, lying in a sleeping posture <note>This refers to the "Reclining Buddha," representing the Buddha at the moment of his death and entry into Nirvana</note>, and according to their tradition, he has lain in that posture for 6,000 years. His doors and windows are always open..."

"and everyone has the liberty to see him; and when he awakes, this world is to be annihilated." This is certainly the same idol as those of Turfan <note>original: "Tarkan"</note> and Campion <note>A historical name for Kan-chou, modern Zhangye in China, a city on the Silk Road</note>; their size and posture prove as much. And the name *Kiack*, as we said before, may possibly be the same as *Iach*; and that the same as *Shak* <note>Referring to Shakyamuni, the Buddha</note>. The repetition of the syllables in *Kiackiack* brings to my mind the oldest name of Bacchus, or *Bakchebakchos* <note>original Greek: Βακχέβακχος</note>; together with the repeated shouts <note>original: "vociferations"</note> in his mysteries, by which perhaps they meant to invoke him as the supreme deity, or God of Gods. As to the time of his sleeping, it is indeed a longer term than the ancients allowed to Bacchus, for he is said to have slept only three years; but stories of this kind lose nothing, and rather gain, by time and telling. I think, however, that the fable of a god's sleeping at all was probably derived from India, and that, in the opinion of the natives, their idol was of the greatest antiquity. And had they reckoned only 4,000 years instead of 6,000, it might be thought a chronological argument in favor of Bacchus.

# The History and Chronology

<margin>Jagrenat, or Jagannath.</margin>
I cannot leave the subject of India without taking notice of another deity of very great antiquity; for he is reported to have come into that country three or four thousand years ago. He is called Jagrenat, or (1) Jagannath, named after the place where a magnificent temple is built to him on the Coromandel coast near the Ganges. This temple, or Pagoda <note>original: "Pagod"</note>, is of a very unusual structure, though few travelers have thought it worth their notice. Monsieur (2) Tavernier, by his own account, seems to have been admitted into it; he says it is the most significant idolatrous temple in all India, that it is filled with a multitude of statues and extraordinary <note>original: "monstrous," which in this period meant "unnatural" or "huge" in form</note> images, and specifically has one chief idol placed on an altar called *Resora*. However, he gives no description of the building itself. Our countryman Captain Hamilton, who could not gain admittance even though he offered money, has provided us with a drawing <note>original: "draught"</note> of the exterior form of the temple and a short account of the chief idol within. "The temple," (3) he says, "is built in the shape of a Canary wine cask <note>original: "Canary Pipe," referring to a large barrel used for transporting wine from the Canary Islands</note> set on its end, about 40 or 50 yards high. About the middle of it is an image of an ox carved from a single stone, larger than a live one. He looks toward the southeast, and his hindquarters are fixed in the wall. The structure is topped with a crown of the same diameter as its middle, and the temple

The nights were spent beating on small drums <note>original: "Tabors"</note> and brass cymbals, with songs of praise to Jagannath <note>original: "Jagarenat"</note>; who is merely a stone god, not carved into any figure, but an irregular, pyramidal black stone weighing about 400 or 500 pounds, with two expensive diamonds placed near the top to represent eyes; and a nose and mouth painted with vermilion <note>A bright red pigment</note>." This information he received from one of his servants, a Hindu <note>original: "gentow," a term used by early European travelers to refer to Hindus</note>, who was sent to the temple for that purpose; and it matches the account of Tavernier, though the latter says the idol was made of black sandalwood. One might suspect that this was the same deity that the Arabs worshipped in the form of a (3) square stone, and which some have thought referred to their god Bacchus. ...weightier concerns of profit and commerce, they would favor the cause of learning enough to obtain some information concerning this deity. I do not mean from the traditions of the common people <note>original: "vulgar"</note>, but from the books of the Brahmins <note>original: "Bramins"</note>; to which I suppose they may now have easy access, since we have established such a strong presence in that part of India.

<margin>Why Hercules is joined with Bacchus.</margin>
I connect the history of Hercules to that of Bacchus because both seem to be foreign heroes, and neither belonged to the highest rank of gods in Greece, though they were otherwise of the greatest importance. Other nations, besides the Greeks, claimed a right to Hercules as well as Bacchus. He was worshiped by the Phoenicians at Tyre, though how the Tyrians came to know of him cannot now be discovered. Nor can the Egyptians give a better account of how they encountered him. The Libyans are said to have had some knowledge of him, and perhaps had as much right to claim him as any of the others, but no more. All of them told myths about him without regard for chronology or order; the most we can gather from them is that, wherever he came from, he was a person of unparalleled strength and courage. His first actions seem to have been performed in India, and any clarity we can expect regarding his case must come from the history of Alexander the Great. (1) Strabo, following Eratosthenes, thought these exploits of Bacchus and Hercules were fictions created by the Macedonians to honor their king:

***

(1) "For all those who were around Alexander preferred the marvelous rather than the truth." <note>original Greek: Πάντες μὲν γὰρ οἱ περὶ Ἀλέξανδρον τὸ θαυμαστὸν ἀντὶ τ' ἀληθὲς ἀποδέχονται μᾶλλον. Strabo, Book 15, page 698.</note>

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

<sig>N 2</sig>

But surely there must have been some grounds for the report of their expeditions into India; for no reason can be assigned why these two gods, rather than any other, should have been chosen <note>original: "pitched upon"</note> to flatter Alexander. (1) Megasthenes, who lived closer to the time of Alexander and was present on the scene, had the best opportunity to inquire into the truth of the reports, and he always acknowledged them to be true history.

<margin>Concerning the era of Hercules.</margin>
The era of Hercules is as unknown as that of Bacchus. Considered as a Greek, he was one of the youngest of the gods, being the reputed son of Alcmena, wife of Amphitryon, king of Thebes. Here we must refer to a new era—namely, the Destruction of Troy, which may be placed in round numbers at 1,200 years before Christ. The actions ascribed to the Grecian Hercules, such as his sacking of Troy in the reign of Laomedon and his expedition with the Argonauts <note>The band of heroes who sailed with Jason to find the Golden Fleece</note>, can scarcely place him more than fifty years before that epoch. And yet (2) Herodotus says, "That from Hercules, son of Alcmena, to his own time was 900 years." This would make him a hundred years older and place him, by our reckoning, 1,350 years before Christ. However that may be, it is certain that there must have been another Hercules, much older than this Theban. (3) Arrian says, "Her—

Strabo, Book 15, page 687.</note>

# Of the Fabulous Ages.

<sig>N 2</sig>

...but surely there must have been some grounds for the report of their expeditions into India; for no reason can be assigned why these two gods, rather than any other, should have been chosen <note>original: "pitched upon"</note> to flatter Alexander. (1) Megasthenes, who lived closer to the time of Alexander and was present on the scene, had the best opportunity to inquire into the truth of the reports, and he always acknowledged them to be true history.

<margin>Concerning the era of Hercules.</margin>
The era of Hercules is as unknown as that of Bacchus. Considered as a Greek, he was one of the youngest of the gods, being the reputed son of Alcmena, wife of Amphitryon, king of Thebes. Here we must refer to a new era—namely, the Destruction of Troy, which may be placed in round numbers at 1,200 years before Christ. The actions ascribed to the Greek Hercules, such as his sacking of Troy in the reign of Laomedon and his expedition with the Argonauts <note>The legendary heroes who sailed with Jason to find the Golden Fleece</note>, can scarcely place him more than fifty years before that era <note>original: "epoch"</note>. And yet (2) Herodotus says, "That from Hercules, son of Alcmena, to his own time were 900 years." This would make him a hundred years older and place him, by our reckoning, 1,350 years before Christ. Regardless of this <note>original: "Be this as it will"</note>, it is certain that there must have been another Hercules, much older than this Theban. (3) Arrian says, "Hercules

---

(1) "Megasthenes and a few others consider the accounts concerning Hercules and Dionysus <note>Bacchus</note> to be trustworthy; but most others, including Eratosthenes, consider them untrustworthy and mythical, just like the stories told among the Greeks." <note>original Greek: Καὶ τὰ περὶ Ἡρακλέους δὲ, καὶ Διονύσου Μεγασθένης μὲν μετ' ὀλίγων πιστὰ ἡγεῖται· τῶν δ' ἄλλων οἱ πλείους, ὧν ἐστὶ καὶ Ἐρατοσθένης, ἄπιστα καὶ μυθώδη, καθάπερ καὶ τὰ παρὰ τοῖς Ἕλλησιν. Strabo, Book 15, page 687.</note>

(2) "For Hercules, the son of Alcmena, lived nine hundred years before my time." <note>original Greek: Ἡρακλέϊ δὲ τῷ Ἀλκμήνης κατὰ εἰνακόσια ἔτεα ἐς ἐμέ. Herodotus, Book 2, chapter 145.</note>

(3) "For there is in Tyre a temple of Hercules of as great antiquity as human memory can preserve, not of the Argive Hercules, the son of Alcmena; for Hercules has been honored in Tyre many generations earlier." <note>original Greek: Ἔστι γὰρ ἐν Τύρῳ ἱερὸν Ἡρακλέους παλαιότητα ᾗ μνήμη ἀνθρωπίνη διασώζεται, οὐ τοῦ Ἀργείου Ἡρακλέους, τοῦ τῆς Ἀλκμήνης· πολλαῖς γὰρ γενεαῖς πρότερον τιμᾶται ἐν Τύρῳ Ἡρακλῆς. Arrian, Expedition of Alexander, Book 2, page 43, Stephens edition.</note>

# The History and Chronology

"...[Her]cules was worshipped at Tyre many ages before the son of Alcmena <note>The Greek hero Hercules, son of Zeus and the mortal Alcmena</note>," which we have no reason to question; but the account of the Tyrians themselves is altogether incredible. (1) They affirmed that the temple of Hercules was as old as their city, which was built 2,300 years before the time of Herodotus; such was the vanity of the Tyrians! In Egypt, the priests informed Herodotus that (2) "Hercules was admitted into the number of the Twelve Gods 17,000 years before the reign of Amasis." These, reduced to luni-solar years <note>Years calculated by the cycles of both the moon and the sun; ancient chronologists often used this to explain impossibly long lifespans or eras</note>, may make about 1,417 years, and with the reign of Amasis, 1,987. "The Egyptians," says (3) Diodorus, "affirmed that Hercules, son of Alcmena, was born 10,000 years after the older Hercules." These, by the same reduction, amount to about 833, and with the time which I have allowed him (or 1,250 BC), will make 2,083. I lay no great stress upon the Egyptian chronology, nor indeed upon the Greek before the Trojan era; but these are the only helps we have, and we must be content with them. All I think that we can conclude from this is that the Egyptians received Hercules as a deity about 2,000 years, or more, before the Christian era—which, as we observed, was about the reign of Osiris. And though I do not affirm that he, or Bacchus, lived as late as the age of Osiris, yet I think this was the time when both [were...]

---

(1) <note>original Greek: Ἔφασαν γὰρ, ἅμα Τύρῳ οἰκιζομένῃ καὶ τὸ ἱερὸν τοῦ θεοῦ ἱδρυνθῆναι· εἶναι δὲ ἔτεα ἀφ' οὗ Τύρον οἰκέουσι τριηκόσια καὶ δισχίλια.</note> "For they said that the temple of the god was established at the same time Tyre was founded, and that it has been two thousand three hundred years since they have lived in Tyre." Herodotus, Book 2, chapter 44.

(2) <note>original Greek: Ἀλλά τις ἀρχαῖός ἐστι θεὸς Αἰγυπτίοισι Ἡρακλέης· ὡς δὲ αὐτοὶ λέγουσι, ἔτεα ἐστὶ ἑπτακισχίλια καὶ μύρια ἐς Ἄμασιν βασιλεύσαντα, ἐπεί τε ἐκ τῶν ὀκτὼ θεῶν οἱ δυώδεκα ἐγένοντο, τῶν Ἡρακλέα ἕνα νομίζουσι.</note> "But Hercules is an ancient god among the Egyptians; as they say themselves, it was 17,000 years before the reign of Amasis, when the twelve gods were produced from the eight, of whom they consider Hercules to be one." Herodotus, Book 2, chapter 43.

(3) <note>original Greek: Τὸν δὲ ἐξ Ἀλκμήνης γενόμενον, ὕστερον πλείοσιν ἔτεσιν ἢ μυρίοις, Ἀλκαῖον ἐκ γενετῆς καλούμενον, ὕστερον Ἡρακλέα μετονομασθῆναι.</note> "But the one born of Alcmena, more than ten thousand years later, was called Alcaeus from birth and was later renamed Hercules." Diodorus Siculus, Book 1, page 14.

The author concludes that the Egyptian worship of Hercules likely began around 2000 BC, coinciding with the era of Osiris.</summary>

->### Of the Fabulous Ages.<-

...were admitted into the number of the gods of Egypt. Osiris, with the counsel of Thoth, gave the Egyptians laws, both sacred and civil. By Thoth, the ancients always understood Mercury, or a person of the same character—a man of letters skilled in the arts of modeling a government. From this time, and no earlier—regardless of what they claimed later—the Egyptians kept a sort of reckoning of time. It is evident to me that all their history must be later than the age of Jupiter and the Titans <note>In Greek mythology, the Titans were the generation of deities preceding the Olympian gods led by Jupiter (Zeus)</note>.

<margin>Hercules and Bacchus were not contemporaries.</margin>
Some modern (1) authors, who identify Bacchus with Moses, Hercules with Joshua, and India with the land of Canaan, allege that these two gods were contemporaries and allies in the invasion of India. However, I can see no foundation for such an opinion, especially if we believe the report of the (2) Indians themselves, who say that "Bacchus was fifteen generations older than Hercules." It is true that we must look for the first Hercules, as well as Bacchus, in India; and although the expeditions of both are mentioned together by authors when they speak of India, they do not mean that these events occurred at the same time, but rather the opposite.

---

(1) "Therefore, let it remain settled and fixed that the ancients once understood Moses and Joshua by the names of Bacchus and Hercules, and the Arabs and Canaanites by the name of the Indians." <note>original: "Ratum igitur fixumque maneat, antiquos olim Bacchi & Herculis, Mosen & Josuam, Indorum vero nomine, Arabes & Cananæos intellexisse."</note> Dickenson, *Delphi Phoenicizing*, p. 40. See also Vossius, *On Idolatry*, Book 1, chapter 27. "That Egyptian Hercules: who, according to the testimony of Possevino, lived about one thousand two hundred years before Alexander—which fits well with the times of Joshua—gave himself as a fellow soldier to Bacchus, and appeared together with him at Mount Nysa <note>A legendary mountain in Greek mythology where the rain nymphs, the Nysiads, raised the infant Bacchus (Dionysus)</note>." <note>original: "Hercules ille Ægyptius: qui Possevino teste, ad annos circiter mille ducentos ante Alexandrum, quod Josuæ temporibus apte convenit, Baccho commilitonem se dedit, & una cum illo ad Nysam montem comparuit."</note> *Delphi Phoenicizing*, p. 37.

(2) "The Indians say that Dionysus <note>Bacchus</note> is fifteen generations older than Hercules." <note>original Greek: Πρεσβύτερον τε Διόνυσον Ἡρακλέος Δέκα καὶ Πέντε γενεῇσιν Ἰνδοὶ λέγουσιν.</note> Arrian, *History of India*, p. 175.

<summary>This page argues that Egyptian history begins with the laws of Osiris and Thoth and refutes theories that Hercules and Bacchus were contemporaries or biblical figures like Moses and Joshua.</summary>
<keywords>Osiris, Thoth, Mercury, Egyptian Law, Bacchus, Hercules, Moses, Joshua, India, Arrian, Chronology, Mythology</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the mythological figures of Hercules and Bacchus, specifically focusing on their relative status and the extent of their legendary conquests in India.</meta>

<margin>*Hercules was not as great a god as Bacchus.*</margin>
Bacchus also has the advantage in terms of character as well as antiquity. We may observe that strength and valor are the chief attributes of Hercules, but beneficence <note>original: "beneficence"; meaning the quality of doing good or performing acts of kindness</note> is more uniquely associated with Bacchus, which makes him superior to the other. Alexander the Great, in his speech to his army, calls "(1) Bacchus a god of higher rank than Hercules." Though this speech was probably written for him by the historian, we may still believe that it reflects the sentiments of Alexander, who modeled his own character after Bacchus rather than Hercules. Besides, Hercules was thought to have entered only the (2) western borders of India, while Bacchus is said to have reigned over the whole country. Therefore, we may safely conclude that Hercules's conquests were not performed at the same time, nor were they in any degree comparable to those of Bacchus. The reason for this might be the different state of affairs after Bacchus arrived in India. He had nothing more to do than to overrun a vast country that was sparsely populated and to subdue a primitive and disorganized <note>original: "rude, and unconnected"</note> people. However, the regulations and laws established by Bacchus afterward made the later undertaking of Hercules much more difficult.

---

<margin>(1) "Indeed, the labors of Dionysus <note>Bacchus</note> were not few, a god who was then of a higher rank than Hercules." <note>original Greek: Οὐ μὲν δὴ οὐδὲ Διονύσου, ἀκρεστέρῳ τότε θεοῦ ἢ καθ' Ἡρακλέα, ὀλίγοι πόνοι.</note> Arrian, *The Campaigns of Alexander*, Book 5, p. 120.</margin>
<margin>(2) "Regarding Hercules, some report that he reached only as far as the western borders in the opposite direction, while others say he reached both sides." <note>original Greek: Περὶ δ' Ἡρακλέος οἱ μὲν ἐπὶ τ' ἀναντία μόνον μέχρι τῶν ἑσπερίων περάτων ἱστορᾶσιν, οἱ δ' ἐφ' ἑκάτερα.</note> Strabo, *Geography*, Book 15, p. 687.</margin>

<summary>This page argues for the superiority of Bacchus over Hercules based on his character of beneficence and the greater geographical extent of his Indian conquests. It suggests that Bacchus's early civilization of India made the country more difficult for Hercules to conquer later.</summary>

### Of the Fabulous Ages.
<header>Of the Fabulous Ages.</header>

<margin>*Hercules worshipped in India.*</margin>
Arrian (1) says, "There were many reports of Alexander’s expedition into India, and of Bacchus before him invading and conquering the Indians; but not much was said of Hercules." However, he must have performed something very considerable and worthy of notice to make himself remembered for so long in that country. The Indians believed him to be a native of India; and as such, he was worshipped by the (2) Surasenians <note>An ancient Indian people located in the region of Mathura.</note>. He is (3) said to have had many sons, and only one daughter called Pandæa, to whom he bequeathed a large territory in India, named Pandæa after her, along with elephants and a great army of cavalry and infantry. This was probably a tradition of the Pandæans; though we are not bound to believe that they descended from Hercules, nor that Hercules had such a daughter. The (4) Sibæ, an Indian nation, were reported to be the descendants of those who assisted Hercules at the—

---

(1) <note>original Greek: Ἀλλὰ καὶ Ἀλεξάνδρῳ γὰρ στρατεῦσαι ἐπὶ Ἰνδοὺς μοῦνον· καὶ περὶ Ἀλεξάνδρου, Διονύσου πέρι πολλὸς λόγος κατέχει, ὡς καὶ τότε στρατευσάντων ἐς Ἰνδοὺς, καὶ καταστρεψαμένων Ἰνδούς, Ἡρακλέους δ' πέρι οὐ πολλός. Arrian. Hist. Ind.</note> "But as for Alexander, he was the only one to lead an army against the Indians; and while there is much talk concerning Alexander and Bacchus, who both campaigned in and conquered India, there is not much said regarding Hercules." — Arrian, *History of India*.

(3) <note>original Greek: Καὶ τούτῳ [Herculi] ἄρσενας μὲν παῖδας πολλοὺς κάρτα γενέσθαι ἐν τῇ Ἰνδῶν γῇ, θυγατέρα δὲ μουνογενέην· ὄνομα δὲ εἶναι τῇ παιδὶ Πανδαίην· καὶ τὴν χώρην ἵνα τε ἐγένετο, καὶ ἧς τινος ἐπέτρεψεν αὐτὴν ἄρχειν Ἡρακλέης, Πανδαίην, τῆς παιδὸς ἐπώνυμον. The territory where she was born, and which Hercules gave her to rule, was called Pandæa after her." Pliny adds: "From these comes the nation of the Pandae, the only Indians ruled by women. They say only one child of that sex was born to Hercules, and for that reason, she was granted a special kingdom as a preferred gift." — Pliny, *Natural History*, Book 6, Chapter 20.

(4) <note>original Greek: Προσῆκον ἐπὶ τοὺς ὀνομαζομένους Σίβας· τούτους δὲ φασὶν ἀπογόνους εἶναι τῶν μεθ' Ἡρακλέους ἐπὶ τὴν Ἄορνον πέτραν στρατευσάντων. Diodor. Some Indians likewise affirmed that the (2) older Hercules was dressed in the same manner as the Theban <note>Hercules of Thebes is the most famous Greek version of the hero, known for his twelve labors.</note>; I suppose equipped with a club, lion skin, bow, and arrows. This last might be a fiction created by the Greeks to support the story of their Theban god, and the attack at Aornus might be another to flatter their king; but still, there must have been a general belief that Hercules had something to do in those parts, otherwise such fictions could not have been believed <note>original: "swallowed"</note>.

<margin>*The Egyptian Hercules.*</margin>
Herodotus (3) says, "The Egyptians had three orders of deities. The first consisted of Eight Gods, of which number was Pan <note>The god of nature and the wild.</note>, the oldest of all. The Second consisted of Twelve, of which number was Hercules. The Third consisted of those who were born of the Twelve, of which number was Bacchus <note>Dionysus.</note>." This is but a weak and unsatisfactory account of their gods, and it looks as if they meant to make the whole thing a mystery, as they did with almost everything else. If these differed in dignity according to the order of time, Bacchus seems to be demoted <note>original: "degraded"</note>; for by other Egyptian accounts, he was both—

---

<margin>(1) <note>original Greek: Ἄορνον δέ τινα πέτραν, ἧς τὰς ῥίζας ὁ Ἰνδὸς ὑπορρεῖ πλησίον τῶν πηγῶν, Ἀλεξάνδρου κατ’ μίαν προσβολὴν σεμνύοντες, ἔφασαν τὸν Ἡρακλέα τρὶς μὲν προσβαλεῖν τῇ πέτρᾳ ταύτῃ, τρὶς δ’ ἀποκρουσθῆναι.</note> "Regarding a certain rock called Aornus, the base of which the Indus flows past near its source: while they boast that Alexander took it in a single assault, they say that Hercules attacked this rock three times and was three times repulsed." — Strabo, Book 15, page 688.</margin>

<margin>(2) <note>original Greek: Τὴν σκευὴν ἢ ἔτυχε ὁ Ἡρακλέης ἥντινα ἐφόρεε, Μεγασθένης λέγει, ὅτι ὁμοίην τῷ Θηβαίῳ Ἡρακλεῖ, ὡς αὐτοὶ Ἰνδοὶ ἀπηγέονται.</note> "Megasthenes says that the attire which Hercules happened to wear was similar to that of the Theban Hercules, as the Indians themselves relate." — Arrian, *History of India*, page 174.</margin>

<margin>(3) <note>original Greek: Παρ’ Αἰγυπτίοισι δὲ Πὰν μὲν ἀρχαιότατος, καὶ τῶν Ὀκτὼ Πρώτων λεγομένων θεῶν εἶναι; Ἡρακλῆς δὲ, τῶν Δευτέρων, τῶν Δυώδεκα λεγομένων· Διόνυσον δὲ τῶν Τρίτων, οἳ ἐκ τῶν Δυώδεκα θεῶν ἐγένοντο.</note> "Among the Egyptians, Pan is the oldest and is said to be one of the Eight Primary Gods; Hercules is one of the Second order, called the Twelve; and Dionysus is of the Third order, who were born of the Twelve gods." — Herodotus, Book 2, chapter 145.</margin>

->OSIRIS, THE MOST ANCIENT KING OF ALL, TO THE IMMORTAL GODS:<-

He is not so optimistic <note>original: "sanguine"</note> as to declare it genuine or as old as it claims to be (that is, from the time of Osiris, Saturn, and Rhea). However, he thinks it was based on the most ancient Egyptian theology because the eight primary gods are mentioned here. "Yet it is entirely derived from the ancient and genuine theology of the ancient Egyptians, because in this inscription the eight primary gods of the Egyptians are mentioned." <note>original: Id tamen ex prisca & genuina antiquorum Ægyptiorum Theologia omnino petitum est, quod in hac inscriptione Octo memorentur Primarii Ægyptiorum Dii.</note> *Egyptian Pantheon*, Volume 1, page 18. It is true that eight gods are here mentioned: *Spirit, Heaven, Sun, Moon, Earth, Night, Day, and Love, the father of all things that are or shall be.* But then I ask, which of these is Pan? For Herodotus, by Pan, Hercules, and Bacchus, seems to mean real personages and not these ideal divinities. The learned Abbé Banier <note>Antoine Banier (1673–1741), a French scholar who interpreted myths as historical events (Euhemerism).</note> (*Mythology*, Book 2, chapter 3) thought he had found them in Diodorus: "Of whom some were also kings in Egypt; when their names are translated, some have the same names as the celestial ones, while others have their own names." <note>original: Ὧν ἐνίους καὶ βασιλεῖς γεγονέναι κατὰ τὴν Αἴγυπτον, μεθερμηνευομένων δ' αὐτῶν, τινας μὲν ὁμωνύμους ὑπάρχειν τοῖς οὐρανίοις, τινας δ' ἰδίαν ἐσχηκέναι, προσηγορίας.</note>

> Sun, Saturn, Rhea, Jupiter, Juno, Vulcan, Vesta, Mercury. <note>original: Ἥλιον, Κρόνον, Ῥέαν, Δία, Ἥραν, Ἥφαιστον, Ἑστίαν, Ἑρμῆν. / Solem, Saturnum, Rheam, Jovem, Junonem, Vulcanum, Vestam, Mercurium.</note> Diodorus Siculus, Book 1, page 8.

But here the same difficulty occurs as before: Pan is omitted from this list.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of ancient deities, moving from the Egyptian "Eight Gods" to a comparison between the seniority of Belus and Osiris, and the theory that these religious systems originated in India.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>The History and Chronology</header>

The question is whether Belus or Osiris was the more ancient deity of the two. For though Uranus and Saturn, if considered as mortal men, might have lived before either of them, the historical claim of Belus (or Jupiter, if I may call him that) seems to be established by clearer chronological evidence than that of Osiris or any other deity. Once his status as a god was established, it was almost necessary—and certainly not difficult—to invent a lineage connecting him to other gods and other gods to him.

This absurdly impious practice spread through both the Eastern and Western worlds many ages before the coming of Christ. Even in China, a nation whose records are older than any others, this practice is certainly no more than seventeen hundred years old; specifically, it dates to when the worship of <term>Fo</term> <note>Fo is the Chinese name for the Buddha; the author is dating the arrival of Buddhism to China.</note> was brought into China from India. The Indians were always regarded as a deeply religious people by the Chinese, just as the ancient Greeks held the same opinion of the Egyptians because of their countless deities and religious rites. Because the Greeks lacked sufficient knowledge of other countries, they concluded that these traditions originated naturally in Egypt. However, I believe they all originally derived from another source, and I am not unsupported by ancient writers in this theory. For if we may believe Plutarch, "Phylarchus <note>An Athenian historian (c. 3rd century BCE) often cited by Plutarch.</note> held that—

---

(1) "Before the superstition of the idol **FO** <note>original: FOE</note> was imported from India, no image of false gods and no statue existed in China." Philippe Couplet, *Preface to Chinese Science*, p. 78. <note>Philippe Couplet was a 17th-century Jesuit missionary to China who published works on Chinese history and philosophy.</note>

(2) "The Chinese have no sciences, and their religion and most of their laws are derived from the Indians; indeed, they are of the opinion that the Indians taught them the worship of idols and consider them a very religious nation." Mr. Renaudot's *Account of India and China by Two Mohammedan Travellers in the Ninth Century*. London, 1733, p. 36.

(3) "Nor should we disregard Phylarchus, who writes that [Bacchus] first brought from the Indians into Egypt two bulls, one named Apis and the other Osiris." <note>original: Οὐδὲ Φυλάρχου μὴ καταφρονεῖν, γράφοντος ὅτι πρῶτος εἰς Αἴγυπτον ἐξ Ἰνδῶν ἤγαγε δύο βοῦς, ὧν τῷ μὲν Ἄπις ὄνομα, τῷ δ' Ὄσιρις.</note> Plutarch, *On Isis and Osiris*.

<meta>This page continues the argument that ancient Egyptian religious practices originated in India, citing similarities in architecture and animal worship. It then transitions to a broader discussion of human history starting from the Great Flood.</meta>

<header>Of the Fabulous Ages.</header>

"Bacchus first brought the gods Apis and Osiris from India into Egypt." Since the discoveries of modern travelers, we find descriptions of (1) Indian temples, some of which are built in the style of Egyptian architecture, with galleries and flat roofs supported by colonnades. As for the worship performed there, I believe there is hardly any superstition of the ancient Egyptians that cannot be matched by—and is still practiced in—India today. These include the monstrous images of their deities; the worship of the cow or ox; and the veneration paid to other animals such as dogs, cats, sphinxes, monkeys, birds, and fishes. These customs are still prevalent among idolaters in various parts of India. Therefore, with respect to more expert opinions, I consider India to be the original birthplace of <term>idolatry</term> <note>The worship of physical images or idols as gods.</note> and Bacchus to be the first "hero-god" mentioned in history.

<margin>The original state of nature and nations.</margin>
Since we have assumed our two heroes lived in times of the most obscure antiquity, it may help to illustrate their history if we take a short survey of the state of nature and nations in the earliest ages. Here it will be necessary to go back to that ever-memorable era of the <term>Deluge</term> <note>The Great Flood, a narrative found in many ancient cultures, most famously the story of Noah in the Bible.</note>; a fact of which all nations preserved a memory. It is not to be expected that, after such a vast distance of time, all nations should agree on the exact timing or specific circumstances of it; it is sufficient that they retained the ge—

# Of the Mythological Ages
<note>original: "Fabulous Ages"; here referring to the era of myths and legends following the Great Flood.</note>

...from there, the earth was repopulated. Here, I beg leave to recall to the reader's mind the opinion of (1) Salmasius <note>Claude Salmasius (1588–1653) was a renowned French classical scholar and polymath.</note>, a person of as great learning and judgment as any the last age produced. "There is no nation," he says, "either of Europe or Asia, but what came originally from the north; from here, most parts of Europe and Asia were planted with inhabitants. That vast region of **Scythia** <note>A vast area of central Eurasia inhabited by nomadic peoples in antiquity.</note>, situated to the north and extending far to the east and west, brought forth all those people, along with their languages, who overflowed Europe and Asia. And by discharging its excess population towards the south, it produced the different nations of both those countries."

<margin>*Nations without discipline naturally decline into savagery.*</margin>

History, reason, and experience all agree that when families grew too numerous for one country, they had to move and settle in others. There, they lived for some time without intermixing with each other and without laws, under no government except for what was held by the chiefs of each particular family. The swarms that issued from this "general hive," being thus left to themselves, naturally declined into a state of <term>barbarity</term> <note>original: "barbarity"; here meaning a primitive or uncivilized state lacking formal legal and social structures.</note>, regardless of what they were at first. They continued in this state for ages, until some person of greater intelligence and strength reduced the separate clans into one community and made laws for the government of the

---

(1) <note>original Latin: "Nulla Europæ fere gens, nec Asiæ, quin a Septentrione promanaverit. Inde propagines profectæ populorum quibus Europæ Asiæque pleræque partes consitæ fuerunt. Scythia igitur, quæ ad septentrionem, omnes ferme gentes evomuit cum suis linguis, quæ Europam & Asiam inundarunt. Ut autem vastissima illa Scytharum regio fuit, & late porrecta ad orientem & occidentem, versus meridiem eructando varias hinc in Europa, inde in Asia, produxit gentes."</note> "There is almost no nation in Europe or Asia that did not originate from the North. From there, the lineages of peoples departed to populate most parts of Europe and Asia. Therefore, Scythia, located to the north, poured out nearly all nations along with their languages, which then flooded Europe and Asia. Since that region of the Scythians was immense and stretched far to the east and west, its expansion toward the south produced various nations, some in Europe and others in Asia." — Salmasius, *On the Hellenistic Language* <note>original: "De Hellenistica"</note>, page 366.

<margin>*Examples of their reformation among the Chinese and Russians.*</margin>
We may form an idea of this change in the state of nature by looking at the different customs of two neighboring nations: the wandering Tartars <note>A historical name for various nomadic Turkic and Mongol peoples of Central and Northern Asia.</note> and the Chinese. The former observe little or no social refinement in the common actions of life, such as eating or drinking; they live by plunder <note>original: "rapine"</note> and depend for their survival on their cattle and the wild fruits of the earth. In contrast, the Chinese—as if instructed by the gods—cultivate the land, promote every academic and practical art <note>original: "liberal and mechanical art"</note>, and live with the utmost politeness. This blessing they have enjoyed for several thousand years, and their historical records <note>original: "annals"</note> immortalize the memory of their benefactors, though we do not find that they ever paid divine honors to them as was done in other countries. And without looking as far away as China, consider what a change has been brought about in the manners of the Russians within the memory of people still living! This was achieved through the efforts of one man <note>A reference to Peter the Great (1672–1725), who enacted sweeping reforms to westernize and modernize the Russian Empire.</note>, a native of the country who improved himself through the experience he gained in nations more civilized than his own. From these examples, we may conclude that the first heroes of antiquity were endowed with extraordinary natural talents, which were further refined by educa-

<margin>*The beginning and progress of science.*</margin>
Scholars <note>original: "The learned"</note> of the last and present centuries, misled by the Greeks themselves while tracing science back to its original source, seem to me to have taken a much more indirect route. This is because they failed to pay attention to the opinions of Salmasius <note>Claude Saumaise (1588–1653), a renowned French classical scholar known for his immense learning.</note>, who pointed out a much more direct path. Sacred history—which should indeed be our guide in uncertain cases—has biased them in favor of Egypt, the Holy Land, and the countries lying to the east of there, where science certainly flourished to an impressive degree in ancient times. However, if science spread according to the natural course of things, we must turn our thoughts toward the north. This path may not be as well-traveled as the other, and it may be overgrown with thorns and briars, but once cleared, it will be found to be the shorter road to the original source. For many ages, the Scythians <note>An ancient nomadic people from the Eurasian Steppes, often depicted as "barbaric" by Greek and Roman historians despite their complex culture.</note> have been considered a barbarous people, but it cannot be proven that they were so from the beginning. The spirit of the northern nations has primarily expressed itself through warfare, but even in this, a strength of intellect was required to lead them. The (1) Scythians in ancient times three times gained control of the empire of Asia. Their defeats of Cyrus and Darius <note>Cyrus the Great and Darius I, powerful kings of the Persian Empire who attempted to subdue the Scythians.</note> are lasting evidence of their military skill; our Western world has sufficiently

# The History and Chronology

...propagated to Persia, Assyria, India, and even to China itself. (1) Strabo says that "Bactria, which neighbors Aria, was fruitful in everything except oil <note>original Greek: Πάμφορον δ' εἶναι τὴν Βακτριανὴν, πλὴν ἐλαίου, πλησίον τῇ Ἀρίᾳ παρακειμένην (Pámphoron d' eînai tēn Baktrianēn, plēn elaíou, plēsíon tē Aría parakeiménēn).</note>." The natural fertility of the soil, therefore, invited people to settle there until it was overcrowded with inhabitants. The dense population of this country—which is one argument for its level of civilization <note>original: politeness</note>—can be judged by what was reported nearly two thousand years ago: that it then had (2) a thousand cities under the control of the Greeks after they had destroyed the Persian Empire. 

I take Bactria to be what is now called Bukhara <note>A region in modern-day Uzbekistan.</note>—both Greater and Lesser Bukhara—stretching from nearly the Caspian Sea eastward to the borders of China. (3) "Bokharia, Bukharia, Bogaria, Bokaria, or Boharia, as it may be written in various ways, is the name currently given to the country or tract of land between Karasm <note>Also known as Khwarezm, a large oasis region in Central Asia.</note> and the great sandy desert bordering China. It signifies the country of the *Bukhars*, which (4) Abulghazi Khan <note>A 17th-century ruler and historian from the Khanate of Khiva.</note> informs us is a Mongol word meaning a *learned man*. He adds that this is because all those who wish to be instructed in languages and sciences go to Bukhara." From this, it appears that this name was given by the Mongols—

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the historical identity of Bactria (modern-day Bukhara), specifically addressing the origins and etymology of the name "Bukhar" and its connection to ancient geography.</meta>

# Of the Fabulous Ages

"...Mongols, who conquered this country in the time of Genghis Khan. The same author mentions the Bukhars elsewhere, but in those places, the term seems to be restricted to the inhabitants of the city of Bukhara." I readily agree with this learned writer and his source, Abulghazi, that the word (1) *Bukhar* is the northern name for a *learned man*. However, I cannot accept the conclusion he draws, if I understand him correctly: namely, that the name Bucharia was imposed by the Mongols during or after the time of Genghis Khan. To me, the opposite seems clear, since a person would first have to go into Bucharia before he could be called a *Bukhar*. 

Bucharia is therefore not the new name, but the old name of the country; and, if I am not mistaken, it means nothing other than *Bactria*. Just as Mount *Cas* was the *Caucasus* <note>original: Cascasus or Cavcasus</note> of the ancients; as their *Maracanda* is now *Samarkand*; as *Sogdiana* is *Al Sogd*; and *Chorasmia* is *Karasm* or *Khwarazm*; so Bocharia is only the later name for Bactria. Furthermore, the city of *Bukhara* seems to be the ancient *Bactra*, the capital of the country. The schools of Bukhara and Samarkand still maintain their ancient reputation for learning, and the soil of Greater Bucharia still reflects the ancient fertility of Bactria. "It must be confessed," [says an (2) author...

***

(1) What the Mongols called *Boghar* or *Bukhar*, a Saxon would write as *Boc-mann* <note>original: "Boc pen," likely representing the Old English *bōcmann*</note>, meaning a "man of books" or a "man of learning" <note>original Latin: *Vir Librorum*</note>. *Boc* is the old northern word for a *book*, originally referring to the wood of the *beech tree*—in Saxon, *Boc-treow* <note>original: "Boc tpeop"</note> or "book-tree"—on which their characters were anciently engraved. The Greek *Biblos* <note>original Greek: Βίβλος</note> and the Latin *Liber* carry the same meaning, both signifying the physical materials on which writing was performed.

(2) The notes of the French editors [Bentinck] on Abulghazi Khan’s *History of the Turks and Tartars*, page 455.
